Subgroups of Classical Groups that are Transitive on Subspaces Thanks: The authors gratefully acknowledge the support of the Australian Research Council (ARC) Discovery Project Grants DP160102323 and DP190100450.
Michael Giudici, S. P. Glasby and Cheryl E. Praeger

Abstract
. For each finite classical group G G , we classify the subgroups of G G which act transitively on a G G -invariant set of subspaces of the natural module, where the subspaces are either totally isotropic or nondegenerate. Our proof uses the classification of the maximal factorisations of almost simple groups. As a first application of these results we classify all point-transitive subgroups of automorphisms of finite thick generalised quadrangles.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
primary 20G40, 20B10; secondary 57S17, 05B25

August 8, 2026

1.
Introduction

Work on classifying subgroups of finite classical groups that are transitive on a family of subspaces goes back to the seminal results of Christoph Hering
[
H1
,
H2
]
in the 1970s and 1980s. These results led to a complete classification of all subgroups of semilinear transformations of a finite vector space
V
that are transitive on nonzero vectors. For each such subgroup, the corresponding subgroup of the projective group is transitive on
1
-subspaces. Conversely, for each subgroup of the projective group which is transitive on
1
-subspaces, its full preimage in
\Gamma\mathrm{L}(V)
is transitive on nonzero vectors. Adjoining the group of translations to a transitive subgroup
H\leqslant\Gamma\mathrm{L}(V)
yields a
2
-transitive subgroup of the affine group on
V
. Indeed Hering’s classification of transitive semilinear groups is equivalent to the classification of finite
2
-transitive permutation groups of affine type. His work also led Hering to discover two new sporadic linear spaces
[
H3
]
, and inspired others to construct new geometries and designs
[
B
,
Pf
]
.

Over the years, subspace actions have been studied in relation to problems in group theory, geometry and combinatorics. For example, they are a natural source of primitive actions. Indeed, in the 1980s, Oliver King
[
King1981
,
King1981a
,
King1982
]
showed that, with a few specified exceptions, each action by a finite classical group on a family of totally isotropic or nondegenerate subspaces is primitive.

Our aim in this paper is to classify all subgroups of finite classical groups that act transitively on the set of all subspaces of a given isometry type. We classify all subgroups of
\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{n}(q)
that are transitive on the set of all
k
-dimensional subspaces for some fixed
k
(Theorem
3.1
).
For unitary and symplectic groups we concentrate on the nondegenerate and totally isotropic subspaces while for orthogonal groups we focus on the nondegenerate and totally singular subspaces, and also the nonsingular subspaces of dimension one in the even characteristic case. This completes the program begun in
[
Reg
]
. Some low dimensional cases were dealt with by Kantor and Liebler
[
KLrank3
]
but our proofs are independent of theirs.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
V
be a vector space over a finite field equipped with a nondegenerate quadratic, hermitian or alternating form. Let
\mathcal{U}
be the set of all totally isotropic (or totally singular) subspaces of a given dimension, or the set of all nondegenerate subspaces of
V
of a given isometry type. If
H
is a group of semisimilarities of the form and acts transitively on
\mathcal{U}
then
H
is known (see Table
1.1
).

More details about the exact collections of subspaces
\mathcal{U}
considered in the case where
V
is equipped with a nondegenerate quadratic form are given in Section
2.3
and Table
2.3
. For example we do not treat the case where
V
has dimension
4
and is equipped with a quadratic form of
+
type. We also determine all groups which are transitive on each of the two types of maximal totally singular subspaces for quadratic forms of
+
type, and all groups that are transitive on all subspaces of the same similarity type for quadratic forms.
Table
1.1
provides the relevant result numbers for each type of form and type of subspaces considered.
As was the case in
[
Reg
]
*Chapter 4, we often specify that a subgroup transitive on a family of subspaces
\mathcal{U}
should have a certain property and we do not guarantee that every subgroup with this property is transitive on
\mathcal{U}
.

\mathrm{O}^{+}

A similar problem for classical algebraic groups has been studied by Liebeck, Saxl and Seitz in
[
LSS
]
. Not only are there far fewer possibilities for algebraic classical groups than for finite classical groups, some examples have no finite analogue, for example in even characteristic the group
\mathrm{Sp}_{2m}
contains the subgroup
\mathrm{SO}_{2m}
which acts transitively on the set of nondegenerate subspaces of dimension
2k
for any
k
satisfying
1\leqslant k\leqslant m-1
, for details see
[
LSS
]
.

Our main method will be to use group factorisations.
Whenever we have a finite almost simple classical group
G
on
V
, a
G
-invariant family
\mathcal{U}
of proper subspaces of
V
, and a subgroup
H<G
transitive on
\mathcal{U}
, we obtain a factorisation
G=AH
where
A
is the stabiliser
G_{U}
of a subspace
U\in\mathcal{U}
. In fact
G=AH
if and only if
H
is transitive on
\mathcal{U}
. If both factors
A,H
are maximal and core-free, then
G=AH
is called a
maximal core-free factorisation
.
Such factorisations of the almost simple classical groups were classified by Liebeck, Saxl and the third author in
[
Factns
]
, their major motivation, and first application, being to classify the maximal subgroups of the finite alternating and symmetric groups
[
LPS4
]
. Hence if
G
acts primitively on
\mathcal{U}
then the classification in
[
Factns
]
provides us with the
maximal core-free
subgroups of
G
that are transitive on
\mathcal{U}
. All possibilities for
H
(that is, with the maximality assumption removed) for some of the subspace actions of finite classical groups were determined by Liebeck, Saxl and the third author in
[
Reg
]
*Chapter 4 as part of their strategy to classify all exact factorisations of almost simple groups
G
with one factor maximal and core-free. (A factorisation
G=AB
is called
exact
if
A\cap B=1
.) This resulted firstly, in the
classification of all almost simple primitive permutation groups containing a regular subgroup
[
Reg
]
*Theorem 1.1 and secondly in the classification of certain families of so-called ‘
B
-groups’
[
Reg
]
*Corollary 1.5 and Cayley graphs
[
Reg
]
*Theorem 1.6.

We expect that our classification will be useful in many contexts. As a first application we classify all point-transitive subgroups acting on the (finite thick) classical generalised quadrangles in Theorem
9.1
. Previously only the point regular subgroups were known, as an application of
[
Reg
]
by Bamberg and the first author
[
BamG
]
.

The outline of this paper is as follows. Section
2
describes our strategy and
establishes the notation we use. We discuss the linear case, and some small dimensional orthogonal groups, in Section
3
.
The unitary case is considered in Sections
3.2
(for
n=2
) and
4
(for
n>2
), and the symplectic case in Section
5
. The odd dimensional
orthogonal case is dealt with in Section
7
, and the even dimensional case in Sections
6
(minus type) and
8
(plus type). Our classification of point-transitive groups on
classical generalised quadrangles is proved in Section
9
.

Remark 1.2
.

Some of the subspace actions we consider were already dealt with in
[
Reg
]
*Chapter 4, as the results were needed for the classification in
[
Reg
]
of the regular subgroups of the finite almost simple primitive permutation groups. Our study covered more subspace actions of classical groups, and we needed both to use and to extend the results in
[
Reg
]
. In so-doing we uncovered a few issues:

\circ

In some of the results in
[
Reg
]
*Chapter 4 the list of possibilities is a ‘superset’: not all the listed groups mentioned are actually examples. This is mentioned in
[
Reg
]
*text and Remark on p. 16. However we needed more detailed information about some of these cases.

\circ

In some of the results in
[
Reg
]
*Chapter 4, the groups listed are actually subgroups of the projective classical groups, rather than the classical groups themselves. We have clarified some of these cases.

\circ

In one case we found a missing family of examples.

Here we summarise details of these issues and point to where they are addressed in our paper. We organise this according to the relevant results in
[
Reg
]
*Chapter 4.

(a)

[
Reg
]
*Lemma 4.1 determining types of subspaces admitting transitive proper subgroups of classical groups. For
[
Reg
]
*Lemma 4.1(iv), that is the case ‘
G_{0}=\Omega_{2m+1}(q)
(
q
odd,
m\geqslant 3
)’, the possibility ‘
M=P_{1}
(
m=3
)’ should be added (see Theorem
7.1
(a) and its proof, especially Table
7.4
). For
[
Reg
]
*Lemma 4.1(vii), that is, the ‘exceptional cases’, the possibility ‘
G_{0}=\mathrm{PSL}_{4}(2)
,
M=P_{2}
’ should be added (see the proof of Theorem
3.1
).

(b)

[
Reg
]
*Lemma 4.2 for
\Gamma\mathrm{Sp}_{2m}(q)
acting on nondegenerate 2-subspaces. The possibilities ‘
\mathrm{Sp}_{m/2}(q^{4})
(
m/2
even,
q=2
)’ in
[
Reg
]
*Lemma 4.2(i), and ‘
\mathrm{G}_{2}(q^{4})
(
m=12
,
q=2
)’ in
[
Reg
]
*Lemma 4.2(ii), as subgroups of
\Gamma\mathrm{Sp}_{2m}(2)
, lead to no examples. See Lemma
5.1
.

(c)

[
Reg
]
*Lemma 4.3 for
\Gamma\mathrm{U}_{2m}(q)
acting on nondegenerate 1-subspaces. Two listed groups in this result are the images in
\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{U}_{2m}(q)
, namely the groups
\mathrm{PSU}_{4}(3)
and
M_{22}
listed in
[
Reg

*Lemma 4.3(iv) as subgroups of
\Gamma\mathrm{U}_{6}(2)
should be
3\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\mathrm{PSU}_{4}(3)
and
3\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}M_{22}
, respectively. (See Theorem
4.1

4.1
and Case 7 in the proof. See also

BHRD

(d)

listed in

Remark 1.3
.

In their work on rank three permutation representations of classical groups, Kantor and Liebler determined the subgroups
K
of
\Gamma\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(q)
,
\Gamma\mathrm{U}_{4}(q)
and
\Gamma\mathrm{U}_{5}(q)
that are transitive on the totally isotropic 1-dimensional or 2-dimensional subspaces
[
KLrank3
]
*Corollary 5.12 and the subgroups of
\Gamma\mathrm{U}_{3}(q)
that are transitive on totally isotropic 1-subspaces. They also determined the subgroups of
\Gamma\mathrm{O}^{+}_{6}(q)
that are transitive on totally singular 1-subspaces
[
KLrank3
]
*Lemma 5.15. We note the following corrections/clarifications:

(a)

[
KLrank3
]
*Corollary 5.12(vi) lists
\mathrm{SU}_{3}(2)^{\prime\prime}\trianglelefteqslant K\leqslant\Gamma\mathrm{U}_{4}(2)
. This is our
3^{1+2}_{+}
in Theorem
4.1
(c) and Table
4.3
.

(b)

[
KLrank3
]
*Corollary 5.12(vii) lists
\textup{C}_{3}^{3}\rtimes\mathrm{Alt}_{4}\trianglelefteqslant K\leqslant\Gamma\mathrm{U}_{4}(2)
. This corresponds with our case
\textup{C}_{3}^{3}\trianglelefteqslant K
in Theorem
4.1
(c) and Table
4.3
.

(c)

[
KLrank3
]
*Corollary 5.12 is missing
3_{-}^{1+2}\trianglelefteqslant K\leqslant\Gamma\mathrm{U}_{4}(2)
acting transitively on totally isotropic 2-subspaces as in Theorem
4.1
(c) and Table
4.3
. This example has previously been observed in
[
Reg
]
*Table 16.1

(d)

[
KLrank3
]
*Corollary 5.12(x) lists
E\rtimes\textup{C}_{5}\trianglelefteqslant K\leqslant\Gamma\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(3)
where
E
is extraspecial of order
2^{5}
. This should read as
E\rtimes\textup{C}_{5}\leqslant K
to allow the possibility that
E\rtimes\mathrm{Alt}_{5}\trianglelefteqslant K
as in Theorem
5.2
(a) Table
5.1
.

(e)

[
KLrank3
]
*Corollary 5.12 is missing
\textup{C}_{19}\trianglelefteqslant K\leqslant\Gamma\mathrm{U}_{4}(8)
acting transitively on totally isotropic 2-subspaces as in Theorem
4.1
(c) and Table
4.3
. This example has previously been observed in
[
Reg
]
*Table 16.1. A similar example is missing in the list of subgroups of
\Gamma\mathrm{U}_{3}(8)
acting transitively on totally isotropic 1-subspaces in
[
KLrank3
]
*Lemma 2.8, see Theorem
4.1
(b) and Table
4.2
. This example has previously been observed in
[
Factns
]
*Table 3.

(f)

[
KLrank3
]
*Lemma 2.8(iv) lists
K^{\prime}=\mathrm{Alt}_{7}\leqslant\Gamma\mathrm{U}_{3}(5)
. This should be
K^{\prime}=3\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\mathrm{Alt}_{7}
as in Theorem
4.1
(b) Table
4.2
. See also
[
BHRD
]
*Table 8.6.

\triangle

.

We would like to take this opportunity to note an example of a regular subgroup of an almost simple group that is missing from
[
Reg
]
*Table 16.1. There should be an extra row as follows:

[

*p25. The possibility
n=3
and

should also be listed as
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(8)
has a proper subgroup of index dividing
n\log q

Comparing orders we see that
|G|=|A||B|
and
|A\cap B|\leqslant 3
. However, 3 does not divide
|B\cap\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(8)|
while all elements of order 3 in A lie in
\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(8)
. Hence
A\cap B=1
and

, as desired.

Remark 1.5
.

We have been informed by Pablo Spiga
[
Spiga
]
that there is a factorisation
\Omega_{8}^{+}(4).2=N_{2}^{-}\mathrm{GO}_{8}^{-}(2)
which is missing from
[
Factns
]
*Table 4. Here
\mathrm{GO}_{8}^{-}(2)=N_{\Gamma\mathrm{O}^{+}_{8}(4)}(A)
where
A
is an image of the subfield subgroup
\Omega_{8}^{-}(2)
under triality. The existence of this factorisation has been confirmed in
Magma
. The gap in the argument occurs in
[
Factns
]
*(5.1.15)(b)(iii) on pp. 106–107 and suggests that there is at most one other missing factorisation of this form, namely
\Omega_{8}^{+}(16).4=N_{2}^{-}(\mathrm{GO}_{8}^{-}(4).2)
. However, its existence is yet to be determined. In both cases, that is for
q=4
and
16
, we have that
N_{2}^{-}\cap\mathrm{GO}_{8}^{-}(q^{1/2})\cap\Omega_{8}^{+}(q)=\mathrm{SL}_{2}(q^{1/2})\times\mathrm{GO}_{2}^{-}(q)
and is contained in the stabiliser in
\Omega_{8}^{-}(q^{1/2})
of an elliptic 4-subspace of the 8-dimensional vector space over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
equipped with a nondegenerate elliptic quadratic form. In particular, comparing orders yields that
\Omega_{8}^{-}(q^{1/2})
is not transitive on
\mathcal{N}_{2}^{-}
in either case and
\mathrm{GO}_{8}^{-}(4)
is not transitive when
q=16
.

\triangle

2.
Our strategy and notation

Let
V
be an
n
-dimensional vector space over the field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. For
1\leqslant k<n
, our first goal is to determine all subgroups of
\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{n}(q)
that act transitively on the set
\mathcal{U}
of all subspaces of dimension
k
. We do this in Section
3
.

In the rest of this section we discuss the families of classical groups, and their subspace actions, that we will consider.

2.1.
Some notation for classical groups and their subgroups

Our notation for the classical groups is recorded in Tables
2.1
and
2.2
. It differs from both
[
KL
]

and
[
BHRD
]
but is influenced by both. Type
\mathrm{O}^{\circ}
refers to odd-dimensional orthogonal groups while types
\mathrm{O}^{-}
and
\mathrm{O}^{+}
refer to even-dimensional orthogonal groups. Column
\Gamma
refers to the group of all semisimilarities of the form, column
C
refers to the group of all similarities, column
I
the group of all isometries and column
S
the group of all isometries of determinant one. We follow
[
Taylor
]
*p. 136 and define
\Omega_{n}(q)
to be the derived subgroup of
\mathrm{GO}_{n}(q)
. We use the standard term ‘general linear’ and
the standard notation ‘
\mathrm{GL}
’, instead of ‘conformal linear’ and ‘
CL
’
even though the latter would be more consistent in Table
2.1
. Finally, for a
statement
P
,
we use the Iverson bracket notation
[P]
to mean 1 if
P
is true, and 0
if
P
is false. Hence
(q-1,2)^{[n\geqslant 3]}
equals 2 if
n\geqslant 3
and
q
is odd,
and equals 1 otherwise.

, and here
q=p^{f}
.

\textup{PGO}^{\circ}

\mathrm{P}\Omega^{\circ}

{\bf O}^{\varepsilon}

\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{O}^{\varepsilon}

f

\textup{PCO}^{\varepsilon}

\delta_{2}

\mathrm{PSO}^{\varepsilon}

\mathrm{P}\Omega^{\varepsilon}

We denote an extension of
N
by
Q
by
N\ldotp Q
, a split extension
by
N\colon Q
or
N\rtimes Q
, and a nonsplit extension by
N\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}Q
. Finally, if
X\leqslant\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{n}(q)
then we use the notation
X^{R}
to indicate that
X
is reducible on
V
.

2.2.
Formed spaces: types of subspaces

Next we assume that
V
is equipped with a nondegenerate hermitian, alternating or quadratic form and
G
is the group of all semisimilarities of the form.
This means, in the case of a hermitian or alternating form that the radical
V^{\perp}=0
.
The case of a quadratic form needs more comment: a vector is
singular
if its value under the quadratic form is zero, and otherwise it is
nonsingular
. Also a quadratic form determines a symmetric bilinear form, called its
polar form
, that is also
G
-invariant, and the quadratic form is said to be nondegenerate if the radical
V^{\perp}
of its polar form is either zero, or
q
is even,
n
is odd, and
V^{\perp}
is a nonsingular
1
-subspace.

Thus in all cases
G
preserves a sesquilinear form on
V
. A subspace
U
is called
nondegenerate
if
U\cap U^{\perp}=0
, and
totally isotropic
if
U\cap U^{\perp}=U
, that is to say,
U\subseteq U^{\perp}
. Again we comment further on the quadratic form case: a subspace
U
is
totally singular
if all its vectors are singular. In a totally isotropic subspace
U
, the set
U^{*}
of all singular vectors forms a subspace of
U
, and either
U^{*}=U
, or
q
is even and
U^{*}
has codimension
1
in
U
. Moreover, the stabiliser
G_{U}
leaves
U^{*}
invariant.
Thus in the quadratic form case we will usually deal with totally singular subspaces.

Broadly speaking, our goal is to describe all subgroups of
G
that are transitive on the set
\mathcal{U}
of all proper nonzero subspaces of
V
of a given isometry type. For
U\in\mathcal{U}
, the stabiliser
G_{U}
also leaves invariant
U^{\perp}
and
U\cap U^{\perp}
. If
U\cap U^{\perp}=0
then
U
is nondegenerate. On the other hand, if
U\cap U^{\perp}\neq 0
, then
U\cap U^{\perp}
is totally isotropic and
G_{U}\leqslant G_{U\cap U^{\perp}}\leqslant G
. In the cases of an hermitian or alternating form, or a quadratic form in odd characteristic, we have proper containment
G_{U\cap U^{\perp}}<G
and, if we know all the subgroups
H
of
G
that are transitive on the set of
G
-translates of
U\cap U^{\perp}
, then we need to identify among these subgroups
H
the ones which are transitive on
\mathcal{U}
, or equivalently, those subgroups
H
for which
H_{U\cap U^{\perp}}
is transitive on the subspaces in
\mathcal{U}
containing
U\cap U^{\perp}
. The case of a quadratic form with
q
even is different. If
G_{U\cap U^{\perp}}<G
, then
G_{U}
leaves invariant the unique subspace
(U\cap U^{\perp})^{*}
of singular vectors in
U\cap U^{\perp}
. Either this totally singular subspace is nonzero, or
U=U\cap U^{\perp}
is a nonsingular
1
-subspace (not equal to
V^{\perp}
since
G_{U\cap U^{\perp}}<G
), and the same comments apply for subgroups
H
transitive on the set of
G
-translates of the totally singular
(U\cap U^{\perp})^{*}
or nonsingular
U\cap U^{\perp}
, respectively. On the other hand if
G_{U\cap U^{\perp}}=G
then
U\cap U^{\perp}=V^{\perp}
and, provided
U\neq V^{\perp}
, the quotient
U/V^{\perp}
is a proper nonzero nondegenerate subspace of the symplectic space
V/V^{\perp}
.
As
G
is isomorphic to the corresponding symplectic group, the possible transitive subgroups are given by our results for symplectic groups.

Because of this discussion, and our need to undertake a manageable classification, we therefore restrict our attention to
G
-invariant families
\mathcal{U}
of nondegenerate subspaces and of totally isotropic subspaces; and in the case of orthogonal spaces with
q
even, families of nondegenerate subspaces, totally singular subspaces, and nonsingular
1
-subspaces (distinct from the radical).

2.3.
Formed spaces: dimensions of subspaces

We denote the set of all nondegenerate subspaces of dimension
k
by
\mathcal{N}_{k}
, and the set of all totally isotropic (or totally singular) subspaces of dimension
k
by
\mathcal{P}_{k}
.
In the case of orthogonal spaces with
q
even, we also denote the set of nonsingular
1
-subspaces by
\mathcal{N}_{1}
. We will sometimes use the notation
\mathcal{P}_{k}(V)
or
\mathcal{N}_{k}(V)
if we wish to specify the underlying vector space
V
. In a classical group we often use
P_{k}
to denote the stabiliser of a totally isotropic subspace of dimension
k
(or a totally singular
k
-subspace in the orthogonal case), and
N_{k}
to denote the stabiliser of a nondegenerate subspace of dimension
k
. Again, we will sometimes use the notation
P_{k}(G)
or
N_{k}(G)
if we wish to specify the classical group
G
. Our notation for
P_{k}
is consistent with
[
KL
]
but inconsistent with
[
Factns
]
and
[
Reg
]
, which we explain below.

For a totally isotropic (or totally singular) subspace
U
, since
U\subseteq U^{\perp}
and
\dim(U)+\dim(U^{\perp})=n
, the subspace
U
has dimension at most
n/2
. For a nondegenerate subspace
U
, the stabiliser
G_{U}
also fixes the orthogonal complement
U^{\perp}
. In the hermitian and alternating cases
U^{\perp}
is also nondegenerate and
G_{U}=G_{U^{\perp}}
. The groups
\mathrm{SU}_{n}(q)
and
\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(q)
act transitively on
\mathcal{P}_{k}
and
\mathcal{N}_{k}
, so we only need to find all transitive subgroups
H
not containing these groups, and moreover for both
\mathcal{P}_{k}
and
\mathcal{N}_{k}
, we may assume that the dimension
k\leqslant n/2
.

The remaining comments are about the orthogonal (or quadratic form) case. It is more complicated than the other cases, and so we record in
Table
2.3
the families
\mathcal{U}
of subspaces that we consider. Let
U\in\mathcal{U}
. First we deal with totally isotropic subspaces. If
U
is not totally singular, then, as explained above,
q
is even,
U
is restricted to be a nonsingular
1
-subspace (not
V^{\perp}
), and
\mathcal{U}
is as in row
6
of Table
2.3
. Suppose now that
U
is totally singular. The group
\Omega_{n}^{\varepsilon}(q)
, for
\varepsilon\in\{\circ,+,-\}
, acts transitively on
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{k}
if
k=\dim(U)<n/2
, as in row
1
of Table
2.3
. Totally singular
n/2
-subspaces exist only if
\varepsilon=+
, and
\Omega_{n}^{+}(q)
has two orbits on
\mathcal{P}_{n/2}
which we denote by
\mathcal{P}_{n/2}^{+}
and
\mathcal{P}_{n/2}^{-}
, as in row
2
of Table
2.3
. (This notation differs from that in
[
Factns
]
and
[
Reg
]
where
\mathcal{P}_{n/2}
and
\mathcal{P}_{n/2-1}
are used to denote the two
\Omega_{n}^{+}(q)
-orbits, but is more consistent with
[
KL
]
.) When
q
is odd these two orbits are fused by an element of
\mathrm{GO}^{+}_{n}(q)
that is not in
\mathrm{SO}_{n}^{+}(q)
, while when
q
is even they are fused by an element of
\mathrm{GO}^{+}_{n}(q)=\mathrm{SO}^{+}_{n}(q)
that is not in
\Omega_{n}^{+}(q)
. Also
U,W\in\mathcal{P}_{n/2}
lie in the same
\Omega_{n}^{+}(q)
-orbit if and only the codimension of
U\cap W
in
U
(and hence in
W
) is even.

Now we consider nondegenerate subspaces
U
. Here either
k
is even, or both
k
and
q
are odd, and in both cases there are two isometry types of nondegenerate
k
-subspaces
U
. If
k
is even, these are the subspaces where the restriction of the quadratic form is hyperbolic or elliptic, and we denote the set of all such subspaces by
\mathcal{N}_{k}^{+}
or
\mathcal{N}_{k}^{-}
respectively. If
k,q
and
n
are all odd then a nondegenerate
k
-subspace
U
can be distinguished by the isometry type of
U^{\perp}
, and thus we use
\mathcal{N}_{k}^{\varepsilon}
for
\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}
to denote the set of all nondegenerate
k
-subspaces
U
such that
U^{\perp}
is of type
\varepsilon
. Finally, when
k
and
q
are odd and
n
is even, there are two isometry types of nondegenerate
k
-subspaces and one can be mapped to the other by a similarity of the whole space that is not an isometry. We still denote the two classes by
\mathcal{N}_{k}^{\varepsilon}
, where
\varepsilon\in\{+,-\},
and they can be distinguished by the discriminant of the restriction of the quadratic form. This is most easily seen when
k=1
: here the two isometry types are those for which the quadratic form is a square and those where it is a nonsquare. We will use
N_{k}^{\varepsilon}
to denote the stabiliser of an element of
\mathcal{N}_{k}^{\varepsilon}
when we want to distinguish its isometry type.

If
k>n/2
then
G_{U}
is also the stabiliser of the
(n-k)
-subspace
U^{\perp}
, and moreover
U^{\perp}
is nondegenerate if either
n-k
is even or
n-k
and
q
are both odd. It follows that one of
U
or
U^{\perp}
satisfies the conditions of row 3, 4 or 5 of Table
2.3
, except in the case where
q
is even and one of
U,U^{\perp}
has odd dimension less than
n/2
. However, for
q
even, an odd dimensional subspace is not nondegenerate, and hence in this exceptional case the nondegenerate subspace
U
has even dimension
k>n/2
and
n
is odd. Here
U^{\perp}
contains the nonsingular
1
-subspace

V^{\perp}
. If
U^{\perp}=V^{\perp}
then
U
is as in row 7 of Table
2.3
, and
G_{U}
corresponds to the stabiliser of a quadratic form in the

G
-action on the symplectic space
V/V^{\perp}
. If
U^{\perp}\neq V^{\perp}
then
U^{\perp}/V^{\perp}
is a proper nonzero nondegenerate subspace of the symplectic space
V/V^{\perp}
. As
G
is isomorphic to the corresponding symplectic group, the possible transitive subgroups are given by our results for symplectic groups, and so we do not consider this case.

Thus Table
2.3
summarises the main families
\mathcal{U}
that we consider for the orthogonal groups with ‘socle’
\Omega_{n}^{\varepsilon}(q)
.
In all rows of this table the group
\Omega_{n}^{\varepsilon}(q)
, for
\varepsilon\in\{\circ,+,-\}
, acts transitively on
\mathcal{U}
,

[
BG
]
*Lemma 2.5.10.
Unfortunately, our methods do not work for non-simple groups such as
\mathrm{P}\Omega_{4}^{+}(q)\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q)^{2}
. Hence we omit the groups
\textup{O}_{4}^{+}(q)
.
In addition, when
n
is even,
we also identify subgroups of
\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{n}^{+}(q)
that act transitively on

\mathcal{P}_{n/2}=\mathcal{P}_{n/2}^{+}\cup\mathcal{P}_{n/2}^{-}
(Proposition
8.5
),
and if
k
and
q
are both odd, subgroups of
\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{n}^{\varepsilon}(q)
that are transitive on
\mathcal{N}_{k}=\mathcal{N}_{k}^{+}\cup\mathcal{N}_{k}^{-}
(Propositions
6.3
and
8.6
).

2.4.
Some useful lemmas

We begin with the following lemma to aid switching between matrix groups and their projective versions.
For a finite group
G
, by
G^{(\infty)}
we mean the last term in the derived series; and we say that
G
is
quasisimple
if
G=G^{(\infty)}
and
G/Z(G)
is a nonabelian simple group.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
G
and
G_{0}
be groups with
G^{(\infty)}
quasisimple and let
\pi:G\rightarrow G_{0}
be a homomorphism such that
\pi(G^{(\infty)})=G_{0}^{(\infty)}\neq 1
. Suppose that
M\leqslant G
satisfies
G_{0}^{(\infty)}\leqslant\pi(M)
. Then
G^{(\infty)}\leqslant M
.

Proof.

Let
K=\ker\pi
,
H=G^{(\infty)}
and
H_{0}=G_{0}^{(\infty)}
.
Let
Y=M\cap\pi^{-1}(H_{0})
and observe that

\pi(Y)=\pi(M)\cap H_{0}=H_{0}=\pi(H)
. Clearly
K\cap H\trianglelefteqslant H
and
Y\cap H\trianglelefteqslant Y
. Since
G/H
is soluble, so too is
YH/H\cong Y/(Y\cap H)
. However,
\pi(Y)=H_{0}
has no nontrivial soluble quotients. Thus
\pi(H)=\pi(Y)=\pi(Y\cap H)
and hence
H=(Y\cap H)(K\cap H)
. Since
H
is quasisimple and
K\cap H\neq H
, it follows that
K\cap H\leqslant\mathrm{\sf Z}(H)
.

Suppose that
H=AB
where
B\leqslant\mathrm{\sf Z}(H)
. If
a\in A
,
b\in B
and
c\in H
, then the identity
[ab,c]=[a,c]^{b}[b,c]=[a,c]
, shows that

H^{\prime}=[AB,H]=[A,H]=[AB,A]=A^{\prime}
. Taking
A=Y\cap H
and
B=K\cap H
shows that
H=H^{\prime}=A^{\prime}\leqslant Y\cap H
. Therefore
H\leqslant Y\leqslant M
, as desired.
∎

We also state the following variation of
[
Reg
]
*Lemma 2.6.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
G=AB
be a factorisation of an almost simple classical group with
A
maximal and core-free in
G
. Let
L=\mathrm{soc}(G)
and suppose that
B
is maximal among core-free subgroups of
G
. Define
G^{*}=BL
. Then
G^{*}=(G^{*}\cap A)B
. Moreover, this is a maximal core-free factorisation of
G^{*}

with
B=N_{G}(B\cap L)
unless
L=\mathrm{P}\Omega_{8}^{+}(3)
, or
(L,A\cap L,B\cap L)
is one of the following:

(a)

L=\mathrm{PSL}_{2m}(q)
with
\gcd(q-1,m)>1
,
A\cap L={\rm Stab}(V_{1}\oplus V_{2m-1})
, and
B\cap L=N_{L}(\mathrm{PSp}_{2m}(q))
.

(b)

L=\mathrm{P}\Omega^{+}_{2m}(q)
with
mq
odd,
A\cap L=N_{L}(\mathrm{GL}_{m}(q)/\langle-I\rangle)
and
B\cap L=N_{1}
.

Proof.

By
[
Reg
]
*Lemma 2.6 with
B^{*}=B
it follows that
G^{*}=(G^{*}\cap A)B
. Suppose now that
L\neq\mathrm{P}\Omega_{8}^{+}(3)
.
Since
L
is a classical group, and
M_{12}
is not,
[
Reg
]
*Lemma 2.6 shows that either
the triple
(L,A\cap L,B\cap L)
is given by parts (a) or (b), or
G^{*}=(G^{*}\cap A)B
is a maximal core-free
factorisation. Assume that the latter holds. It remains to prove that
B=N_{G}(B\cap L)
. Since
L\trianglelefteqslant G
we have that
B\leqslant N_{G}(B\cap L)
. Since
B
is maximal in
G^{*}
it follows from the argument in the proof of
[
Wilson1985
]
*Lemma 2.1 that
B\cap L\neq 1
. Hence
L\not\leqslant N_{G}(B\cap L)
. Thus
N_{G}(B\cap L)
is core-free in
G
and so the maximality of
B
among core-free subgroups of
G
implies that
B=N_{G}(B\cap L)
.
∎

We also need the following number theoretic lemma.

Lemma 2.3
.

If
q=p^{f}
where
p
is prime,
m\geqslant 2
and
f\geqslant 1
, then either
m=2
,
f=1
and
p\in\{3,7\}
or

(q^{m}-1)\nmid 2mf(q-1)^{2}
.

Proof.

Let
N=(q^{m}-1)/(q-1)
and
d=(N,q-1)
. Suppose that
N\mid 2mf(q-1)
. Since
N/d
divides
2mf(q-1)/d
and

N/d
and
(q-1)/d
are coprime, we see that

N/d
divides
2mf
. However,
N=\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}q^{i}\equiv m\mod(q-1)
,
so it follows that
d=(m,q-1)
and
N
divides
2m^{2}f
. Thus

2^{(m-1)f}\leqslant p^{(m-1)f}=q^{m-1}<\frac{q^{m}-1}{q-1}\leqslant 2m^{2}f.

However,
2^{(m-1)f}\leqslant 2m^{2}f
has only 13 solutions for
(m,f)
. Since
11^{(m-1)f}\leqslant 2m^{2}f
has no solutions, we see that
p\in\{2,3,5,7\}
. Of the
52
possibilities for
(m,f,p)
, the only possibilities that satisfy
(p^{fm}-1)\mid 2mf(p^{f}-1)^{2}
have
m=2
,
f=1
and
p\in\{3,7\}
.
∎

Remark 3.2
.

Observe that
\mathrm{Sp}_{2}(q)=\mathrm{SL}_{2}(q)
and
\Gamma\mathrm{Sp}_{2}(q)=\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{2}(q)
. Moreover, all 1-dimensional subspaces of
V=(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{2}
are totally isotropic. Thus Theorem
3.1
in the case
n=2
also provides all the subgroups of
\Gamma\mathrm{Sp}_{2}(q)
that are transitive on the set of all totally isotropic
1
-subspaces.

\triangle

We use Theorem
3.1
to deal with some small dimensional orthogonal groups in Subsection
3.1
and the two-dimensional unitary groups in Subsection
3.2
.

3.1.
Some small dimensional orthogonal groups

Isomorphisms between some small dimensional orthogonal groups and other classical groups make it more convenient to treat
such groups in tandem with the other classical groups to which they are isomorphic. Since the
2
-dimensional orthogonal groups are soluble,
we only consider dimensions at least
3
. Apart from the generic isomorphisms
\mathrm{P}\Omega_{2m+1}(q)\cong\mathrm{PSp}_{2m}(q)
for all
m
when
q
is even, the exceptional isomorphisms involve groups of dimensions up to
6
and are the following:

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}\Omega_{3}(q)\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q),

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}\Omega_{4}^{+}(q)\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q)^{2},

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}\Omega_{4}^{-}(q)\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q^{2}),

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}\Omega_{5}(q)\cong\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(q)\;(q\;\;{\rm odd}),

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}\Omega_{6}^{+}(q)\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{4}(q),

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}\Omega_{6}^{-}(q)\cong\mathrm{PSU}_{4}(q).

We omit the non-simple case
\mathrm{P}\Omega_{4}^{+}(q)\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q)^{2}
, as discussed in Subsection
2.3
.

These isomorphisms of simple groups influence the subgroup structure of the corresponding automorphism groups, in particular giving useful information about subspace stabilisers. For example, the exceptional isomorphism
\mathrm{P}\Omega_{6}^{+}(q)\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{4}(q)
relates a subspace stabilizer
in the
\mathcal{N}_{1}
-action of
\mathrm{P}\Omega_{6}^{+}(q)
on
V=(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{6}
to a symplectic group
\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(q)
acting on
W=(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{4}
– not a subspace stabilizer. The action of
\mathrm{PSL}_{4}(q)
on the set of cosets of
\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(q)
is not one we are considering for linear groups, and so must be treated specially for the orthogonal groups
\mathrm{P}\Omega_{6}^{+}(q)
. On the other hand, exceptional isomorphisms can relate
subspace stabilisers, sometimes for different types of spaces, for example, the stabilisers of subspaces in
\mathcal{P}_{1}(V)
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}(W)
correspond under the isomorphism
\mathrm{P}\Omega_{6}^{+}(q)\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{4}(q)
.

In Table
3.2
, we list the subgroups corresponding (under the displayed exceptional isomorphisms) to stabilisers in the orthogonal groups of the relevant totally singular, nondegenerate, and nonsingular
1
-subspaces.
Justifications for these correspondences will be given in the places where we deal with the individual families. Essentially, the correspondences are determined using the structure of the subspace stabilisers given in
[
BHRD
]
*Table 2.3, p. 61. Parabolic subgroups have a normal subgroup (the unipotent radical) whose order is a power of
q
, so parabolic subgroups correspond to parabolic subgroups and the exponent of
q
often determines the correspondences
P_{i}\leftrightarrow P_{j}
in Table
3.2
. For example,
P_{1}(\mathrm{P}\Omega_{6}^{+}(q))=P_{2}(\mathrm{PSL}_{4}(q))
has a unipotent radical of order
q^{4}
, and not
q^{5}
. The remaining cases use
[
BHRD
]
*Table 2.3 and the maximal subgroups in dimensions
2
and
4

[
BHRD
]
*pp. 377, 381, 392.

\mathrm{P}\Omega_{5}(q)\cong\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(q)
take
q
to be odd. The case
q
even is handled by Theorem
7.2
.

In the remainder of this section we use Theorem
3.1
to deal with those orthogonal groups isomorphic to linear groups,
namely
\mathrm{P}\Omega_{3}(q),\mathrm{P}\Omega_{4}^{-}(q),
and
\mathrm{P}\Omega_{6}^{+}(q)
. The groups
\mathrm{P}\Omega_{5}(q)
are dealt with in Section
5
for
q
odd, and in Section
7.2
for
q
even, while the groups
\mathrm{P}\Omega_{6}^{-}(q)
are handled in Subsection
4.1
.

Note that for
G=\mathrm{GO}_{3}(2)
, the set
\mathcal{N}_{2}^{-}
has size one and so we exclude this case below.

Theorem 3.3
.

Suppose that
H\leqslant\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{3}(q)
acts transitively on a set
\mathcal{U}
of subspaces of the natural module
V=(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{3}
given in Table
2.3
, with
\Omega_{3}(q)\not\leqslant H
and if
q=2
then
\mathcal{U}\neq\mathcal{N}_{2}^{-}
. Then one of the following holds, where
\overline{H}
is the image of
H
in
\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{2}(q)
and
q=p^{f}
with
p
prime:

(a)

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
H\leqslant N_{\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{3}(q)}(\mathrm{GO}_{2}^{-}(q))=N_{2}^{-}
, or

\textup{C}_{2}^{2}\trianglelefteqslant H
and
q\in\{5,7,11,23\}
, or
\mathrm{Alt}_{5}\trianglelefteqslant H
and
q\in\{9,11,19,29,59\}
; or

(b)

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{1}^{-}
with
q
odd, and
H\leqslant P_{1}
; or

(c)

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{2}^{-}
with
q>2
even, and either
H\leqslant P_{1}
, or
q=4
and
H\leqslant N_{G}(\Omega_{2}^{+}(4))=N_{2}^{+}
, or
q=16
and
\mathrm{GO}_{3}(4).4\trianglelefteqslant H
where the ‘
4
’ induces a field automorphism of order
2
on
\mathrm{GO}_{3}(4)
; or

(d)

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}
with
q=4
and
\mathrm{GO}_{2}^{-}(4).2\trianglelefteqslant H\leqslant N_{2}^{-}
, where the ‘
2
’ acts as a field automorphism.

Proof.

By Table
2.3
, one of the following holds:
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{1}
, or
q
is odd and
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{1}^{+}
or
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{-}
, or
q
is even and
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{1},\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}
or
\mathcal{N}_{2}^{-}
. Moreover,
q\neq 2
when
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{2}^{-}
. Thus
G=\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{3}(q)
fixes
\mathcal{U}
setwise. By
[
Taylor
]
*11.6 Theorem we have
\mathrm{SO}_{3}(q)\cong\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(q)
and so
\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{3}(q)\cong(Z\times\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(q))\rtimes\textup{C}_{f}
where
q=p^{f}
,
Z
is the group of scalar matrices and
\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{3}(q)\cong\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{2}(q)
.

For each
X\leqslant\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{3}(q)
let
\overline{X}
denote the image of
X
in
\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{2}(q)
. Let
H\leqslant\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{3}(q)
act transitively on
\mathcal{U}
such that
\Omega_{3}(q)\not\leqslant H
. We note that
q\geqslant 3
, for if
q=2
then
\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{3}(q)=\mathrm{GO}_{3}(2)\cong\mathrm{Sym}_{3}
and
\Omega_{3}(2)\cong\mathrm{Alt}_{3}
, and since
|\mathcal{P}_{1}|=|\mathcal{N}_{1}|=|\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}|=3
divides
|H|
it follows that
\Omega_{3}(2)\trianglelefteqslant H
, a contradiction.

When
q=3
we have
|Z|=2
,
\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{3}(3)=\mathrm{CO}_{3}(3)\cong\textup{C}_{2}\times\mathrm{Sym}_{4}
, and
\mathrm{Alt}_{4}\cong\Omega_{3}(3)\not\leqslant H
. Here
|\mathcal{P}_{1}|=4
,
|\mathcal{N}_{1}^{+}|=6
and
|\mathcal{N}_{1}^{-}|=3
. Thus if
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{1}
then
\overline{H}\leqslant D_{8}\cong\textup{C}_{4}\rtimes\textup{C}_{2}
, as in part (a). If
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{1}^{-}
then any subgroup of
G
of order divisible by 3 is transitive on
\mathcal{U}
and since
H\not\geqslant\Omega_{3}(3)
, it follows that
H\leqslant\textup{C}_{2}\times\mathrm{Sym}_{3}=P_{1}
, as in part (b). Finally, if
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{1}^{+}
then a
Magma
calculation shows that
\mathrm{Alt}_{4}\cong\Omega_{3}(3)\trianglelefteqslant H
, a contradiction.

From now on we assume that
q\geqslant 4
. Thus
\mathrm{P}\Omega_{3}(q)\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q)
is a nonabelian simple group and so by Lemma
2.1
, we have
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q)\not\leqslant\overline{H}
.

Case
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{1}
.
Comparing orders and group structure we see that
\overline{P_{1}}
is a maximal parabolic subgroup of
\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{2}(q)
. In particular,
\overline{H}
acts transitively on the set of 1-dimensional subspaces of a 2-dimensional vector space over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Thus by Theorem
3.1
either
\textup{C}_{2}^{2}
or
\mathrm{Alt}_{5}\trianglelefteqslant\overline{H}
for particular values of
q
, or
\overline{H}\leqslant\textup{C}_{q+1}\rtimes\textup{C}_{2f}
. Since
\mathrm{SO}_{3}(q)\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q)
the first two cases imply that
\textup{C}_{2}^{2}
or
\mathrm{Alt}_{5}\trianglelefteqslant H
, while comparing
[
BHRD
]
*Tables 8.1 and 8.7 we deduce that the last case implies
H\leqslant N_{G}(\Omega_{2}^{-}(q))=N_{2}^{-}
. Thus
H
satisfies part (a) of the theorem.

Case

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{1}^{-}
with
q
odd, or
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{2}^{-}
with
q>2
even. In these cases
\mathrm{GO}^{-}_{2}(q)\cong D_{2(q+1)}
, and we have
G_{U}=(Z\times D_{2(q+1)})\rtimes\textup{C}_{f}
, whence
\overline{G_{U}}=N_{\overline{G}}(D_{\delta(q+1)})
where
\delta=\gcd(2,q)
. The latter subgroup is maximal in
\overline{G}
by
[
BHRD
]
*Tables 8.1 and 8.2, so the conditions of Lemma
2.2
hold for the factorisation

\overline{G}=\overline{H}N_{\overline{G}}(D_{\delta(q+1)})
. Hence by Lemma
2.2
we get a maximal core-free factorisation of an almost simple group
G^{*}
with socle
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q)
and one factor being
N_{G^{*}}(D_{\delta(q+1)})
. Thus by
[
Factns
]
*Theorem A, Tables 1 and 3, either
q=4,5,9
or
16
, or
\overline{H}\leqslant\overline{P_{1}}
. The latter case implies that
H\leqslant P_{1}
as in part (b) or (c), and so it remains to deal with the small values of
q
. When
q=4
we have
|\mathcal{N}_{2}^{-}|=6
and a
Magma
calculation implies that either
H\leqslant P_{1}
or
\overline{H}\cong\textup{C}_{6}
or
D_{12}
. Since
\mathrm{GO}_{3}(4)\cong\mathrm{Alt}_{5}
has a unique class of subgroups isomorphic to
\textup{C}_{3}
and
\Omega_{2}^{+}(4)\cong\textup{C}_{3}
, it follows that
H\leqslant N_{G}(\Omega_{2}^{+}(4))=N_{2}^{+}
as in part (c).
When
q=5
we have
|\mathcal{N}_{1}^{-}|=10
and so any subgroup of
G
with order divisible by 10 is contained in
P_{1}
as in part (b). When
q=9
we have
|\mathcal{N}_{1}^{-}|=36
, and all subgroups with order divisible by
9
are contained in
P_{1}
as in part (b). When
q=16
we have
|\mathcal{N}_{2}^{-}|=120
, and either
H\leqslant P_{1}
or
\overline{H}=(\mathrm{Alt}_{5}\times 2)\cdot 2
as in part (c), see
[
Factns
]
*5.1.1, p. 91. Moreover, by Lemma
2.1
,
\mathrm{Alt}_{5}\cong\mathrm{GO}_{3}(4)\lhd H
. Since
N_{G}(\mathrm{GO}_{3}(4))=(\mathrm{GO}_{3}(4)\times Z).4
where the 4 induces a field automorphism of order 2 on
\mathrm{GO}_{3}(4)
, it follows that
\mathrm{GO}_{3}(4).4\trianglelefteqslant H
as in part (c).

Case

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{1}^{+}
with
q
odd, or
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}
with
q
even. In these cases
\mathrm{GO}_{2}^{+}(q)\cong D_{2(q-1)}
, and we have
\overline{G_{U}}=N_{\overline{G}}(D_{\delta(q-1)})
where
\delta=\gcd(2,q)
. By
[
BHRD
]
*Tables 8.1 and 8.2,
\overline{G_{U}}
is maximal in
\overline{G}
unless
q=4
or
5
. When
q=4
we have
|\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}|=10
and a
Magma
calculation shows that
\overline{H}=F_{20}
. Now
\textup{C}_{5}\cong\Omega_{2}^{-}(4)\leqslant\mathrm{GO}_{3}(4)\cong\mathrm{Alt}_{5}
. Since
G
has a unique conjugacy class of subgroups of order 5 and
|Z|=3
, it follows that
\Omega_{2}^{-}(4)\trianglelefteqslant H
. Now
N_{G}(\Omega_{2}^{-}(4))=(\mathrm{GO}_{2}^{-}(4)\times Z).2=N_{2}^{-}
where the 2 acts as a field automorphism on
\mathrm{GO}_{2}^{-}(4)
. Since
|Z|=3
and
\overline{H}=F_{20}
it follows that
\mathrm{GO}_{2}^{-}(4).2\trianglelefteqslant H
as in part (d). When
q=5
we have
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(5)\cong S_{5}
and
|\mathcal{N}_{1}^{+}|=15
. A
Magma
calculation then shows that
\mathrm{Alt}_{5}\cong\Omega_{3}(5)\trianglelefteqslant H
, which is not the case. Hence
q\neq 5
and the conditions of Lemma
2.2
hold for the factorisation

\overline{G}=\overline{H}N_{\overline{G}}(D_{\delta(q-1)})
. By Lemma
2.2
we get a maximal core-free factorisation of an almost simple group
G^{*}
with socle
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q)
and one factor being
N_{G^{*}}(D_{\delta(q-1)})
. However
[
Factns
]
*Theorem A, Tables 1 and 3 implies that there are no such factorisations unless possibly when
q=9
. When
q=9
we have
|\mathcal{U}|=|\mathcal{N}_{1}^{+}|=45
. However, all subgroups of
\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{2}(9)
with order divisible by
45
contain
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(9)
.

Case

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{1}
with
q
even. Here
\mathcal{U}
consists of the
q^{2}-1
nonsingular
1
-subspaces of
V
, apart from
V^{\perp}
. For
U\in\mathcal{U}
, the
2
-subspace
U+V^{\perp}
contains a unique totally singular
1
-subspace, say
W
, and
G_{U+V^{\perp}}=G_{W}
contains
G_{U}
. It follows that

G
acts imprimitively on
\mathcal{U}
inducing an action on a system of imprimitivity that is permutationally isomorphic to its action on
\mathcal{P}_{1}
. In particular,
the subgroup
H
is transitive on
\mathcal{P}_{1}
since it is transitive on
\mathcal{U}
. Then by part (a), and since
q
is even, we have
\overline{H}\leqslant\textup{C}_{q+1}\rtimes\textup{C}_{2f}
.
However, since the scalar subgroup
Z
acts trivially on
\mathcal{U}
the size
|\mathcal{U}|=q^{2}-1
must divide
|\overline{H}|
and hence must divide
2f(q+1)
. Thus
q-1
divides
2f
, a contradiction.
∎

Theorem 3.4
.

Suppose that
H\leqslant\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{4}^{-}(q)
acts transitively on a set
\mathcal{U}
of subspaces of the natural module
V=(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{4}
given by Table
2.3
, and where
\Omega_{4}^{-}(q)\not\leqslant H
.
Then one of the following holds, where
\overline{H}
is the image of
H
in
\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{2}(q^{2})
:

(a)

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
H\leqslant N_{\Gamma\mathrm{O}^{-}_{4}(q)}(\Omega_{2}^{-}(q^{2}))
, or
q=3
and
\mathrm{Alt}_{5}\trianglelefteqslant H
; or

(b)

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{1}^{\pm}
,
q=3
and
\mathrm{Alt}_{5}\trianglelefteqslant H
(one class of subgroups for each of
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{+}
and
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{-}
); or
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{1}
and either
q=2
and
H=\mathrm{GO}_{2}^{-}(4).2
where the
`2
’ acts as a field automorphism, or
q=4
and
\mathrm{GO}_{2}^{-}(4^{2}).4\trianglelefteqslant H
where the ‘
4
’ acts as a field automorphism; or

(c)

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{2}^{\pm}
,
q=2
and
H=\mathrm{GO}_{2}^{-}(4).2
where the ‘
2
’ acts as a field automorphism.

Proof.

By Table
2.3
,
\mathcal{U}
is one of
\mathcal{P}_{1}
,
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{\varepsilon}
(with
q
odd and
\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}
),
\mathcal{N}_{1}
(with
q
even), or
\mathcal{N}_{2}^{\varepsilon}
(with
\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}
). Fix
U\in\mathcal{U}
and set
G=\textup{Stab}_{\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{4}^{-}(q)}(\mathcal{U})
. Then
G=\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{4}^{-}(q)
unless
q
is odd and
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{1}^{\varepsilon}
, in which case
G
is an index two subgroup of
\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{4}^{-}(q)
.

By
[
KL
]
*Proposition 2.9.1 we have
\Omega_{4}^{-}(q)\cong\mathrm{P}\Omega_{4}^{-}(q)\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q^{2})
and we deduce that
\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{4}^{-}(q)\cong\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{2}(q^{2})
. For
X\leqslant\Gamma\mathrm{O}^{-}_{4}(q)
we denote the image of
X
in
\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{2}(q^{2})
by
\overline{X}
.

(a) Suppose first that
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{1}
. Comparing orders and group structure, we find that, for
P_{1}=G_{U}
, the image
\overline{P_{1}}
is a maximal parabolic subgroup of
\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{2}(q^{2})
. In particular
\overline{H}
acts transitively on the set of 1-dimensional subspaces of a 2-dimensional vector space over
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}
. Thus
\overline{H}
is given by Theorem
3.1
, and so, since
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q^{2})\not\leqslant\overline{H}
, either
\overline{H}\leqslant\textup{C}_{q^{2}+1}\rtimes\textup{C}_{4f}
or
q=3
and
\mathrm{Alt}_{5}\trianglelefteqslant\overline{H}
. Since
\Omega_{4}^{-}(q)\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q^{2})
the latter implies that
\mathrm{Alt}_{5}\trianglelefteqslant H
, while comparing
[
BHRD
]
*Tables 8.1 and 8.17 we deduce from the former that
H\leqslant N_{G}(\Omega_{2}^{-}(q^{2}))
, so part (a) holds.

(b) Next suppose that
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{1}
with
q
even, or
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{\varepsilon}
with
q
odd. Here
G=\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{4}^{-}(q)
when
q
is even,
and is an index two subgroup when
q
is odd. Since
\Omega_{3}(q)\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q)
, we see from
[
BHRD
]
*Table 8.17 that
\overline{G_{U}}=N_{\overline{G}}(\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q))
, which is maximal in
\overline{G}
unless
q=2
. For
q=2
we have
\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{4}^{-}(2)=\mathrm{SO}_{4}^{-}(2)\cong\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(4)\cong\mathrm{Sym}_{5}
and
|\mathcal{N}_{1}|=10
; and a
Magma
calculation shows, since
\Omega_{4}^{-}(2)\not\leqslant H
, that
H\cong F_{20}
. Since
\mathrm{SO}_{4}^{-}(2)
has a unique conjugacy class of subgroups of order 5, it follows that
H=\mathrm{GO}_{2}^{-}(4).2
where the ‘
2
’ acts as a field automorphism, as in (b). When
q>2
, the conditions of Lemma
2.2
hold for the factorisation
\overline{G}=\overline{G_{U}}N_{\overline{G}}(\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q))
, and so there is maximal core-free factorisation of an almost simple group
G^{*}
with socle
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q^{2})
with one factor being
N_{G^{*}}(\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q))
. Thus by
[
Factns
]
, either
q=3
, or
q=4
and
\overline{H}=N_{\overline{G}}(D_{34})=\textup{C}_{17}\rtimes\textup{C}_{8}
. When
q=4
the group
Z
of scalar matrices has order 3 and so
\textup{C}_{17}\cong\Omega_{2}^{-}(4^{2})\trianglelefteqslant H\leqslant(\mathrm{GO}_{2}^{-}(4^{2})\times Z).4
where the
4
acts a field automorphism of
\mathrm{GO}_{2}^{-}(4^{2})
. Since
\overline{H}=\textup{C}_{17}\rtimes\textup{C}_{8}
it follows that
\mathrm{GO}_{2}^{-}(4^{2}).4\trianglelefteqslant H
as in part (b). For
q=3
, a
Magma
calculation shows that
\mathrm{Alt}_{5}\trianglelefteqslant\overline{H}
, and since
\Omega_{4}^{-}(3)\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(9)
it follows that
\mathrm{Alt}_{5}\trianglelefteqslant H
. Moreover,
G
has two conjugacy classes of
\mathrm{Alt}_{5}
-subgroups fused in
\mathrm{CO}_{4}^{-}(q)
, and each class of subgroups acts transitively on only one of the two sets
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{+}
or
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{-}
. Thus part (b) holds

(c) Finally consider
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{2}^{\varepsilon}
. If
U\in\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}
then
U^{\perp}\in\mathcal{N}_{2}^{-}
. Thus
H
is transitive on
\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}
if and only if it is transitive on
\mathcal{N}_{2}^{-}
, and so we assume that
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}
. By
[
BHRD
]
*Table 8.17 we have that
\overline{G_{U}}=N_{\overline{G}}(D_{q^{2}-1})
and is maximal in
\overline{G}
unless

. Thus by Lemma

and
[
Factns
]

or

. When

and by a

. We deduce as above that

as in part (c). When

and a

H
.
∎

Proof.

By Table
2.3
,
\mathcal{U}
is one of
\mathcal{P}_{1}
,
\mathcal{P}_{2}
,
\mathcal{P}_{3}^{\varepsilon}
, or
\mathcal{N}_{2}^{\varepsilon}
(with
\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}
), or
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{\varepsilon}
or
\mathcal{N}_{3}^{\varepsilon}
(with
q
odd and
\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}
), or
\mathcal{N}_{1}
(with
q
even).
Fix
U\in\mathcal{U}
and set
G=\textup{Stab}_{\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{6}^{+}(q)}(\mathcal{U})
. Then
G=\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{6}^{+}(q)
unless
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{3}^{\varepsilon}
, or
q
is odd and
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{1}^{\varepsilon}
or
\mathcal{N}_{3}^{\varepsilon}
, in which case
G
is an index two subgroup of
\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{6}^{+}(q)
.

By
[
Taylor
]
*12.18 Theorem,
\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{O}^{+}_{6}(q)\cong\mathrm{Aut}(\mathrm{PSL}_{4}(q))
. For any
X\leqslant\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{6}^{+}(q)
we denote the image of
X
in
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{4}(q))
by
\overline{X}
. Moreover, if
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{3}^{\varepsilon}
then it follows from
[
Taylor
]
*12.16 Theorem that
\overline{G}\cong\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{4}(q)
. We denote the 4-dimensional vector space over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
acted on by
\mathrm{SL}_{4}(q)
by
W
.

(a1)
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{1}
. By
[
Taylor
]
*p. 187,
\mathcal{P}_{1}
is in one-to-one correspondence with the set of 2-dimensional subspaces of
W
. Thus
H
acts transitively on
\mathcal{P}_{1}
if and only if
\overline{H}
acts transitively on the set of 2-dimensional subspaces of
W
. Thus by Theorem
3.1
, either
\mathrm{PSL}_{4}(q)\trianglelefteqslant\overline{H}
or
q=2
and
\mathrm{Alt}_{7}\trianglelefteqslant H
. In the first case, Lemma
2.1
implies that
\Omega_{6}^{+}(q)\trianglelefteqslant H
as
\Omega_{6}^{+}(q)
is quasisimple, and this is a contradiction. Thus part (a) holds for this case.

(a2)
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{2}
. Comparing composition factors of subspace stabilisers, we see that there is a one-to-one correspondence between the elements of
\mathcal{P}_{2}
and the flags
X<Y
where
X
is a 1-dimensional subspace of
W
and
Y
is a hyperplane of
W
. By
[
BHRD
]
*Table 8.8, the stabiliser of such a flag is maximal in
\overline{G}=\mathrm{Aut}(\mathrm{PSL}_{4}(q))
. Then by Lemma
2.2
and
[
Factns
]
(except potentially when
q=2
as here
\mathrm{PSL}_{4}(2)\cong\mathrm{Alt}_{8}
),
\mathrm{PSL}_{4}(q)\trianglelefteqslant\overline{H}
and we again have that
\Omega_{6}^{+}(q)\trianglelefteqslant H
, a contradiction. If
q=2
then
|\mathcal{P}_{2}|=105
and a
Magma
calculation reveals that
\mathrm{Alt}_{7}\trianglelefteqslant H
is the unique example, as in part (a).

(b)
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{3}^{\varepsilon}
. In this case
\overline{G}=\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{4}(q)
and
G=A
. Moreover, if
\tau\in\Gamma\mathrm{O}^{+}_{6}(q)\backslash A
then
H
is transitive on
\mathcal{P}_{3}^{+}
if and only if
H^{\tau}
is transitive on
\mathcal{P}_{3}^{-}
, so we may assume that
\varepsilon=+
. By
[
Taylor
]
*12.16 Theorem,
\mathcal{P}_{3}^{+}
is in one-to-one correspondence with the set of 1-dimensional subspaces of
W
(and
\mathcal{P}_{3}^{-}
is in one-to-one correspondence with the set of 3-dimensional subspaces of
W
). Thus
\overline{H}
is transitive on the set of 1-dimensional subspaces of
W
and so is provided by Theorem
3.1
. We note that
\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(q)
appears in
\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{6}^{+}(q)
as a copy of
\Omega_{5}(q)
fixing a hyperplane (see
[
BHRD
]
*Tables 8.31 and 8.32). Also, the
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(q^{2})
-subgroup of
\mathrm{SL}_{4}(q)
that is transitive on 1-subspaces is a
\mathcal{C}_{3}
-subgroup of
\mathrm{SL}_{4}(q)
and then comparing
[
BHRD
]
*Tables 8.8 and 8.31 we see that it occurs as
\Omega_{4}^{-}(q)
as a subgroup of
\Omega_{6}^{+}(q)
fixing a nondegenerate 4-subspace. Also note that
\Omega_{6}^{+}(q)\cong\mathrm{SL}_{4}(q)/\langle\pm I_{4}\rangle
and so the examples
2_{-}^{1+4}\ldotp\textup{C}_{5}
,
2_{-}^{1+4}\ldotp\mathrm{Alt}_{5}
and
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(5)
and appearing in Table
3.1
for
q=3
become
2^{4}\ldotp\textup{C}_{5}
,
2^{4}\ldotp\mathrm{Alt}_{5}
and
\mathrm{Alt}_{5}
in this case. Furthermore,
\mathrm{Alt}_{6}
occurs as
\Omega_{5}(2)^{R}\leqslant\Omega_{6}^{+}(2)
. Finally, for
\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{1}(q^{4})\leqslant\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{4}(q)
, note that
\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{1}(q^{4})\leqslant\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{2}(q^{2})\leqslant\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{4}(q)
. Moreover, comparing
[
BHRD
]
*Tables 8.1 and 8.31 we see that the middle subgroup in this chain yields an
N_{2}^{-}
subgroup of
A
as
\Omega_{4}^{-}(q)\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q^{2})
. Then as
D_{2(q^{2}+1)}\cong\mathrm{GO}_{2}^{-}(q^{2})
and
D_{2(q+1)}\cong\mathrm{GO}_{2}^{-}(q)
we deduce that
H\leqslant N_{A}(\Omega_{2}^{-}(q^{2}))\leqslant N_{4}^{-}
.
Thus case (b) holds.

(c)
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{1}^{\varepsilon}
(with
q
odd and
\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}
), or
\mathcal{N}_{1}
(with
q
even). This case was dealt with in
[
Reg
]
*Lemma 4.5 and
H
is as given in part (c).

(d1)
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{2}^{-}
. Here
G=\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{6}^{+}(q)
and since
\Omega^{-}_{4}(q)\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q^{2})
we deduce from
[
BHRD
]
*Table 8.8 that
\overline{G_{U}}=N_{\overline{G}}(\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q^{2}))
, which is maximal in
\overline{G}
. Let
\overline{B}
be a subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathrm{PSL}_{4}(q))
that contains
\overline{H}
and is maximal subject to being core-free. Then by Lemma
2.2
and
[
Factns
]
we have that
\overline{B}
is the stabiliser of a 1-dimensional subspace of
W
, a 3-dimensional subspace of
W
, or a decomposition of
W
into a 1-dimensional space and a complementary 3-subspace. Moreover, in the third case
q=2
or
4
.

Suppose that we are in one of the first two cases. As discussed in case (b), 1-dimensional subspaces and 3-dimensional subspaces of
W
correspond to totally singular 3-subspaces of
V
. Thus
H
is contained in the stabiliser
B
of a totally singular 3-subspace
E
of
V
and we have a factorisation
B=HB_{U}
. Note that
U\cap E=0
and following
[
Factns
]
*(3.6.2b), p. 68 we see that
Y:=E\cap U^{\perp}
has dimension 1 so that
V=U^{\perp}+E
. Moreover
Y
is
B_{U}
-invariant, so
H
acts transitively on the 1-dimensional subspaces of
E
. Thus by Theorem
3.1
, modulo its unipotent radical
H
induces either
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(q)
or a subgroup of
\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{1}(q^{3})
on
E
. Now

|\mathcal{N}_{2}^{-}|=\frac{|\mathrm{GO}^{+}_{6}(q)|}{|\mathrm{GO}_{2}^{-}(q)||\mathrm{GO}_{4}^{-}(q)|}=\frac{1}{2}q^{4}(q^{3}-1)(q+1)

divides
|H|
, and
H\leqslant[q^{3}]\rtimes\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{3}(q)
. Thus
|H^{E}|
is divisible by
q/(2,q)
. Suppose that
H^{E}\leqslant\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{1}(q^{3})
. Thus
q/(2,q)
divides
3f
and so
q\leqslant 4
. Similarly, if
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(q)\leqslant H^{E}\leqslant\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{3}(q)
and
q>4
then
q^{4}/(2,q)
does not divide
|H^{E}|
and so
H\cap R\neq 1
, where
R
is the unipotent radical of
B
. Since
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(q)
acts irreducibly on
R
(view it as a subgroup of
\mathrm{SL}_{4}(q)
) it follows that either
q\leqslant 4
, or
R\rtimes\mathrm{SL}_{3}(q)\leqslant H
as in part (d). A
Magma
calculation then reveals the list of groups given by Table
3.4
when
q\leqslant 4
.

(d2)
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}(V)
. Since
\mathrm{P}\Omega_{4}^{+}(q)\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q)^{2}
, by comparing
[
BHRD
]
*Table 8.8 and Table 8.31 we deduce that
\overline{G_{U}}
is the stabiliser in
\overline{G}
of a decomposition of
W
into two complementary
2
-subspaces. The usual argument combined with
[
Factns
]
shows that (except possibly for
q=2
as
\mathrm{PSL}_{4}(2)\cong\mathrm{Alt}_{8}
), no possibility for
H
occurs. A
Magma
calculation reveals that in the exceptional case
q=2
there are no examples either.

(e)
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{3}^{\varepsilon}(V)
(with
q
odd and
\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}
). By comparing
[
BHRD
]
*Tables 8.8 and 8.31,
\overline{G_{U}}
is an index two subgroup of
N_{\overline{G}}(\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q)^{2})
, which is maximal in
\overline{G}
and which, as a subgroup of
\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{4}(q)
, is of type
\mathrm{GO}_{4}^{+}(q)
. Let
\overline{B}
be a subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathrm{PSL}_{4}(q))
that contains
\overline{H}
and is maximal subject to being core-free. Then by Lemma
2.2
we have a maximal core-free factorisation of an almost simple group with socle
\mathrm{PSL}_{4}(q)
where one of the factors has socle
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q)^{2}
. However, by
[
Factns
]
there is no such factorisation for
q
odd. ∎

3.2.
Unitary groups in small dimension

Since
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q)\cong\mathrm{PSU}_{2}(q)
we now deal with unitary groups in dimension
n=2
. Let
K=\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}
where
q=p^{r}
for some prime
p
, let
k=\mathbb{F}_{q}\leqslant K
, and let
V
be a 2-dimensional vector space over
K
. Let
(\cdot,\cdot)
be a nondegenerate hermitian form on
V
and let
\{e,f\}
be a basis of
V
such that
(e,e)=(f,f)=0
and
(e,f)=1
. Choose
0\neq\zeta\in K
such that
\zeta^{q}+\zeta=0
. We take a new basis
\{e,f^{\prime}\}
for
V
where
f^{\prime}=\zeta^{q}f
, and note that the Gram matrix for
(\cdot,\cdot)
with respect to this new basis is
\zeta J
where
J=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. Hence
(\alpha e+\beta f^{\prime},\gamma e+\delta f^{\prime})=(\alpha\delta^{q}-\beta\gamma^{q})\zeta
.

Consider the group
\Gamma\mathrm{U}_{2}(q)
of all semisimilarities of
(\cdot,\cdot)
. Let
A\in\mathrm{GL}_{2}(q^{2})
. Then
A
lies in
\mathrm{CU}_{2}(q)
precisely when
AJA^{\sigma T}=\delta_{A}J
for some
\delta_{A}\in K^{\times}
, where
\sigma
is the automorphism of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(q^{2})
that raises each entry of a matrix to its
q^{\mathrm{th}}
-power. A simple calculation shows that

AJA^{\sigma T}=\det(A)J
for all
A\in\mathrm{GL}_{2}(k)
. Thus
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(k)\leqslant\mathrm{CU}_{2}(q)
and
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(k)\leqslant\mathrm{GU}_{2}(q)
. Comparing orders shows that
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(k)=\mathrm{SU}_{2}(q)
. Note that for
A=\lambda I_{2}
with
\lambda\in K^{\times}
, we have
\delta_{A}=\lambda^{q+1}\in k^{\times}
. Thus for each
A\in\mathrm{GL}_{2}(k)
, there exists
\lambda\in K^{\times}
such that
\lambda A\in\mathrm{GU}_{2}(q)
. Letting
Z
be the subgroup of all
K
-scalars we have
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(k)Z\leqslant\mathrm{CU}_{2}(q)
and comparing orders we get equality. In particular,
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(q)\cong\mathrm{PCU}_{2}(q)=\mathrm{PGU}_{2}(q)
.

Let
\phi
be the transformation of
V
given by
(\alpha e+\beta f^{\prime})^{\phi}=\alpha^{p}e+\beta^{p}f^{\prime}
. Then
\phi
is a semisimilarity with
(u^{\phi},v^{\phi})=\zeta^{1-p}(u,v)^{p}
for all
u,v\in V
. Thus
\Gamma\mathrm{U}_{2}(q)=\langle\mathrm{CU}_{2}(q),\phi\rangle
is a group of order
2r|\mathrm{CU}_{2}(q)|
. Note that
\phi
normalises the subgroup
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(k)
and induces a field automorphism of order
r
. In particular, letting
z=\phi^{r}
we see that
z
centralises
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(k)
. Thus we have.

(1)

\begin{array}[]{ll}&\mbox{$G=\langle\mathrm{GL}_{2}(k),Z,\phi\rangle=\Gamma\mathrm{U}_{2}(q)$, $\overline{G}=\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{U}_{2}(q)$, and
$\overline{G}/\langle z\rangle\cong\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{2}(q)$, and}\\
&\mbox{let $\pi\colon\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{U}_{2}(q)\to\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{2}(q)$ be the natural epimorphism with kernel $\langle z\rangle$. }\end{array}

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
U
be a
1
-subspace of
V=K^{2}
, let
G,\overline{G}
, and

\pi
be as in
(
1
)
.

(a)

If
U
is totally isotropic then
\pi(\overline{G}_{U})
is the stabiliser in
\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{2}(q)
of a
1
-subspace of
k^{2}
.

(b)

If
U
is nondegenerate then
\pi(\overline{G}_{\{U,U^{\perp}\}})=N_{\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{2}(q)}(D_{2(q+1)})\cong\textup{C}_{q+1}\rtimes\textup{C}_{2r}
.

Proof.

(a) Since
\overline{G}
is transitive on the set of totally isotropic
1
-subspaces of
V
we may assume that
U=\langle e\rangle_{K}
. Since
Z
and
\phi
fix
U
it follows that
\overline{G}_{U}=\langle\mathrm{GL}_{2}(k)_{U},Z,\phi\rangle
. Noting that
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(k)_{U}=\mathrm{GL}_{2}(k)_{W}
, where
W=\langle e\rangle_{k}
, we deduce that
\pi(\overline{G}_{U})=\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{2}(q)_{W}
.

(b) Note that the totally isotropic
1
-subspaces of
V
are
\langle e\rangle
and
\langle\lambda e+f^{\prime}\rangle
for
\lambda\in k
. Thus the nondegenerate
1
-subspaces are those of the form
\langle\lambda e+f^{\prime}\rangle
with
\lambda\in K\backslash k
. Note that
\langle\lambda e+f^{\prime}\rangle^{\perp}=\langle\lambda^{q}e+f^{\prime}\rangle
and so the element
z
interchanges each nondegenerate
1
-subspace with its orthogonal complement. Thus
z
lies in the kernel of the action of
G
on pairs of orthogonal nondegenerate 1-subspaces. Note that there are
q^{2}-q
nondegenerate 1-subspaces in
V
and so there are
q(q-1)/2
orthogonal pairs. Moreover,
\overline{G}
acts transitively on the set of nondegenerate 1-subspaces. Let
U=\langle v_{1}\rangle
and
U^{\perp}=\langle v_{2}\rangle
, be nondegenerate
1
-subspaces over
K
. Then
\{v_{1}\,v_{2}\}
is a basis for
V
and we see that
\mathrm{GU}_{2}(q)_{\{U,U^{\perp}\}}\cong\textup{C}_{q+1}\,\mathrm{wr}\,\textup{C}_{2}
. Moreover, the map

\varphi:\lambda_{1}v_{1}+\lambda_{2}v_{2}\mapsto\lambda_{1}^{p}v_{1}+\lambda_{2}^{p}v_{2}

is a semisimilarity of order
2r
that fixes
U
and
U^{\perp}
. Thus

\Gamma\mathrm{U}_{2}(q)_{\{U,U^{\perp}\}}=\mathrm{GU}_{2}(q)_{\{U,U^{\perp}\}}Z\rtimes\langle\varphi\rangle

and so
\pi(\overline{G}_{\{U,U^{\perp}\}})\cong\textup{C}_{q+1}\rtimes\textup{C}_{2r}=N_{\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{2}(q)}(D_{2(q+1)})
.
∎

As discussed in Subsection
2.3
, the
\overline{G}
-orbits on subspaces that we consider are
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{N}_{1}
.
We now determine the subgroups of
G
that are transitive on
\mathcal{P}_{1}
or
\mathcal{N}_{1}
.

Theorem 3.7
.

Let
G,\overline{G}
, and
\pi
be as in
(
1
)
, and suppose that
H\leqslant G
acts transitively on the set
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{1}
or
\mathcal{N}_{1}
of subspaces of the natural module
V=(\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}})^{2}
, such that

\mathrm{SU}_{2}(q)\not\leqslant H
and
q\geqslant 2
.
Then either

(a)

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{1}
and one of
\pi(\overline{H})\leqslant N_{\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{2}(q)}(D_{2(q+1)})
, or
\textup{C}_{2}^{2}\lhd\pi(\overline{H})
with
q\in\{5,7,11,23\}
, or
A_{5}\lhd\pi(\overline{H})
with
q\in\{9,11,19,29,59\}
;
or

(b)

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{1}
and
H\leqslant P_{1}
, or
q=16
and
\pi(\overline{H})=N_{\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{2}(16)}(\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(4))
, or
q=2
.

Proof.

If
\mathrm{PSU}_{2}(q)=\pi(\overline{H})
, then Lemma
2.1
would imply that
\mathrm{SU}_{2}(q)\leqslant H
, contrary to our assumption. Hence
\mathrm{PSU}_{2}(q)\not\leqslant\pi(\overline{H})
.

(a) Suppose that
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{1}
and let
U\in\mathcal{U}
. Then
G=G_{U}H
and so by Lemma
3.6
we have
\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{2}(q)=\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{2}(q)_{W}\pi(\overline{H})
, where
W
is a 1-subspace of
k^{2}
. Thus the possibilities for
\pi(\overline{H})
are given by Theorem
3.1
, and so either
\pi(\overline{H})\leqslant N_{\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{2}(q)}(D_{2(q+1)})
, or
\textup{C}_{2}^{2}\lhd\pi(\overline{H})
with
q\in\{5,7,11,23\}
, or
A_{5}\lhd\pi(\overline{H})
with
q\in\{9,11,19,29,59\}
, as in part (a).

(b) Suppose that
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{1}
and let
U\in\mathcal{U}
. Since
H
acts transitively on
\mathcal{N}_{1}
it also acts transitively on the set of pairs of orthogonal nondegenerate 1-subspaces. Hence by Lemma
3.6
,
\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{2}(q)=\pi(\overline{H})N_{\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{2}(q)}(D_{q+1})
. If
q\geqslant 4
then Lemma
2.2
and
[
Factns
]
imply that
\pi(\overline{H})\leqslant P_{1}
, or
q=16
and
\pi(\overline{H})=N_{\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{2}(16)}(\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(4))
, as in part (b). If
q=3
then a
Magma
calculation implies that either
\mathrm{SU}_{2}(3)\leqslant H
or
H\leqslant P_{1}
. Finally, if
q=2
then
|\mathcal{N}_{1}|=2
and so any subgroup
H
of even order that is not contained in
N_{1}
is transitive.
∎

4.
Unitary groups

Let
V=(\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}})^{n}
be equipped with a nondegenerate hermitian form. As discussed in Subsection
2.3
, the families
\mathcal{U}
of subspaces of
V
that we consider are the sets
\mathcal{P}_{k}
of totally isotropic
k
-subspaces, and
\mathcal{N}_{k}
of nondegenerate
k
-subspaces, for
1\leqslant k\leqslant n/2
. For each of these sets
\mathcal{U}
, it follows from Witt’s Lemma that
\mathrm{GU}_{n}(q)
is transitive on
\mathcal{U}
, and it can be shown that
\mathrm{SU}_{n}(q)
is also transitive on
\mathcal{U}
, and moreover
\mathcal{U}
is invariant under
\Gamma\mathrm{U}_{n}(q)
.
In Theorem
4.1
we classify the subgroups of
\Gamma\mathrm{U}_{n}(q)
that are transitive on
\mathcal{U}
. Part (a) of this result is essentially a summary of
[
Reg
]
*Lemma 4.3. Part (b) for
n=4
and 5, and part (c) for
n=4
were done in
[
KLrank3
]
*Corollary 5.12 (see Remark
1.3
). We use Theorem
4.1
to deal with the six-dimensional orthogonal groups of minus type.

.

Proof.

Let
G=\Gamma\mathrm{U}_{n}(q)
and
H\leqslant G
be transitive on
\mathcal{U}
such that
\mathrm{SU}_{n}(q)\not\leqslant H
. Let
U\in\mathcal{U}
. Then we have a factorisation
G=HG_{U}
. If
U
is a nondegenerate subspace of dimension
n/2
then we also have a factorisation
G=HX
, where
X
is the stabiliser of the decomposition
V=U\oplus U^{\perp}
, which has
G_{U}
as an index two subgroup. By
[
BHRD
,
KL
]

X
is maximal in
G
unless
(n,q)=(4,2)
. When
(n,q)=(4,2)
, computations in
Magma
show that
\mathrm{SU}_{4}(2)\trianglelefteqslant H
, while for
(n,q)\neq(4,2)
, Lemma
2.2
and
[
Factns
]
*3.3.3 and 3.3.4 imply that there are so such factorisations. Thus
for actions on nondegenerate
k
-subspaces we have
k<n/2
. Suppose that
(n,q)\neq(3,2)
. Then by
[
King1981
]
and
[
King1981a
]
,
\mathrm{SU}_{n}(q)
acts primitively on
\mathcal{U}
, and so
G_{U}
is maximal and core-free in
G
.

We denote the image in
\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{U}_{n}(q)
of a subgroup
X\leqslant G
by
\overline{X}
. Then we have a factorisation
\overline{G}=\overline{H}\,\overline{G}_{U}
of the almost simple group
\overline{G}
. Since
\mathrm{SU}_{n}(q)
is quasisimple, Lemma
2.1
implies that
\mathrm{PSU}_{n}(q)\not\leqslant\overline{H}
. Let
\overline{B}
be maximal among core-free subgroups of
\overline{G}
containing
\overline{H}
and let
G^{*}=\overline{B}\,\mathrm{PSU}_{n}(q)
. Then by Lemma
2.2
,
\overline{B}
is maximal in
G^{*}
and we have a maximal core-free factorisation
G^{*}=\overline{B}(G^{*})_{U}
. Moreover,
\overline{B}=N_{\overline{G}}(\overline{B}\cap L)
and so letting
B
be the full preimage of
\overline{B}
in
G
, the Correspondence Theorem implies that
B=N_{G}(B\cap\mathrm{SU}_{n}(q))
. Since
(n,q)\neq(3,2)
the possibilities for
\mathcal{U}
and
\overline{B}
are given by
[
Factns
]
. We list the possibilities for
\mathcal{U}
in Table
4.4
along with the excluded case
(n,q)=(3,2)
.

we have

Case

. Here

. In this case

. By

in

is

and so

. Calculations with
Magma
show that

as in Table
4.2
.

Case

(n,q)=(3,5)
. It follows from
[
Factns
]
*p. 13 that
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{1}
and

. The preimage in
\mathrm{GU}_{3}(5)
of

is

]
*Table 8.6 the nonsplit and absolutely irreducible

3\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\mathrm{Alt}_{7}
.
Calculations with

show that
3\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\mathrm{Alt}_{7}\trianglelefteqslant H
.
Therefore
3\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\mathrm{Alt}_{7}\trianglelefteqslant H\leqslant 3\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\mathrm{Sym}_{7}
agreeing with Table
4.2
.

Case

(n,q)=(3,8)
. It follows from
[
Factns
]
*p. 13 that
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{1}
and

\overline{H}\leqslant N_{\overline{G}}(19.3)
, and moreover

, where

.
Note that

divides

. Now

is an exact factorisation
by
[
Factns
]
*p. 98. Since
|\mathrm{Aut}(L):L|=3^{2}\cdot 2
, it follows that

3\times 19\ldotp 9\trianglelefteqslant\overline{H}
.
The preimage of

in
G

is isomorphic to

\textup{C}_{513}\rtimes\textup{C}_{9}=\langle a,b\mid a^{9}=b^{513}=1,b^{a}=b^{4}\rangle
by

,
and so we have the entry in Table
4.2
.

and

. Here

. Thus Lemma

.

Case

. The preimage of

in

is the nonsplit absolutely irreducible subgroup

by

]
*Table 8.57. There are no

has no factorisations
by
[

*p. 16. Hence

3\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\textrm{J}_{3}\trianglelefteqslant H
agreeing with Table
4.2
.

*Lemma 4.3. However, the groups

and

. By Lemma

and

and

*p. 11, where

is the stabiliser of a nondegenerate

1
-subspace
\langle v\rangle
.

Case
8(a):
(m,q)=(2,2)
. Here we use
Magma
to construct
the subgroups
H
of
\Gamma\mathrm{U}_{4}(2)
that act transitively on
\mathcal{P}_{2}
with

\mathrm{SU}_{4}(2)\not\leqslant H
. Such subgroups
H
either contain
\mathrm{SU}_{3}(2)
as a normal subgroup, or have a normal subgroup
H_{0}
such that

H_{0}\in\{3_{+}^{1+2},3_{-}^{1+2},\textup{C}_{3}^{3}\}
as in
Table
4.3
. Thus case (c) holds in this case.

Case
8(b):
(m,q)=(2,3)
. Here we use
Magma
to determine all

H\leqslant\Gamma\mathrm{U}_{4}(3)
that act transitively on
\mathcal{P}_{2}
with

\mathrm{SU}_{4}(3)\not\leqslant H
. There are 14 conjugacy classes of such
H
,
and each normalizes and contains a subgroup conjugate to
H_{0}
where

H_{0}\in\{\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(7),4_{b}\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(4),\mathrm{SU}_{3}(3)\}
. The first two
possibilities are in Table
4.3
, while the last is listed in the main statement of Theorem
4.1
(c). Note also that the group
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(7)
given here is reducible and contained in
N_{1}
.

Case
8(c):
(m,q)\not\in\{(2,2),(2,3)\}
. Here
G_{U}=P_{m}
and
H\leqslant B=N_{1}=G_{\langle v\rangle}
where
\langle v\rangle

is a nondegenerate
1
-subspace. Then
G=G_{U}H
and
B=B_{U}H
where

B_{U}=B\cap G_{U}=G_{\langle v\rangle,U}
.
Since
B
fixes the nondegenerate
(2m-1)
-subspace

\langle v\rangle^{\perp}
, it also preserves the decomposition

V=\langle v\rangle\oplus\langle v\rangle^{\perp}
. However,

\mathrm{SU}(\langle v\rangle)\times\mathrm{SU}(\langle v\rangle^{\perp})\trianglelefteqslant B=G\cap(\Gamma\mathrm{U}(\langle v\rangle)\times\Gamma\mathrm{U}(\langle v\rangle^{\perp}))

by
[
KL
]
*Lemma 4.1.1(ii). Moreover, the projection

\pi\colon B\rightarrow\Gamma\mathrm{U}(\langle v\rangle^{\perp})
satisfies

\mathrm{SU}(\langle v\rangle^{\perp})\trianglelefteqslant\pi(B)=\Gamma\mathrm{U}(\langle v\rangle^{\perp}).

Note that
m>1
. If
\mathrm{SU}_{2m-1}(q)\leqslant\pi(H)
then since
\mathrm{SU}_{2m-1}(q)
is quasisimple (because
(m,q)\neq(2,2)
), it follows from Lemma
2.1
that
\mathrm{SU}_{2m-1}(q)\trianglelefteqslant H
as in the statement of Theorem
4.1
(c). Suppose then that
\mathrm{SU}_{2m-1}(q)\not\leqslant\pi(H)
.
As
B=B_{U}H
,
we have a factorisation
\Gamma\mathrm{U}_{2m-1}(q)=\pi(B)=\pi(B_{U})\pi(H)
. The totally isotropic subspace
W:=U\cap\langle v\rangle^{\perp}
is fixed by

B_{U}=G_{\langle v\rangle,U}
and so
\pi(B_{U})\leqslant P_{m-1}(\pi(B))
.
Thus factoring out by scalars we obtain a core-free factorisation of an almost simple group with socle
\mathrm{PSU}_{2m-1}(q)
and with one factor being
P_{m-1}
.
Hence Lemma
2.2
and
[
Factns
]
*p. 10, p. 13 imply that
(2m-1,q)
equals
(3,3),(3,5)
or
(3,8)
. Thus, since
(m,q)\neq(2,3)
, we have

(m,q)=(2,5)
or
(2,8)
. For
(m,q)=(2,5)
, a
Magma
computation shows that there are also transitive subgroups containing
H_{0}=3\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\mathrm{Alt}_{7}
as a normal subgroup (and contained in
N_{1}
), as listed in Table
4.3
. When
(m,q)=(2,8)
a similar computation
with
Magma
shows that there are two possibilities for
\overline{H}
, namely
\overline{H}=\textup{C}_{513}\rtimes\textup{C}_{9}
or
(\textup{C}_{513}\rtimes\textup{C}_{9})\rtimes\textup{C}_{2}
, where in both cases
\overline{H}\cap\mathrm{PSU}_{4}(8)=\textup{C}_{513}\rtimes\textup{C}_{3}
.
Thus
\textup{C}_{19}\lhd H
. This covers
all the cases in Table
4.3
.
∎

4.1.
Six dimensional orthogonal groups of minus type

As we observed in Subsection
3.1
,
\mathrm{P}\Omega_{6}^{-}(q)\cong\mathrm{PSU}_{4}(q)
, and it makes sense to treat these orthogonal groups here, now that we have completed the analysis for unitary groups. Recall Remark
1.2
(d) which points out a small discrepancy between one of the groups in Theorem
4.2
(c) and the statement of
[
Reg
]
*Lemma 4.4(iii).

Theorem 4.2
.

Suppose that
H\leqslant\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{6}^{-}(q)

\mathcal{U}

and

, or

where
(H_{0},m,q)
are listed in Table
4.5
; or

Proof.

Note that
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2}
,
\mathcal{N}_{2}^{\varepsilon}
(with
\varepsilon=\pm
), or
\mathcal{N}_{1}
(with
q
even), or
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{\varepsilon}
or
\mathcal{N}_{3}^{\varepsilon}
(with
