and
q
odd) (see Table
2.3
). Fix
U\in\mathcal{U}
and set
G=\textup{Stab}_{\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{6}^{-}(q)}(\mathcal{U})
. Then
G=\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{6}^{-}(q)
unless
q
is odd and
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{1}^{\varepsilon}
or
\mathcal{N}_{3}^{\varepsilon}
, in which case
G
is an index two subgroup of
\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{6}^{-}(q)
.

By
[
Taylor
]
*Corollary 12.35,
\Omega_{6}^{-}(q)\cong\mathrm{SU}_{4}(q)/\langle-I_{4}\rangle
. Then by comparing orders and the fact that
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathrm{PSU}_{4}(q))=\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{U}_{4}(q)
we deduce that

\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{6}^{-}(q)\cong\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{U}_{4}(q)
. For any
X\leqslant\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{6}^{-}(q)
we denote the image of
X
in
\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{U}_{4}(q)
by
\overline{X}
. Denote by
W
the natural module
(\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}})^{4}
for
\mathrm{SU}_{4}(q)
.

Case

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{1}
. Comparing composition factors we see that if
X
is the stabiliser in
\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{6}^{-}(q)
of a totally singular 1-subspace of
V
then
\overline{X}
is the stabiliser of a totally isotropic 2-subspace of
W
. Thus
\overline{H}
acts transitively on the set of totally isotropic 2-subspaces of
W
. Thus using Theorem
4.1
(c) and the fact that
\Omega_{6}^{-}(q)\cong\mathrm{SU}_{4}(q)/\langle\pm I_{4}\rangle
, implies that either
\mathrm{SU}_{3}(q)\trianglelefteqslant H
or
H_{0}\trianglelefteqslant H
where
H_{0}
is given in Table
4.5
, so part (a) holds. Note from the proof of Theorem
4.1
that, in the case where

H_{0}\cong\textup{C}_{19}
, the group
\overline{H}
satisfies
\overline{H}\cap\mathrm{P}\Omega_{6}^{-}(q)=\textup{C}_{513}\rtimes\textup{C}_{3}
.

Case

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{2}
. Again, by comparing composition factors we see that if
X
is the stabiliser of an element of
\mathcal{P}_{2}
then
\overline{X}
is the stabiliser of a totally isotropic 1-subspace of
W
. Thus
\overline{H}
is transitive on the set of totally isotropic 1-subspaces of
W
and so, by Theorem
4.1
(b) with
n=4
, we have that
q=3
and
\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(4)\trianglelefteqslant\overline{H}
. Then
[
BHRD
]
*Table 8.34 implies that
2\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(4)\trianglelefteqslant H
, and part (b) holds.

Case

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{1}^{\varepsilon}
(with
q
odd) or
\mathcal{N}_{1}
(with
q
even). Note that when
q
is odd, a similarity in
\mathrm{CO}_{6}^{-}(q)
that is not an isometry, interchanges
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{+}
and
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{-}
and so we may assume that
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{1}^{+}
in this case. Since
G_{U}
has
\mathrm{P}\Omega_{5}(q)\cong\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(q)
as a composition factor, we see from
[
BHRD
]
*Table 8.10 that
\overline{G_{U}}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{5}
-subgroup normalising
\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(q)
, and is maximal in
\overline{G}
. Let
\overline{B}
be a subgroup of
\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{U}_{4}(q))
that contains
\overline{H}
and is maximal subject to being core-free. Then by Lemma
2.2
and
[
Factns
]
we see that either
\overline{B}
is the stabiliser a nondegenerate 1-subspace
W_{1}
of
W
, or
q=3
and
\overline{B}=N_{\overline{G}}(\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(4))
. Suppose first that
\overline{B}
is the stabiliser in
\overline{G}
of
W_{1}
and let
W_{3}=W_{1}^{\perp}.
Then considering
\overline{B}^{W_{3}}
either
q=2
or we obtain a factorisation of an almost simple group with socle
\mathrm{PSU}_{3}(q)
. Thus by
[
Factns
]
, either
\mathrm{PSU}_{3}(q)\trianglelefteqslant\overline{H}
, or
q=2,3,5
or 8. In the first case, Lemma
2.1
implies that
\mathrm{SU}_{3}(q)\trianglelefteqslant H
. We deal with these small values of
q
, and also the case where
q=3
and
\overline{B}=N_{\overline{G}}(\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(4))
, with
Magma
. When
q=2
, there are five possibilities for
H
and all contain
3_{+}^{1+2}
as a normal subgroup. When
q=3
we see that either
\mathrm{SU}_{3}(3)
or
2\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(4)
is normal in
H
. For
q=5
, we see that
\mathrm{SU}_{3}(q)\trianglelefteqslant H
. Finally, for
q=8
note that
|\mathcal{N}_{1}|=32832
and
[
Factns
]
*Table 3 implies that either
\mathrm{SU}_{3}(8)\trianglelefteqslant H
or
\overline{H}\leqslant N_{\overline{G}}(\textup{C}_{19})
. However,
|N_{\overline{G}}(\textup{C}_{19})|=9234
, which eliminates the latter possibility. Thus part (c) holds.

Case

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}
. Here
G_{U}
has
\mathrm{P}\Omega_{4}^{-}(q)\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q^{2})
as a unique insoluble composition factor but
\overline{G_{U}}
is not a parabolic subgroup of
\overline{G}
. Thus comparing
[
BHRD
]
*Tables 8.10 and 8.33 we see that
\overline{G_{U}}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{2}
-subgroup of
\overline{G}\cong\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{U}_{4}(q)
preserving a decomposition of
W
into a pair of complementary totally isotropic 2-subspaces. Moreover,
\overline{G_{U}}
is maximal for all
q>2
. If
q>2
we let
\overline{B}
be a subgroup of
\overline{G}
that contains
\overline{H}
and is maximal subject to being core-free. Then Lemma
2.2
and
[
Factns
]
imply that
q=4
and
\overline{B}
is the stabiliser in
\overline{G}
of a nonsingular 1-subspace of
W
. A
Magma
calculation and application of Lemma
2.1
then reveals that
\mathrm{SU}_{3}(4)\trianglelefteqslant H
. For
q=2
, a
Magma
calculation shows that
3_{+}^{1+2}\lhd H
.
This verifies part (d)

Case

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{2}^{-}
. Here
G_{U}
has
\mathrm{P}\Omega_{4}^{+}(q)\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q)^{2}
as a section. Thus by comparing
[
BHRD
]
*Tables 8.10 and 8.33 we see that
\overline{G_{U}}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{2}
-subgroup of
\overline{G}\cong\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{U}_{4}(q)
preserving a decomposition of
W
into a pair of orthogonal nondegenerate 2-subspaces, and is maximal for
q\geqslant 3
. Thus by Lemma
2.2
, when
q\geqslant 3
we get a core-free maximal core-free factorisation of
\overline{G}
. However, by
[
Factns
]
no such factorisation exists. Thus
q=2
. A
Magma
calculation then shows that there are no examples with
\Omega_{6}^{-}(q)\not\leqslant H
.

Case

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{3}^{\varepsilon}
. Here
q
is odd and the two isometry classes are interchanged by a similarity. Hence
G
is an index two subgroup of
\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{6}^{-}(q)
and

G_{U}=G_{U^{\perp}}
preserves an orthogonal decomposition
V=U\oplus U^{\perp}
. Thus we may assume that
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{3}^{+}
in this case. Also
G_{U}
has
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q)^{2}
as a section. Comparing
[
BHRD
]
*Tables 8.10 and 8.33 we see that
\overline{G_{U}}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{5}
-subgroup of
\overline{G}\cong\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{U}_{4}(q)
of type
\mathrm{SO}_{4}^{+}(q)
, and is maximal in
G
for
q\geqslant 5
. By
[
Factns
]
, there are no such maximal core-free factorisations and so
q=3
. In this case, a
Magma
calculation implies that
\Omega_{6}^{-}(3)\trianglelefteqslant H
.
∎

5.
Symplectic groups

Let
V=(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{n}
be equipped with a nondegenerate alternating form and let
\mathcal{U}
be the set of all totally isotropic subspaces of
V
of dimension
k\leqslant n/2
, or the set of all nondegenerate subspaces of dimension
k\leqslant n/2
. Note that
n
is always even, and also that every 1-subspace of
V
is totally isotropic, so the case
n=2
was considered in Remark
3.2
. We assume henceforth that
n\geqslant 4
. By Witt’s Lemma,
\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(q)
is transitive on
\mathcal{U}
. In Theorem
5.2
we classify the subgroups
H
of
\Gamma\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(q)
that are transitive on
\mathcal{U}
. We use this result in Subsection
5.1
to deal with the five-dimensional orthogonal groups in odd characteristic.
We note that
\mathrm{PSp}_{n}(q)
is simple unless
(n,q)=(4,2)
, in which case
\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(2)\cong\mathrm{Sym}_{6}
.

Part (c) of Theorem
5.2
was considered in
[
Reg
]
*Lemma 4.2 while parts (a) and (b) for
m=2
were considered in
[
KLrank3
]
*Corollary 5.12. We begin with the following lemma, which enables us to eliminate some possibilities given by
[
Reg
]
*Lemma 4.2(i).

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
G=\mathrm{Sp}_{4m}(2)
with natural module
V=\mathbb{F}_{2}^{4m}
, where
m
is even, and either let
L=\mathrm{Sp}_{m}(16).4
preserving an extension field structure on
V
, or let
L=\mathrm{G}_{2}(16).4
if
m=6
. Then
L
is not transitive on the set
\mathcal{N}_{2}
of nondegenerate
2
-subspaces of
V
. In particular the possibilities
‘
\mathrm{Sp}_{m/2}(q^{4})
(
m/2
even,
q=2
)’
and
‘
\mathrm{G}_{2}(q^{4})
(
m=12
,
q=2
)’
in
[
Reg
]
*Lemma 4.2(ii) lead to no examples.

Proof.

Since in the case
m=6
the subgroup
\mathrm{G}_{2}(16).4
is contained in an extension field subgroup

\mathrm{Sp}_{6}(16).4
, it is sufficient to prove the lemma in the case
L=\mathrm{Sp}_{m}(16).4
.
If
m=2
then a
Magma
computation shows that no subgroup
\mathrm{Sp}_{2}(16).4
of
\mathrm{Sp}_{8}(2)
acts transitively on
\mathcal{N}_{2}
. Assume from now on that
m\geqslant 4
. Let
M=\mathrm{Sp}_{2m}(4).2
be the maximal extension field subgroup of
G
containing
L=\mathrm{Sp}_{m}(16).4
. Using the notation from
[
Factns
]
*p. 47, let
E_{1},\dots,E_{m},F_{1},\dots,F_{m}
be a standard basis for
V=\mathbb{F}_{4}^{2m}
(regarded as the natural module for
M
) relative to the alternating form preserved by
M
, let
Y=\langle E_{1},F_{1}\rangle_{\mathbb{F}_{4}}
, a nondegenerate
2
-subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{4}^{2m}
, and let
\lambda\in\mathbb{F}_{4}\setminus\mathbb{F}_{2}
with trace 1 relative to
\mathbb{F}_{2}
so that
X=\langle E_{1},\lambda F_{1}\rangle_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\in\mathcal{N}_{2}
, a nondegenerate
2
-subspace of
V
contained in
Y
. Note that
G_{X}=\mathrm{Sp}_{4m-2}(2)\times\mathrm{Sp}_{2}(2)
and
M_{Y}=(\mathrm{Sp}_{2m-2}(4)\times\mathrm{Sp}_{2}(4)).2
, and also that
M_{X}
leaves
Y
invariant so
M_{X}\leqslant M_{Y}
.

It is proved in
[
Factns
]
*pp. 47–48 that
G=MG_{X}
and
M=LM_{Y}
are maximal core-free factorisations, and that (importantly)

M_{X}=(M^{\prime})_{X}=\mathrm{Sp}_{2m-2}(4)\times\mathrm{Sp}_{2}(2)\quad\mbox{and}\quad L_{Y}=(L^{\prime})_{Y}=\mathrm{Sp}_{m-2}(16)\times\mathrm{Sp}_{2}(4),

where
M^{\prime},L^{\prime}
denote the derived subgroups. Now
L_{X}\leqslant M_{X}
(since
L\leqslant M
) which is contained in
M_{Y}
(as noted above). Thus
L_{X}\leqslant M_{Y}\cap L=L_{Y}
, and hence
L_{X}\leqslant M_{X}\cap L_{Y}=\mathrm{Sp}_{m-2}(16)\times\mathrm{Sp}_{2}(2)
. On the other hand,
the argument on
[
Factns
]
*p. 47 shows that
L_{X}
contains
\mathrm{Sp}_{m-2}(16)\times\mathrm{Sp}_{2}(2)
, so equality holds.
Thus
M_{X}
is a subgroup of
M_{Y}
of index
|M_{Y}:M_{X}|=2|\mathrm{Sp}_{2}(4):\mathrm{Sp}_{2}(2)|
, while

L_{X}
is a subgroup of
L_{Y}
of index
|L_{Y}:L_{X}|=|\mathrm{Sp}_{2}(4):\mathrm{Sp}_{2}(2)|=|M_{Y}:M_{X}|/2
. Since
G=MG_{X}
and
M=LM_{Y}
we have

|G:G_{X}|=|M:M_{X}|=|M:M_{Y}|.|M_{Y}:M_{X}|=|L:L_{Y}|.\left(2|L_{Y}:L_{X}|\right)=2|L:L_{X}|,

and hence
L
has two equal length orbits on
\mathcal{N}_{2}
.
∎

Theorem 5.2
.

Suppose that
H\leqslant\Gamma\mathrm{Sp}_{2m}(q)
, where

, such that
\mathrm{Sp}_{2m}(q)^{\prime}\not\leqslant H
. Let

and suppose that

of

, where

. Then one of

(a)

and
H_{0}\trianglelefteqslant H
where

is given in
Table

; or

.

; or

Proof.

Let
G=\Gamma\mathrm{Sp}_{2m}(q)
, so
H\leqslant G
is transitive on
\mathcal{U}
with
\mathrm{Sp}_{2m}(q)\not\leqslant H
. Let
U\in\mathcal{U}
. Then we have a factorisation
G=HG_{U}
.
By
[
King1981
]
and
[
King1981a
]
, either
\mathrm{Sp}_{2m}(q)
acts primitively on
\mathcal{U}
and so
G_{U}
is maximal in
G
, or

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{m}
.

Given any
X\leqslant G
we denote the image of
X
in
\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{Sp}_{2m}(q)
by
\overline{X}
. Then we have a factorisation
\overline{G}=\overline{H}\,\overline{G}_{U}
of the almost simple group
\overline{G}=\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{Sp}_{2m}(q)
. When
(m,q)\neq(2,2)
, the group
\mathrm{Sp}_{2m}(q)
is quasisimple and so Lemma
2.1
implies that
\mathrm{PSp}_{2m}(q)\not\leqslant\overline{H}
. When
(m,q)=(2,2)
there are no non-identity scalar matrices and so we again have
\mathrm{PSp}_{2m}(q)\not\leqslant\overline{H}
. Let
L=\mathrm{PSp}_{2m}(q)
, let
\overline{B}
be maximal among the core-free subgroups of
\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{Sp}_{2m}(q)
containing
\overline{H}
, and let
G^{*}=\overline{B}\,L
. Suppose first that
\mathcal{U}\neq\mathcal{N}_{m}
. Then by Lemma
2.2
,
\overline{B}
is maximal in
G^{*}
and we have
G^{*}=\overline{B}(G^{*})_{U}
, which is a maximal core-free factorisation. Thus
\mathcal{U}
and
\overline{B}
are given by
[
Factns
]
. Moreover,
\overline{B}=N_{\mathrm{PSp}_{2m}(q)}(\overline{B}\cap L)
and so letting
B
be the full preimage of
\overline{B}
in
\Gamma\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(q)
, the Correspondence Theorem implies that
B=N_{G}(B\cap\mathrm{Sp}_{2m}(q))
.
By
[
Reg
]
*Lemma 4.1(iii) we have that
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{1}
,
\mathcal{P}_{m}
or
\mathcal{N}_{2}
.
On the other hand, if
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{m}
with
m\geqslant 3
then
m
is even,
(G^{*})_{U}
has index
2
in the stabiliser
(G^{*})_{\{U,U^{\perp}\}}
of the decomposition
V=U\oplus U^{\perp}
. Then Lemma
2.2
implies that
G^{*}=\overline{B}(G^{*})_{\{U,U^{\perp}\}}
is a maximal core-free factorisation. Moreover since
\overline{B}
is transitive on
\mathcal{N}_{m}
,
\overline{B}_{U}
must have index
2
in
\overline{B}_{\{U,U^{\perp}\}}
. It follows from
[
Factns
]
*pp. 10, 12, 13 that
\overline{B}=N_{G^{*}}(\mathrm{SO}_{2m}^{-}(q))
with
q
even. However, by
[
Factns
]
*(3.2.4b), one of
U,U^{\perp}
has
+
type while the other has
-
type relative to the quadratic form for
\overline{B}
, and it follows that
\overline{B}_{\{U,U^{\perp}\}}=\overline{B}_{U}
, which is a contradiction. Thus the cases to be considered are
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{1}
,
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{m}
, and
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{2}
.

Case

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{2}
. This case was dealt with
in
[
Reg
]
*Lemma 4.2, even when
m=2
. The possibilities
\mathrm{Sp}_{m/2}(16)\trianglelefteqslant H
with
q=2
, so that
H\leqslant\mathrm{Sp}_{m/2}(16).4
, and also
G_{2}(16)\trianglelefteqslant H
with
(m,q)=(12,2)
, are listed in
[
Reg
]
*Lemma 4.2(i). However these groups are eliminated by Lemma
5.1
. The remaining groups are listed in Table
5.2
.

Case

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{1}
. Every 1-subspace of the symplectic space
V
is isotropic, so
here
\mathcal{U}
comprises all 1-subspaces and
|\mathcal{U}|=(q^{n}-1)/(q-1)
.
The subgroups of
\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{n}(q)
acting transitively on 1-subspaces are
listed in Theorem
3.1
(a). The subgroups
\mathrm{SL}_{2a}(q^{b})
(
m=ab
)
preserve an extension field structure, and so are not contained in
\mathrm{Sp}_{2m}(q)
(unless
a=1
in which case they are the groups
\mathrm{Sp}_{2}(q^{m})
as in Table
5.1
).
The subgroups
\mathrm{Sp}_{2a}(q^{b})
(
m=ab
), and
\mathrm{G}_{2}(q^{b})^{\prime}
(
m=3b
)
do lie in
\mathrm{Sp}_{2m}(q)
and are listed in Table
5.1
. We argue that
\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{1}(q^{n})\cap\Gamma\mathrm{Sp}_{n}(q)

is usually too small to act transitively on
\mathcal{P}_{1}
. It follows
from
[
Ber
]
*Table 1 that
\mathrm{GL}_{1}(q^{2m})\cap\mathrm{Sp}_{2m}(q)
is cyclic
of order
q^{m}+1
and hence
\mathrm{GL}_{1}(q^{2m})\cap\mathrm{CSp}_{2m}(q)
has order dividing
(q-1)(q^{m}+1)
.
We conclude that

|\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{1}(q^{2m})\cap\Gamma\mathrm{Sp}_{2m}(q)|
divides
2mf(q-1)(q^{m}+1)
. As

|\mathcal{P}_{1}|=(q^{2m}-1)/(q-1)
divides
|H|
, it also divides
2mf(q-1)(q^{m}+1)

and hence
(q^{m}-1)\mid 2mf(q-1)^{2}
. By Lemma
2.3
, the only possibilities are

m=2
and
q\in\{3,7\}
. A calculation with
Magma
shows that
\Gamma\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(7)
has no solvable
transitive subgroups and
\Gamma\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(3)
has
three, but none are metacyclic.
This eliminates the case

H\leqslant\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{1}(q^{2m})
in Theorem
3.1
(a).

It remains to consider the small
dimensional cases with
m\geqslant 2
in the second row of Table
3.1
.
Neither of the possibilities
\mathrm{Alt}_{7}
and
\mathrm{Alt}_{6}
for
(m,q)=(2,2)
arise since

H
does not contain
\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(2)^{\prime}\cong\mathrm{Alt}_{6}
. Next suppose
(m,q)=(2,3)
.
Then the first two possibilities listed satisfy

2_{-}^{1+4}\trianglelefteqslant H\leqslant 2_{-}^{1+4}\ldotp\mathrm{Sym}_{5}
and both arise, and are listed in Table
5.1
,
as
2_{-}^{1+4}\ldotp\mathrm{Sym}_{5}\leqslant\mathrm{CSp}_{4}(3)
(see
[
BHRD
]
*Table 8.12, p. 383).
Similarly
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(5)\leqslant\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(3)
, so we list

H_{0}=\mathrm{SL}_{2}(5)
in Table
5.1
.
Finally for
(m,q)=(3,3)
,
[
BHRD
]
*Table 8.29,  p. 392
or a
Magma
computation shows that
2\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(13)=\mathrm{SL}_{2}(13)

is a subgroup of
\mathrm{Sp}_{6}(3)
.
This justifies all the entries of Table
5.1
.

Case

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{m}
.
Suppose now that
H
is transitive on the set
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{m}
, so
|\mathcal{U}|=\prod_{i=1}^{m}(q^{i}+1)

by
[
BG
]
*pp. 150–151. Then by
[
Factns
]
there are two possibilities for the subgroup
B
:

(b1)

B=N_{G}(\mathrm{SO}_{2m}^{-}(q))
with
q
even by
[
Factns
]
*p. 10; or

(b2)

\overline{B}\cap L=2^{4}.\mathrm{Alt}_{5}
with
(m,q)=(2,3)
by
[
Factns
]
*p. 13.

(b1) Suppose that
B=N_{G}(\mathrm{SO}_{2m}^{-}(q))=\Gamma\mathrm{O}^{-}_{2m}(q)
with
q
even.
By
[
Factns
]
*p. 49,
\mathrm{SO}_{2m}^{-}(q)

is transitive on
\mathcal{P}_{m}
, and since
|\mathcal{P}_{m}|
is odd and

|\mathrm{SO}_{2m}^{-}(q):\Omega_{2m}^{-}(q)|=2
, it follows that

\Omega_{2m}^{-}(q)
is also transitive on
\mathcal{P}_{m}
.
Therefore any
H
satisfying
\Omega_{2m}^{-}(q)\trianglelefteqslant H
is necessarily
transitive on
\mathcal{P}_{m}
as in (b). Henceforth suppose that
\Omega_{2m}^{-}(q)\not\leqslant H
. Then we have a factorisation
B=HB_{U}
.
Since
\Omega_{2m}^{-}(q)
is irreducible on
V
, we get a core-free factorisation of the almost simple group
\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{2m}^{-}(q)
.

By
[
Factns
]
*3.2.4(a), p. 49,
B_{U}\leqslant B_{Y}
, where
Y
is an
(m-1)
-dimensional subspace of
U
which is totally singular with respect to the quadratic form preserved by
\mathrm{SO}^{-}_{2m}(q)
. Thus we have a core-free factorisation of
\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{2m}^{-}(q)
, where one of the factors is the stabiliser of a totally singular
(m-1)
-subspace.
No such factorisation exists for
m\geqslant 4
by
[
Factns
]
*p. 11–13, and if
m=3
then none exists with
q
even by Theorem
4.2
. Thus
m=2
. Since
q
is even
\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(q)
has an outer automorphism
\tau
that interchanges
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
. Hence
(\overline{H})^{\tau}
acts transitively on
\mathcal{P}_{1}
. Thus by part (a) we have
\mathrm{PSp}_{2}(q^{2})\trianglelefteqslant(\overline{H})^{\tau}
and so by
[
Asch
]
*Section 14 we have that
\Omega_{4}^{-}(q)\trianglelefteqslant H
.

(b2) Suppose
(m,q)=(2,3)
and
\overline{B}\cap L=2^{4}.\mathrm{Alt}_{5}
, with
G
acting on
\mathcal{P}_{2}
.
By
[
BHRD
]
*Table 8.12 we have
B\cap\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(3)=2_{-}^{1+4}.\mathrm{Alt}_{5}
. A computation with
Magma
shows that a transitive subgroup of
\mathrm{CSp}_{4}(3)
either contains
2_{-}^{1+4}.\mathrm{Alt}_{5}
or
\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(3)
as a normal subgroup, or is equal to
2_{-}^{1+4}.F_{20}
, as given in part (b) of the statement.
∎

5.1.
Five dimensional orthogonal groups with
q
odd

As we observed in Subsection
3.1
,
\mathrm{P}\Omega_{5}(q)\cong\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(q)
. We treat these orthogonal groups here if
q
is odd, using our analysis of the symplectic groups.
If
q
is even, the groups
\mathrm{P}\Omega_{5}(q)
are treated in Section
7.2
where we deal uniformly with the family
\mathrm{P}\Omega_{n}(q)
for even
q
and odd
n\geqslant 5
.

Theorem 5.3
.

Suppose that
H\leqslant\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{5}(q)
acts transitively on a set
\mathcal{U}
of subspaces of the natural module
V=(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{5}
given by Table
2.3
, where
q
is odd and
\Omega_{5}(q)\not\leqslant H
. Then

(a)

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
q=3
where either
2^{4}.\mathrm{Alt}_{5}\trianglelefteqslant H
, or
H=2^{4}.F_{20}
; or

(b)

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{2}
and either
\Omega_{4}^{-}(q)\trianglelefteqslant H
, or
q=3
and either

2^{4}\ldotp\textup{C}_{5}
,
2^{4}\ldotp\mathrm{Alt}_{5}
or
\mathrm{Alt}_{5}\trianglelefteqslant H
; or

(c)

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{1}^{-}
and
H\leqslant P_{2}
and
H
induces a subgroup of
\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{2}(q)
that is transitive on
1
-subspaces.

Proof.

By Table
2.3
,
\mathcal{U}
is one of
\mathcal{P}_{1}
,
\mathcal{P}_{2}
,
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{\pm}
or
\mathcal{N}_{2}^{\pm}
.
Fix
U\in\mathcal{U}
and set

G=\textup{Stab}_{\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{5}(q)}(\mathcal{U})
. By
[
Taylor
]
*12.31 Theorem,
\Omega_{5}(q)\cong\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(q)
. Also
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(q))=\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(q)
, and comparing orders we deduce that
\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{5}(q)\cong\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(q)
. For any
X\leqslant\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{5}(q)
we denote the image of
X
in
\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(q)
by
\overline{X}
. We denote by
W
the 4-dimensional vector space over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
acted on by
\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(q)
.

Case

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{1}
. By
[
Taylor
]
*p. 196, there is a one-to-one correspondence between
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and the set of totally isotropic 2-dimensional subspaces of
W
. Thus by Theorem
5.2
(b), and the facts that
q
is odd and
\Omega_{5}(3)\cong\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(3)
, we have
q=3
and either
2^{4}.\mathrm{Alt}_{5}\trianglelefteqslant H
or
2^{4}.F_{20}=H
, as in part (a).

Case

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{2}
. By
[
Taylor
]
*p. 196, there is a one-to-one correspondence between
\mathcal{P}_{2}
and the set of totally isotropic 1-dimensional subspaces of
W
. Then by Theorem
5.2
(a) and again using the fact that
\Omega_{5}(q)\cong\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(q)
, we see that either
\mathrm{PSp}_{2}(q^{2})\trianglelefteqslant H
, or
q=3
and
one of
2^{4}\ldotp\textup{C}_{5}\trianglelefteqslant H
,
2^{4}\ldotp\mathrm{Alt}_{5}\trianglelefteqslant H
or
\mathrm{Alt}_{5}\trianglelefteqslant H
. Now
\mathrm{PSp}_{2}(q^{2})\cong\Omega_{4}^{-}(q)
and comparing
[
BHRD
]
*Table 8.22 we see that in the first case
\Omega_{4}^{-}(q)\trianglelefteqslant H
. Thus part (b) holds.

Case

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{1}^{+}
. Noting that
\mathrm{P}\Omega_{4}^{+}(q)\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q)^{2}
and comparing composition factors we see from Table
[
BHRD
]
*Table 8.12 that
\overline{G_{U}}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{2}
-subgroup
of
\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(q)
, and is maximal for all
q
. Thus by Lemma
2.2
we get a maximal core-free factorisation of a group with socle
\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(q)
, and by
[
Factns
]
there are no such factorisations.

Case

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{1}^{-}
. Since
\mathrm{P}\Omega_{4}^{-}(q)\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q^{2})
and comparing composition factors we see from Table
[
BHRD
]
*Table 8.12 that
\overline{G_{U}}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{3}
-subgroup of
\overline{G}
and is maximal for all
q
. It follows from Lemma
2.2
and
[
Factns
]
that
\overline{H}
stabilises a totally isotropic 1-subspace of
W
and so
H
stabilises a totally singular 2-subspace
X
of
V
, that is
H\leqslant P_{2}
. Let
B=G_{X}
so that
H\leqslant B
. Then arguing as in
[
Factns
]
*p.5̇7 we see that
B_{U}
fixes a 1-dimensional subspace of
X
. Since we have a factorisation
B=B_{U}H
it follows that
H
acts transitively on the set of 1-dimensional subspaces of
X
, and part (c) holds.

Case

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{2}^{\varepsilon}
. Again, comparing composition factors and consulting
[
BHRD
]
*Table 8.12 we see that
\overline{G_{U}}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{2}
-subgroup stabilising a decomposition into two totally isotropic 2-subspaces or a
\mathcal{C}_{3}
-subgroup of type
\mathrm{GU}_{2}(q)
. Moreover, they are only maximal for
q\geqslant 5
. By Lemma
2.2
and
[
Factns
]
there are no such maximal factorisations and so
q=3
. However, in this case a
Magma
calculation finds no examples.
∎

6.
Orthogonal groups in even dimension of minus type

Let
V
be a
2m
-dimensional vector space over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
equipped with a nondegenerate quadratic form of minus type. By
[
BG
]
*Lemma 2.5.10,
\Omega_{2m}^{-}(q)
acts transitively on each set
\mathcal{U}
given in Table
2.3
, and we identify transitive subgroups in these actions in Theorem
6.2
. If
kq
is odd then there are two isometry types of nondegenerate
k
-subspaces but only one similarity class. Thus the two orbits
\mathcal{N}_{k}^{\pm}
of
\Omega_{2m}^{-}(q)
are fused in
\mathrm{CO}_{2m}^{-}(q)
, and we find the subgroups transitive on
\mathcal{N}_{k}=\mathcal{N}_{k}^{+}\cup\mathcal{N}_{k}^{-}
in Proposition
6.3
.

First we need the following lemma.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
m\geqslant 4
. If
m
is odd let
A=\mathrm{SU}_{m}(2)\leqslant\Omega^{-}_{2m}(2)
and if
m
is even let
A=\mathrm{SU}_{m}(2)\leqslant\Omega^{+}_{2m}(2)
. Then
A
is transitive on
\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}
.

Proof.

Following
[
Factns
]
*p.60–61, let
F=\mathbb{F}_{2}
,
K=\mathbb{F}_{4}
and view
V=F^{2m}=K^{m}
as an
A
-module with
A=\mathrm{SU}_{m}(2)
. Let
[\ ,\,]
be the
A
-invariant
K
-Hermitian form
on
V
and define
Q:V\rightarrow F
by
Q(v)=[v,v]
for all
v\in V
. Then
Q
is a quadratic form of
+
type when
m
is even and of
-
type when
m
is odd (see for example
[
BG
]
*Construction 2.5.14). Since
A
preserves
Q
this gives us an embedding
A\leqslant\Omega_{2m}^{+}(2)
when
m
is even and an embedding
A\leqslant\Omega_{2m}^{-}(2)
when
m
is odd. Let
\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}
be the set of all
2
-dimensional subspaces
W
of
V
over
F
such that the restriction of
Q
to
W
is nondegenerate of
+
type.

Choose
a,b\in V
such that
[a,a]=0=[b,b]
and
[a,b]=1
, and let
\lambda\in K
such that
\lambda+\lambda^{2}=1
. Then if
(\ ,\ )
is the symmetric bilinear form associated with
Q
we have that
(\lambda a,b)=Q(\lambda a+b)+Q(\lambda a)+Q(b)=\lambda+\lambda^{2}=1
. Hence
U:=\langle\lambda a,b\rangle_{F}\in\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}
. Now
A_{U}
fixes
\langle U\rangle_{K}=\langle a,b\rangle_{K}
, which is a nondegenerate
K
-subspace of dimension 2. By
[
KL
]
*Lemma 4.1.1,
A_{\langle U\rangle_{K}}=(\mathrm{SU}_{m-2}(2)\times\mathrm{SU}_{2}(2)).(q+1)
where the
(q+1)
acts diagonally on each factor so that
A_{\langle U\rangle_{K}}
induces
\mathrm{GU}_{2}(2)
on
\langle U\rangle_{K}
and
\mathrm{GU}_{m-2}(2)
on
(\langle U\rangle_{K})^{\perp}
. Since
[\lambda a,b]=\lambda
while
[b,\lambda a]=\lambda^{2}\neq\lambda
, there is no
g\in\mathrm{GU}_{2}(2)
interchanging
\lambda a
and
b
. As these are the only singular vectors in
U
, it follows that
\mathrm{GU}_{2}(2)_{U}=1
and hence
A_{U}=\mathrm{SU}_{m-2}(2)
. Thus
|A:A_{U}|=2^{2m-3}(2^{m}+1)(2^{m-1}-1)=|\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}|
and so
\mathrm{SU}_{m}(2)
is transitive on
\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}
.
∎

Proof.

Let

\mathcal{U}
such that

. If

, while if

. Thus from now on we assume that

. Note that
G=\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{2m}^{-}(q)
unless

is the set of nondegenerate

-subspaces of a given isometry type and

. Let

. Then we have a factorisation

. By

[

,
King1981a
,
King1982
]

(or see

*Theorems 7.0.1 and 8.1.1 and

*Theorem 2.1.1),
\mathrm{GO}_{2m}^{-}(q)

unless

and
q=2
or

. Further, if
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}
and

, then it follows from
[

]
*Table 8.52 (for

),
[
BHRD
]
*Proposition 2.3.2 (for

), and
[

*Main Theorem (c) and Table 3.5F (for

), that

is primitive on
\mathcal{U}
.
We will treat the exceptional case where
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}
and
q=2
separately at the end of the proof, and so for now we assume that
G_{U}
is maximal in
G
. Then by
[
Reg
]
*Lemma 4.1(v),
\mathcal{U}
is
\mathcal{P}_{1}
, or
\mathcal{N}_{1}
(
q
even), or
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{\pm}
(

odd),
or
\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}
.
The possibilities when
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{1}
(
q
even) or
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{\pm}
(

odd) are determined in
[
Reg
]
*Lemma 4.4. We list these in part (b) (especially Table

) for completeness.

So assume now that
\mathcal{U}
is
\mathcal{P}_{1}
, or
\mathcal{U}
is
\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}
(
q>2
). Denote the image of any subgroup
X
of
\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{2m}^{-}(q)
in
\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{2m}^{-}(q)
by
\overline{X}
and let
L=\mathrm{P}\Omega_{2m}^{-}(q)
. Then we have a factorisation
\overline{G}=\overline{H}\,\overline{G}_{U}
. Since
\Omega_{2m}^{-}(q)
is quasisimple,
L\not\leqslant\overline{H}
. Let
\overline{B}
be maximal among core-free subgroups of
\overline{G}
containing
\overline{H}
and let
G^{*}=\overline{B}L
. Then by Lemma
2.2
,
\overline{B}
is maximal in
G^{*}
and we have a maximal core-free factorisation
G^{*}=\overline{B}(G^{*})_{U}
. In particular, for each
\mathcal{U}
, the possibilities for
\overline{B}

are given by
[
Factns
]
and are collated in Table
6.3
. Moreover,
\overline{B}=N_{\overline{G}}(\overline{B}\cap L)
and so letting
B
be the full preimage of
\overline{B}
in
\Gamma\mathrm{O}^{-}_{n}(q)
, the Correspondence Theorem implies that

B=N_{G}(B\cap\Omega_{2m}^{-}(q))
. We consider separately the three cases in Table

.

Table 6.3.

where

.

Case

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{1}

with
m
odd and
B=N_{G}(\mathrm{SU}_{m}(q))
. Set

A=\mathrm{SU}_{m}(q)
,
F=\mathbb{F}_{q}
,

, view
V=F^{2m}=K^{m}
as
an
A
-module, and let
[\ ,\,]
be the
A
-invariant
K
-Hermitian form
such that
Q(v)=[v,v]
for all
v\in V
, where
Q
is the quadratic form corresponding to
G
. Let
U\in\mathcal{U}
and let

U=\langle u\rangle_{F}
, the 1-dimensional

-subspace containing
u
. Then
Q(u)=0
since
U
is singular. Let
\langle u\rangle_{K}
be the
1-dimensional

-subspace containing

. Since

, the subspace
\langle u\rangle_{K}

[

. Since

, and hence acts transitively on the set of

-subspaces of

. Thus

acts transitively on
\mathcal{P}_{1}

and we list the entry
\mathrm{SU}_{m}(q)\trianglelefteqslant H
in Table
6.1
.

Suppose now that
\mathrm{SU}_{m}(q)\not\leqslant H
. Then we have a factorisation
B=HB_{U}
. Note that
B_{U}
is contained in the stabiliser in
B
of a totally isotropic subspace of dimension 1 over

and so we also have a factorisation
B=HP_{1}(B)
. Since

we obtain a core-free factorisation of an almost simple group with socle
\mathrm{PSU}_{m}(q)
. Since

is odd, it follows from Lemma
2.2
and

]
that

and

. By
[

]
,

is transitive on the 1-dimensional isotropic

-subspaces of

, and since it contains all

-scalars, it is transitive on the set
\mathcal{P}_{1}
of singular 1-dimensional

-subspaces of

. This leads to the example

in Table

. Since

has no factorisations by
[

]
, there are no further examples.

Case

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{1}

with
m=5
,
q=2
and
B=N_{G}(\mathrm{Alt}_{12})=\mathrm{Sym}_{12}
. We view
V=\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\,10}
as a section

of a 12-dimensional

\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\,12}
following
[
Factns
]
*(5.2.16), p. 115, where

S=\{x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\,12}\mid\sum_{i=1}^{12}x_{i}=0\}

and
T=\langle(1,\dots,1)\rangle
.
Clearly,
\mathrm{Sym}_{12}
preserves the quadratic form

Q(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cr}1&\mbox{if}\ |\{i\mid x_{i}=1\}|\equiv 2\pmod{4}\\
0&\mbox{if}\ |\{i\mid x_{i}=0\}|\equiv 0\pmod{4}\end{array}\right.

on

with associated symmetric bilinear form
(x,y)=\sum_{i=1}^{12}x_{i}y_{i}
. Moreover,

.
Thus the action of
\mathrm{Sym}_{12}
on

gives an embedding of
\mathrm{Sym}_{12}
in

(see
[
Factns
]
*p. 34). Now
u=(1,1,1,1,0,\dots,0)
(8 zeroes) lies in

and
Q(u)=0
. We may take

U=\langle u+T\rangle\in\mathcal{P}_{1}
. Now
[
Factns
]
implies that
\mathrm{Alt}_{12}
is transitive on
\mathcal{P}_{1}
, and the possibility
\mathrm{Alt}_{12}\trianglelefteqslant H
is
listed in Table
6.1
. It remains to consider the case where
\mathrm{Alt}_{12}\not\leqslant H
. In this case we have a factorisation
B=HB_{U}
. Now
B_{U}=B\cap(\mathrm{Sym}_{4}\times\mathrm{Sym}_{8})
and so
H
is a 4-homogeneous subgroup of
\mathrm{Sym}_{12}
. Thus
[
Kantor
]
implies that
H
is 4-transitive and so
H=\textup{M}_{12}
. Indeed
B=M_{12}B_{U}
, so
G=M_{12}P_{1}
, and the example
M_{12}
is listed in Table
6.1
, concluding this case.

Case

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}

with
m
odd,
q=4
, and
B=N_{G}(\mathrm{SU}_{m}(q))
.  Here
G=\Gamma\mathrm{O}^{-}_{m}(4)
and so
B=\Gamma\mathrm{U}_{m}(4)
. Suppose first that
\mathrm{SU}_{m}(4)\not\leqslant H
and let
U\in\mathcal{U}
. Then we have a core-free factorisation
B=HB_{U}
. However, since
m
is odd and
q=4
there are no such factorisations by Lemma
2.2
and
[
Factns
]
, a contradiction. Thus
\mathrm{SU}_{m}(4)\trianglelefteqslant H
as stated in Theorem
6.2
(c). Moreover, by the Remark column of
[
Factns
]
*p. 11 we have
\overline{H}=N_{\overline{G}}(\mathrm{SU}_{m}(4))
and so
H
and
\Gamma\mathrm{U}_{m}(4)
project onto the same subgroup of
\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{2m}^{-}(4)
, proving the assertions of part (c) in this case.

Case

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}

with
q=2
. When
q=2
, if
U\in\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}
then
U
contains a unique nonsingular 1-subspace. Thus if
H\leqslant\mathrm{GO}_{2m}^{-}(2)
acts transitively on
\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}
then it is also transitive on
\mathcal{N}_{1}
. Hence
H
is given in part (b) of the theorem. By
[
BG
]
*Table 4.1.2 and the fact that
|\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}|=|\mathcal{N}_{2m-2}^{-}|
we have that
|\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}|=2^{2m-3}(2^{m}+1)(2^{m-1}-1)
. Hence, unless
m=7
, it follows that
|H|
is divisible by a primitive prime divisor of
2^{m-1}-1
. Looking at the five possibilities for
H_{0}
and using the fact that
m\geqslant 4
, it follows that
m
is odd and
\mathrm{SU}_{m}(2)\trianglelefteqslant H
. By Lemma
6.1
this is indeed an example as listed in part (c) of the Theorem.
∎

As mentioned in Subsection
2.3
, when
kq
is odd we also determine the subgroups which are transitive on
\mathcal{N}_{k}=\mathcal{N}_{k}^{+}\cup\mathcal{N}_{k}^{-}
.

Proposition 6.3
.

Suppose that
H\leqslant\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{2m}^{-}(q)
, where
m\geqslant 2
and
q
is odd, such that
\Omega_{2m}^{-}(q)\not\leqslant H
. Let
V=(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{2m}
and suppose that
H
acts transitively on the set of all nondegenerate
k
-subspaces of
V
for some odd
k
. Then
k=1
,
m
is odd, and either
\mathrm{SU}_{m}(q)\lhd H
, or
(m,q)=(3,3)
and
2\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(4)\lhd H
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{k}=\mathcal{N}_{k}^{+}\cup\mathcal{N}_{k}^{-}
, the set of all nondegenerate
k
-subspaces of
V
.
Note that
\Gamma\mathrm{O}^{-}_{2m}(q)
has an index two subgroup with orbits
\mathcal{N}_{k}^{+}
and
\mathcal{N}_{k}^{-}
on
\mathcal{U}
. Thus
H
has an index two subgroup
H^{+}
which is transitive on both
\mathcal{N}_{k}^{+}
and
\mathcal{N}_{k}^{-}
. Hence by Theorem
6.2
,
k=1
, and either
m
is odd with
H
is as in the statement of the proposition, or
(m,q)=(2,3)
and
\mathrm{Alt}_{5}\lhd H^{+}
. However, as noted in the proof of Theorem
3.4
, each of the latter groups (containing an
\mathrm{Alt}_{5}
-subgroup) acts transitively on only one of the
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{\varepsilon}
and so does not provide an example. The other possibilities do indeed provide examples: by
[
KL
]
*Proposition 4.3.18,
\Omega_{2m}^{-}(q)
contains only one conjugacy class of
\mathrm{SU}_{m}(q)
-subgroups and so each such subgroup acts transitively on each orbit
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{\varepsilon}
. Moreover, since there is only one conjugacy class, the normaliser in
\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{2m}^{-}(q)
of
\mathrm{SU}_{m}(q)
contains an element of
\mathrm{CO}_{2m}^{-}(q)\backslash\mathrm{GO}^{-}_{2m}(q)
and so
N_{\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{2m}^{-}(q)}(H^{+})
is transitive on
\mathcal{U}
. Similarly, when
(m,q)=(3,3)
we see from
[
BHRD
]
*Table 8.34 that
H^{+}=2\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(4)
is normalised by an element of
\mathrm{CO}^{-}_{6}(3)\backslash\mathrm{GO}^{-}_{6}(3)
and so
N_{\mathrm{CO}^{-}_{6}(3)}(2\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(4))
acts transitively on
\mathcal{U}
.
∎

7.
Orthogonal groups in odd dimension

Let
V=(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{n}
, with
n
odd, equipped with a nondegenerate quadratic form
Q
.
The form
Q
and the associated polar form
(\cdot,\cdot)
may be chosen as follows. Relative to a basis

e_{1},\dots,e_{m},f_{1},\dots,f_{m},d
for
V
we
define

Q\left(zd+\sum_{i=1}^{m}(x_{i}e_{i}+y_{i}f_{i})\right)=z^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}x_{i}y_{i}\quad\textup{and}\quad(u,v)=Q(u+v)-Q(u)-Q(v).

Note that
Q(d)=1
and
(d,d)=2
. If
q
is even then
V^{\perp}=\langle d\rangle
, a nonsingular
1
-subspace, while if

q
is odd then the Gram matrix for
(\cdot,\cdot)
is invertible (as
(d,d)=2\neq 0
) and the radicals of both
Q
and
(\cdot,\cdot)
are trivial. Thus the cases where
q
is odd and even are quite different and are treated in separate Subsections
7.1
and
7.2
.

7.1.
Orthogonal groups in odd dimension
n=2m+1\geqslant 3
and
q
odd

Let
\mathcal{U}
be the set of all totally singular subspaces of
V
of dimension
k\leqslant m
, or the set of all nondegenerate subspaces of dimension
k\leqslant m
of a fixed isometry type. Then by
[
BG
]
*Lemma 2.5.10,
\Omega_{n}(q)
is transitive on
\mathcal{U}
. Note that if
k
is odd then there are two isometry types of nondegenerate subspaces of dimension
k
but only one similarity type. However, their orthogonal complements have even dimension and are not similar (for some isometry type they are of plus type and for the other isometry type they are of minus type.) Hence
\mathcal{U}
is also a
\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{n}(q)
-orbit.

The following formulas for
|\mathcal{U}|
are useful references.

(3)

|\mathcal{P}_{m}|=\prod_{i=1}^{m}(q^{i}+1),\qquad\textup{and}\qquad|\mathcal{N}_{1}^{\varepsilon}|=\frac{q^{m}(q^{m}+\varepsilon 1)}{2}.

Proof.

Let
G=\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{2m+1}(q)
. By Table
2.3
,
\mathcal{U}
is
\mathcal{P}_{k}
or
\mathcal{N}_{k}^{\varepsilon}
for some

and
\varepsilon=\pm
, and by our discussion above,
G
leaves
\mathcal{U}
invariant. Let
H\leqslant G
act transitively on
\mathcal{U}
such that
\Omega_{2m+1}(q)\not\leqslant H
. If
m=1
then the result follows from Theorem
3.3
, while if
m=2
then it follows from Theorem
5.3
. Thus from now on we assume that
m\geqslant 3
.
Let
U\in\mathcal{U}
. Then we have a factorisation
G=HG_{U}
. Since
m\geqslant 3
and
q
is odd,
[
King1981
]
and
[
King1981a
]
imply that
\mathrm{GO}_{n}(q)
acts primitively on
\mathcal{U}
unless

and
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}
. We will treat the latter case separately at the end. Thus we assume for now that
G
acts primitively on
\mathcal{U}
and so
G_{U}
is maximal in
G
.

We denote the image of each
X\leqslant\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{2m+1}(q)
in
\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{2m+1}(q)
by
\overline{X}
, and let
L=\mathrm{P}\Omega_{2m+1}(q)
. Then we have a factorisation
\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{2m+1}(q)=\overline{H}\,\overline{G}_{U}
. Since
\Omega_{2m+1}(q)
is quasisimple, Lemma
2.1
implies that
L\not\leqslant\overline{H}
. Let
\overline{B}
be maximal among core-free subgroups of
\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{2m+1}(q)
containing
\overline{H}
and let
G^{*}=\overline{B}L
. Then by Lemma
2.2
,
\overline{B}
is maximal in
G^{*}
and we have a core-free maximal factorisation
G^{*}=\overline{B}(G^{*})_{U}
. In particular
\mathcal{U}
and
\overline{B}
are given by
[
Factns
]
. Moreover,
\overline{B}=N_{\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{2m+1}(q)}(\overline{B}\cap L)
and so letting
B
be the full preimage of
\overline{B}
in
\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{n}(q)
, the Correspondence Theorem implies that
B=N_{G}(B\cap\Omega_{2m+1}(q))
.

Besides a large number of factorisations with
m=q=3
listed
on
[
Factns
]
*p. 13, we must consider the cases
in Table
7.4
listed on
[

]
*p. 11, p. 12. We note the case
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{1}
when

was missed in
[
Reg
]
*Lemma 4.1.

Case

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{1}
. By
[
Factns
]
, the only possibility is
m=3
and
B=N_{G}(\mathrm{G}_{2}(q))
. Moreover,
\mathrm{G}_{2}(q)
is indeed transitive on
\mathcal{P}_{1}
, as listed in case (a) of the Theorem. Suppose now that
\mathrm{G}_{2}(q)\not\leqslant H
. Then we have a factorisation
B=HB_{U}
, where
U\in\mathcal{P}_{1}
. As observed in
[
Factns
]
*p100,
B_{U}
is a parabolic subgroup of
B
, and there are no such factorisations in the list for almost simple groups with socle
\mathrm{G}_{2}(q)
given in

*Table 5.

Case

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{m}
. By
[
Factns
]
, either
B=N_{1}^{-}
or
(m,q)=(3,3)
. We may take

U=\langle e_{1},\dots,e_{m}\rangle\in\mathcal{P}_{m}
. Suppose first that
H\leqslant N_{1}^{-}=N_{G}(\Omega_{2m}^{-}(q))
. We follow
[
Factns
]
*3.4.1, p. 57 and set
v=e_{1}+\lambda f_{1}
where

\lambda\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}
is a nonsquare and
B=G_{\langle v\rangle}
. Suppose first that
\Omega_{2m}^{-}(q)\not\leqslant H
. Then
N_{1}^{-}=B=HB_{U}
. This factorisation yields the factorisation
B^{v^{\perp}}=H^{v^{\perp}}(B_{U})^{v^{\perp}}
which then yields a factorisation of an almost simple group
X
with socle
\mathrm{P}\Omega_{2m}^{-}(q)
. Moreover, as
B_{U}
fixes the totally singular
(m-1)
-subspace
U\cap v^{\perp}=\langle e_{2},\ldots,e_{m}\rangle
, we obtain a core-free factorisation of
X
with one factor being the stabiliser of a totally singular
(m-1)
-subspace. However, such a factorisation does not exist if
m\geqslant 4

by
[
Factns
]
*pp. 11–13 and Theorem
4.2
implies that when
m=3
we must have
q=3
. We note that for
(m,q)=(3,3)
, other possibilities for
B
also occur and so at this point it is convenient to
use
Magma
to list the subgroups of
\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{7}(3)=\mathrm{GO}_{7}(3)
that act transitively
on
\mathcal{P}_{3}
. The new possibilities are recorded in Table
7.1
.

We conclude this case by showing that
A=\Omega_{2m}^{-}(q)=\Omega_{2m+1}(q)_{v}
acts transitively on
\mathcal{P}_{m}
and so any
H
with
\Omega_{2m}^{-}(q)\trianglelefteqslant H
acts transitively, as recorded in Table
7.1
. Now by
[
BG
]
*Lemma 2.5.10,
A
acts transitively on the set of all totally singular
(m-1)
-subspaces of
v^{\perp}
and so it remains to show that
A_{U\cap v^{\perp}}
acts transitively on the set of elements of
\mathcal{P}_{m}
that contain
U\cap v^{\perp}
. Now
(U\cap v^{\perp})^{\perp}=\langle e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{m},f_{1},d\rangle
and note that
\langle e_{1},f_{1},d\rangle\perp\langle e_{2},\ldots,e_{m}\rangle
. Thus an element
a+b
with
a\in\langle e_{1},f_{1},d\rangle
and
b\in\langle e_{2},\ldots,e_{m}\rangle
is singular if and only if
a
is singular. Hence the elements of
\mathcal{P}_{m}
containing
U\cap v^{\perp}
correspond to the totally singular 1-subspaces of
\langle e_{1},f_{1},d\rangle
. Since
v=e_{1}+\lambda f_{1}\in\mathcal{N}_{1}^{-}
it follows that
W:=\langle e_{1}-\lambda f_{1},d\rangle\leqslant v^{\perp}
is a nondegenerate 2-subspace of minus type. Moreover,
[
KL
]
*Lemmas 4.1.1 and 4.1.12 imply that
A_{U\cap v^{\perp},W}
induces at least
\mathrm{SO}_{2}^{-}(q)
on
W
and hence for each
\mu\neq 0
, is transitive on all vectors
w\in W
with
Q(w)=\mu
. Since
W
is of minus type, any singular 1-subspace of
v\perp W
is spanned by a vector of the form
v+a(e_{1}-\lambda f_{1})+bd
for some
a,b\in\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Such a vector is singular if and only if
b^{2}=\lambda(a^{2}-1)
. However, in this case
Q(a(e_{1}-\lambda f_{1})+bd)=-\lambda a^{2}+b^{2}=-\lambda
, a constant. Hence
A_{U\cap v^{\perp},W}
is transitive on all such vectors and thus all singular 1-subspaces in
\langle v,W\rangle
. Hence
A
is transitive on
\mathcal{P}_{m}
.

Case

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{1}^{-}
. Here there are four possibilities for
B
from Table
7.4
, namely (c1)
P_{m}
, (c2)
N_{G}(\mathrm{G}_{2}(q))
with
m=3
, (c3)
N_{G}(\mathrm{PSp}_{6}(q))
with
m=6
, and
(c4)
N_{G}(\textup{F}_{4}(q))
with
m=12
. Moreover, cases (c3) and (c4) only occur when
q=3^{f}
.
Let
U\in\mathcal{N}_{1}^{-}
where
\dim(U)=1
,
\dim(U^{\perp})=2m

and the quadratic form restricted to
U^{\perp}
has type
\mathrm{O}^{-}
.

(c1) Suppose first that
H\leqslant P_{m}
. As seen above, we have that
N_{1}^{-}\cap P_{m}
fixes an
(m-1)
-dimensional subspace of the totally singular
m
-subspace
W
fixed by
P_{m}
.
Restricting to
W
gives an epimorphism

\pi\colon P_{m}\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{m}(q)
. This gives a factorisation
\pi(P_{m})=\pi(H)\pi(P_{m}\cap N_{1}^{-})
. Since
P_{m}\cap N_{1}^{-}
fixes a hyperplane of
W
it follows that
\pi(H)
is a subgroup of
\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{m}(q)
that is transitive on hyperplanes as in case (c).

(c2) Suppose now that
H\leqslant N_{G}(\mathrm{G}_{2}(q))=B
where
m=3
. Then
B=HB_{U}
. By
[
Factns
]
*p. 100,
\mathrm{G}_{2}(q)_{U}=\mathrm{SU}_{3}(q).2
, and so by the classification of factorisations of almost simple groups with socle
\mathrm{G}_{2}(q)
, see
[
Factns
]
*Table 5, we deduce that either
\mathrm{G}_{2}(q)\trianglelefteqslant H
or
q=3^{f}
and
\mathrm{SL}_{3}(q)\trianglelefteqslant H
as listed in Table
7.2
.

(c3) Suppose next that
H\leqslant N_{G}(\mathrm{PSp}_{6}(q))=B
where
m=6
and
q=3^{f}
. Note that
[
Factns
]
*Lemma A on p. 85 implies that
\mathrm{PSp}_{6}(q)
acts transitively on
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{-}
and so any group
H
with
\mathrm{PSp}_{6}(q)\trianglelefteqslant H
is an example as in Table
7.2
. Suppose then that
\mathrm{PSp}_{6}(q)\not\leqslant H
. Then we have a factorisation
B=HB_{U}
and hence a factorisation of an almost simple group with socle
\mathrm{PSp}_{6}(q)
. By
[
Factns
]
*p. 86 we have that
\mathrm{PSp}_{6}(q)_{U}=(\mathrm{Sp}_{2}(q)\times\mathrm{Sp}_{2}(q^{2}))/\{\pm I\}
. Moreover,
\mathrm{PSp}_{6}(q)_{U}
is contained in an
N_{2}
subgroup of
\mathrm{PSp}_{6}(q)
and hence
B=HN_{2}
. However, by
[
Factns
]
when
q=3^{f}
an almost simple group with socle
\mathrm{PSp}_{6}(3^{f})
does not have a core-free factorisation with one factor being an
N_{2}
subgroup, contradicting
\mathrm{PSp}_{6}(q)\not\leqslant H
.

(c4) Finally, suppose that
H\leqslant N_{G}(\textup{F}_{4}(q))
where
m=12
and
q=3^{f}
. If
\textup{F}_{4}(q)\not\leqslant H
then we obtain a core-free factorisation of an almost simple group with socle
\textup{F}_{4}(q)
. However, by
[
Factns
]
*Table 5 there are no such factorisations when
q
is odd. Thus we must have
\textup{F}_{4}(q)\trianglelefteqslant H
as in Table
7.2
.

Case

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{1}^{+}
. By
[
Factns
]
, either
m=3
and
H\leqslant N_{G}(\mathrm{G}_{2}(q))
or
(m,q)=(3,3)
. Moreover,
\mathrm{G}_{2}(q)
acts transitively on
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{+}
giving us one of the cases in Table
7.3
. Let
U\in\mathcal{N}_{1}^{+}
. Suppose that
H\leqslant N_{G}(\mathrm{G}_{2}(q))=B
such that
\mathrm{G}_{2}(q)\not\leqslant H
. Then we have a factorisation
B=HB_{U}
. By
[
Factns
]
*p. 100 we have
\mathrm{G}_{2}(q)_{U}=\mathrm{SL}_{3}(q).2
and so by the classification of factorisations of almost simple groups with socle
\mathrm{G}_{2}(q)

[
Factns
]
*Table 5 we deduce that
q=3^{f}
and either
\mathrm{SU}_{3}(q)\trianglelefteqslant H
or
f
is odd and
{}^{2}\mathrm{G}_{2}(q)\trianglelefteqslant H
. These cases are listed in Table
7.3
. It remains to consider the exceptional factorisations when
(m,q)=(3,3)
. In this case
\Gamma\mathrm{O}(7,3)=\mathrm{GO}(7,3)
and a
Magma
calculation gives the remaining cases in Table
7.3
. Note that
{}^{2}\mathrm{G}_{2}(3)\cong\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{2}(8)
.

Case

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{2}^{\varepsilon}
with
(\varepsilon,q)\neq(+,3)
. By
[
Factns
]
, we again have that
m=3
and
B=N_{G}(\mathrm{G}_{2}(q))
. Moreover, by
[
Factns
]
*Lemma A p. 100,
\mathrm{G}_{2}(q)
is transitive on
\mathcal{N}_{2}^{\varepsilon}
for all
q
(including the case
(\varepsilon,q)=(+,3)
where
N_{2}^{+}
is not maximal in
G
). Suppose that
\mathrm{G}_{2}(q)\not\leqslant H
. Then we have a factorisation
B=HB_{U}
, where
U\in\mathcal{N}_{2}^{\varepsilon}.
By
[
Factns
]
*p. 101,
|\mathrm{G}_{2}(q)_{U}|=2q(q^{2}-1)(q-\varepsilon 1)
. The possible factors for a core-free factorisation of an almost simple group with socle
\mathrm{G}_{2}(q)
are given in
[
Factns
]
*Table 5, but in no case is the order of the intersection of a factor with
\mathrm{G}_{2}(q)
equal to
|\mathrm{G}_{2}(q)_{U}|
. Thus
\mathrm{G}_{2}(q)\trianglelefteqslant H
, as listed in the case (e) of the Theorem.

It remains to deal with the case where
q=3
and
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}
. Let
U\in\mathcal{U}
. Then
U
contains four 1-dimensional subspaces with two being singular and two being nonsingular. Moreover, the nonsingular subspaces are not isometric and so one lies in
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{+}
and one lies in
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{-}
. Thus if
H\leqslant\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{2m+1}(q)
acts transitively on
\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}
then it also acts transitively on both
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{+}
and on
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{-}
. By parts (c) and (d) it follows that
m=3
and
\mathrm{G}_{2}(3)\lhd H
, as listed in case (e) of the Theorem. As mentioned in the previous paragraph,
\mathrm{G}_{2}(3)
is indeed transitive.
∎

7.2.
Orthogonal groups with odd dimension
n=2m+1\geqslant 3
and

q
even

As discussed above, the quadratic form
Q
on
V=(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{2m+1}
has trivial radical, while the radical
V^{\perp}
of its associated symmetric bilinear form
(\cdot,\cdot)
is the nonsingular 1-subspace
\langle d\rangle
. Moreover, the form
(\cdot,\cdot)
induces a nondegenerate alternating form on
V/V^{\perp}
given by
(u+V^{\perp},v+V^{\perp})=(u,v)
for all
u,v\in V
.
Since
\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{2m+1}(q)
acts faithfully on
V/V^{\perp}
it follows that
\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{2m+1}(q)\cong\Gamma\mathrm{Sp}_{2m}(q)
.
The families
\mathcal{U}
of subspaces we treat in this case (as a careful reading of Table
2.3
reveals) are
\mathcal{P}_{k}
for
k\leqslant m
,
\mathcal{N}_{k}^{\varepsilon}
for even
k\leqslant m
and
\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}
,
\mathcal{N}_{1}
(all nonsingular
1
-subspaces apart from
\langle d\rangle
), and
\mathcal{N}_{2m}^{\varepsilon}
for
\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}
.

Theorem 7.2
.

Suppose that
H\leqslant\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{2m+1}(q)
, where
m\geqslant 1
and
q
is even, such that
\Omega_{2m+1}(q)^{\prime}\not\leqslant H
. Suppose that
H
acts transitively on a set
\mathcal{U}
of subspaces of

given by Table
2.3
. Then either
m=1
and
H
is given by Theorem
3.3
, or
m\geqslant 2
and one of the following holds:

(a)

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{1}
,
H_{0}\trianglelefteqslant H
where
(H_{0},m,q)
is given in
Table

; or

, or

.

.

Proof.

Let
G=\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{2m+1}(q)
. The possibilities for
\mathcal{U}
were given before the statement, and in each case
G
leaves
\mathcal{U}
invariant. Let
H\leqslant G
act transitively on
\mathcal{U}
such that
\Omega_{2m+1}(q)\not\leqslant H
. If
m=1
then the possibilities for
H
are given by Theorem
3.3
. We assume from now on that
m\geqslant 2
. Let
\overline{V}=V/V^{\perp}
and for each
U\in\mathcal{U}
let
\overline{U}=(U+V^{\perp})/V^{\perp}
. Note that
\Omega_{2m+1}(q)=\mathrm{SO}_{2m+1}(q)=\mathrm{GO}_{2m+1}(q)\cong\mathrm{Sp}_{2m}(q)
, unless
(m,q)=(2,2)
, in which case
|\mathrm{SO}_{5}(q):\Omega_{5}(2)|=2
. Hence, if
\Omega_{2m+1}(q)^{\prime}\not\leqslant H
then
H
does not induce
\mathrm{Sp}_{2m}(q)^{\prime}
on
\overline{V}
.

Case

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{k}
for some
k\leqslant m
. Since
U\in\mathcal{U}
is totally singular,
U\cap V^{\perp}=\{0\}
and so
\overline{U}
is a totally isotropic
k
-subspace of
\overline{V}
.
Moreover, since
\mathrm{Sp}_{2m}(q)
acts transitively on the set of totally isotropic
k
-subspaces of
\overline{V}
it follows that all such subspaces of
\overline{V}
arise in this manner. Moreover, by
[
Taylor
]
*p. 143,
U
is the unique totally singular
k
-subspace of
U+V^{\perp}
and so there is a one-to-one correspondence between
\mathcal{U}
and the set of totally isotropic
k
-subspaces of
\overline{V}
.
Thus
H
acts transitively on
\mathcal{U}
if and only if it acts transitively on the set of totally isotropic
k
-subspaces of
\overline{V}
. Thus by Theorem
5.2
we have
k=1
or
m
and
H
is listed there. This gives parts (a) and (b) and note that
\mathrm{Sp}_{2a}(q^{b})\cong\Omega_{2a+1}(q^{b})
.

Case

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{1}
. Here
U\in\mathcal{U}
is a nonsingular 1-subspace other than
V^{\perp}=\langle d\rangle
. Then
U+V^{\perp}
contains a unique singular 1-subspace
\langle w\rangle
and the nonsingular 1-subspaces of
U+V^{\perp}
are
\langle d+\lambda w\rangle
for
\lambda\in\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Moreover, any singular 1-subspace
\langle w\rangle
in
V
gives rise to
q-1
nonsingular 1-subspaces in
V^{\perp}+\langle w\rangle

distinct from
V^{\perp}
. Hence
H\leqslant G
is transitive on
\mathcal{U}
if and only if
H
is transitive on
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and for each
\langle w\rangle\in\mathcal{P}_{1}
, the subgroup
H_{\langle w\rangle}
acts transitively on the set of
1
-subspaces
\langle d+\lambda w\rangle
for
\lambda\in\mathbb{F}_{q}\setminus\{0\}
, equivalently,
H_{\langle w\rangle}
acts transitive on the nonzero vectors in
\langle w\rangle
. Hence by part (a),
H_{0}\trianglelefteqslant H
where
H_{0}
is given in Table
7.5
. Moreover, for both possibilities,
(H_{0})_{\langle w\rangle}
induces a cyclic group of order
q-1
on
\langle w\rangle
(see
[
KL
]
*Lemma 4.1.2 when
H_{0}=\mathrm{Sp}_{2a}(q^{b})
and
[
rW
]
*Section 4.3.5 for
H_{0}=G_{2}(q^{b})^{\prime}
). Thus both possibilities for
H_{0}
are also transitive on
\mathcal{N}_{1}
, proving part (c).

Case

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{k}^{\varepsilon}
for some even
k\leqslant m
and
\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}
. Here
U\in\mathcal{U}
is a nondegenerate
k
-subspace. Hence
V^{\perp}\cap U=\{0\}
and
\overline{U}
is a nondegenerate
k
-subspace of
\overline{V}
. Since
\mathrm{Sp}_{2m}(q)
is transitive on the set of nondegenerate
k
-subspaces of
\overline{V}
, all such subspaces of
\overline{V}
arise in this way. Thus by Theorem
5.2
,
k=2
and
H
is one of the groups in Table
5.2
. In particular,
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}
or
\mathcal{N}_{2}^{-}
. Let
W=U+V^{\perp}
, a nondegenerate
3
-subspace of
V
. Now
H
is transitive on
\mathcal{N}_{2}^{\varepsilon}
if and only if
H_{W}
is transitive on the set of
2
-subspaces of
W
contained in
\mathcal{N}_{2}^{\varepsilon}
.
For
H
as in the first and last columns of Table
5.2
, it follows from
[
Factns
]
*3.2.1(a), p. 47 that
\overline{H}_{\overline{U}}^{\overline{U}}\rhd\mathrm{Sp}_{2}(q)
and so
H_{W}^{W}\trianglelefteqslant\mathrm{GO}_{3}(q)
. By Witt’s Lemma,
\mathrm{GO}_{3}(q)
is transitive on the set of nondegenerate 2-subspaces of
W
of each isometry type, as required.
We note that in the last column, the
\mathrm{Sp}_{2}(q^{2})
is occurring as an extension field group and not as
\Omega_{4}^{-}(q)
as it is not possible for the stabiliser of a nondegenerate hyperplane to be transitive on
\mathcal{N}_{2}^{\varepsilon}
.

For the remaining columns of Table
5.2
,
\mathrm{soc}(H)=\mathrm{G}_{2}(q^{m/3})^{\prime}
. By
[
Cooperstein
]
*p. 27 and Lemma 5.4,
\mathrm{G}_{2}(q^{m/3})
acts transitively on the set of nondegenerate 2-subspaces of a 6-dimensional space over
\mathbb{F}_{q^{m/3}}
equipped with a nondegenerate alternating form such that the stabiliser
K
of such a subspace
X
is the maximal subgroup
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(q^{m/3})\times\mathrm{SL}_{2}(q^{m/3})
. Moreover, by

[
rW
]
*Section 4.3.6 we see that
K
induces
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(q^{m/3})\cong\mathrm{GO}_{3}(q^{m/3})
on the nondegenerate 3-subspace corresponding to
X
of the 7-dimensional vector space over
\mathbb{F}_{q^{m/3}}
equipped with a nondegenerate quadratic form. Thus, if
X=\langle e_{1},f_{1}\rangle
, then the stabiliser in
K
of the
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-span
Y
of
\{e_{1},f_{1}\}
induces
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(q)\cong\mathrm{Sp}_{2}(q)
on
Y
. Hence
\mathrm{GO}_{3}(q)\trianglelefteqslant H_{W}^{W}
acts transitively on the set of subspaces in
\mathcal{N}_{2}^{\varepsilon}
contained in
W
and so each group in Table
5.2
that is transitive on the
\mathcal{N}_{2}
subspaces of
\overline{V}
is transitive on
\mathcal{N}_{2}^{\varepsilon}
, proving part (d).

Case

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{2m}^{\varepsilon}
for some
\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}
. Here
U
is a nondegenerate
2m
-subspace and
(U+V^{\perp})/V^{\perp}=\overline{V}
. By
[
Pollatsek1971
]
*Theorem 1.5,
\mathrm{Sp}_{2m}(q)_{U}=\mathrm{GO}_{2m}^{\varepsilon}(q)
is maximal in
\mathrm{Sp}_{2m}(q)
and so
G_{U}
is maximal in
G
. We assume the same setup as in the proof of Theorem
5.2
,
and in particular that
H\leqslant B
where
\overline{B}
is a core-free subgroup of
\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{Sp}_{2m}(q)
, and that
G^{*}=\overline{B}(G^{*})_{U}
is a core-free maximal factorisation, where
G^{*}
is an almost simple group with socle
L=\mathrm{PSp}_{2m}(q)
. Moreover,
B=N_{G}(B\cap\Omega_{2m+1}(q))
.

Suppose first that
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{2m}^{-}
. Here, when
(m,q)\neq(2,2)
, the transitive
subgroups of
G
not containing
\mathrm{Sp}_{2m}(q)
were computed
in
[
Reg
]
*Lemmas 4.6 with reference to
[
Reg
]
*Lemma 4.5. We note that however, the reference to
[
Reg
]
*Lemma 4.5 forgot to include the possibility that
\Omega_{2m}^{+}(q)\trianglelefteqslant H
when
q=2
or 4.
When
(m,q)=(2,2)
we have
|\mathcal{N}_{4}^{-}|=6
and
\mathrm{GO}_{5}(2)\cong\mathrm{Sym}_{6}
. This gives part (e).

Finally, suppose that
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{2m}^{+}
.
Suppose first that
(m,q)=(2,2)
. Then a
Magma
computation shows that either
\mathrm{Alt}_{6}=\Omega_{5}(2)
or
\mathrm{Alt}_{5}
are normal subgroups of
H
, or
H=F_{20}
. Moreover, both classes of
\mathrm{Alt}_{5}
-subgroups in
G
are transitive. The two
\mathrm{Alt}_{5}
subgroups correspond to
\Omega_{4}^{-}(2)
and
\Omega_{3}(4)
, each of which appears in Table
7.6
, while there is a unique conjugacy classes of
F_{20}
subgroups and so these must be normalising
\Omega_{2}^{-}(2^{2})\cong\textup{C}_{5}
as in the third column of Table
7.6
.
Thus we may assume that
(m,q)\neq(2,2)
, and then
it follows from
[
Factns
]
*p. 10, p. 13 that we have the five cases
in Table
7.7
. For reasons which will become clear later we deal with (f1) last.

. Moreover, a

. Then we have a factorisation

for

.

namely dihedral groups

, we have

. Hence we

. Note that

is the subgroup of

. However, by
[
Factns
]
no such factorisation exists (though one does exist of
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(17)
).

(f1) Here

. It is shown in
[

]
*3.2.1(d) that
(\mathrm{Sp}_{2a}(q^{b}).b)\cap\mathrm{GO}^{+}_{2m}(q)=\mathrm{GO}_{2a}(q^{b}).b

(and the argument holds for all

). It follows that the subgroup
\mathrm{Sp}_{2a}(q^{b})
is also transitive on

, and hence if

\mathrm{Sp}_{2a}(q^{b})\trianglelefteqslant H
then

is transitive on

and we have listed this in Table

. Suppose now that
\mathrm{Sp}_{2a}(q^{b})\not\leqslant H
. Then we have a factorisation
B=HB_{U}
. As just noted,
(\mathrm{Sp}_{2a}(q^{b}))_{U}=\mathrm{GO}^{+}_{2a}(q^{b})
and so the result follows by induction on
m
and noting that
q^{b}\neq 2
.
∎

8.
Orthogonal groups in even dimension of plus type

Let
V=(\mathbb{F}_{q})^{2m}
, with
m\geqslant 4
, equipped with a nondegenerate quadratic form
Q
of plus type and associated bilinear form
(\dot{,}\dot{)}
. We let
\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{m},f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}\}
be a basis for
V
such that
Q(e_{i})=Q(f_{i})=0
and
B(e_{i},f_{j})=\delta_{ij}
for all
i,j
.

In Subsection
8.2
, we find all subgroups of
\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{2m}^{+}(q)
that are transitive on a set
\mathcal{U}
given by Table
2.3
, namely
\mathcal{P}_{k}
for
k<m
,
\mathcal{P}_{m}^{\varepsilon}
for
\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}
,
\mathcal{N}_{k}^{\varepsilon}
for even
k\leqslant m
and
\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}
, and if
q
is odd also
\mathcal{N}_{k}^{\varepsilon}
for odd
k\leqslant m
and
\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}
, and if
q
is even also
\mathcal{N}_{1}
. The union
\mathcal{P}_{m}=\mathcal{P}_{m}^{+}\cup\mathcal{P}_{m}^{-}
is a
\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{2m}^{+}(q)
-orbit, and also if
k
and
q
are both odd then
\mathcal{N}_{k}=\mathcal{N}_{k}^{+}\cup\mathcal{N}_{k}^{-}
is a
\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{2m}^{+}(q)
-orbit. Subgroups acting transitively on these latter sets are determined in Subsection
8.3
, namely in Proposition
8.5
for
\mathcal{P}_{m}
and Proposition
8.6
for
\mathcal{N}_{k}
. The eight-dimensional case needs special attention and we do some preparatory work in Subsection
8.1
.

Remark 8.1
.

The subgroups of
\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{2m}^{+}(q)
that are transitive on
\mathcal{N}_{1}
when
q
is even, or on
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{\varepsilon}
, where
\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}
, when
q
is odd are determined in
[
Reg
]
*Lemma 4.5. However, some of the groups in
[
Reg
]
*Lemma 4.5 are given as subgroups of
\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{2m}^{+}(q)
instead of
\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{2m}^{+}(q)
, and in addition
\mathrm{Alt}_{6}
is incorrectly listed when
(m,q)=(4,3)
(see the proof of Lemma
8.3
(e)). We therefore give a revised version of this classification as part (e) of Theorem
8.4
(see, in particular, Table
8.3
). See also Remark
1.2
(e).

\triangle

The formulas for
|\mathcal{N}_{1}^{\varepsilon}|,|\mathcal{P}_{1}|,|\mathcal{P}_{m}^{\varepsilon}|
, and
|\mathcal{N}_{2}^{\varepsilon}|
(see
[
Factns
]
*pp. 62, 63, 67, 70) are:

(4)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{N}_{1}|

\displaystyle=q^{m-1}(q^{m}-1)\quad\textrm{ for $q$ even},

\displaystyle|\mathcal{N}_{1}^{\varepsilon}|=\frac{q^{m-1}(q^{m}-1)}{2}\quad\textrm{ for $q$ odd,}

(5)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{P}_{1}|

\displaystyle=\frac{q^{2m}-1}{q-1}-q^{m-1}(q^{m}-1),

(6)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{P}_{m}^{\varepsilon}|

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}(q^{i}+1)=|\mathcal{P}_{m-1}|,

\displaystyle|\mathcal{N}_{2}^{\varepsilon}|=\frac{q^{2m-2}(q^{m}-1)(q^{m-1}+\varepsilon 1)}{2(q-\varepsilon 1)}.

The formula for
|\mathcal{P}_{1}|
factors to give
|\mathcal{P}_{1}|=\frac{(q^{m}-1)(q^{m-1}+1)}{q-1}
. When
m=4
, we see that
|\mathcal{P}_{1}|=(q+1)(q^{2}+1)(q^{3}+1)=|\mathcal{P}_{4}^{{\textbf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}$\varepsilon$}}}|
.

8.1.
Eight-dimensional orthogonal groups of plus type

The groups
\mathrm{P}\Omega_{8}^{+}(q)
have more outer automorphisms than their counterparts in higher dimensions and this affects both their subgroup structure and actions on subspace families. We first make some technical comments, in particular concerning our notation.

Remark 8.2
.

When
L=\mathrm{P}\Omega_{8}^{+}(q)
we have
|\mathrm{Aut}(L):\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{8}^{+}(q)|=3
. Let
\tau
be an outer automorphism of
L
of order
3
that is not in
\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{8}^{+}(q)
. It is well-known that
\tau
induces a 3-cycle
(\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{4}^{+},\mathcal{P}_{4}^{-})
. We note that
\tau
does not induce an automorphism of the matrix group
\Omega_{8}^{+}(q)
when
q
is odd. Indeed the stabiliser in
\Omega_{8}^{+}(q)
of a totally singular 1-subspace is not isomorphic to the stabiliser of a totally singular 4-subspace.

There are two types of subgroups
\Omega_{7}(q)
of
L=\mathrm{P}\Omega_{8}^{+}(q)
which
we denote by
\Omega_{7}(q)^{R}
and
\Omega_{7}(q)^{I}
, where
\Omega_{7}(q)^{R}
acts reducibly on
V
fixing a nonsingular 1-subspace,
and
\Omega_{7}(q)^{I}
acts irreducibly on
V
. If
q
is odd, there are
four

L
-conjugacy classes of
\Omega_{7}(q)^{I}
and
two
of
\Omega_{7}(q)^{R}
; we note that
\Omega_{7}(q)^{R}
has preimage
2\times\Omega_{7}(q)
in
\Omega_{8}^{+}(q)
while
\Omega_{7}(q)^{I}
has preimage
2\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\Omega_{7}(q)

[
BHRD
]
*Table 8.50. When
q
is even, there are
two

L
-conjugacy classes of subgroups
\Omega_{7}(q)^{I}
and
one
of
\Omega_{7}(q)^{R}
; and in this case each preimage is isomorphic to
\Omega_{7}(q)\cong\mathrm{Sp}_{6}(q)
. Furthermore,
\tau
induces 3-cycles
(\Omega_{7}(q)^{I},\Omega_{7}(q)^{I},\Omega_{7}(q)^{R})
on these classes, for all values of
q
. We use
{}^{\wedge}\Omega_{7}(q)^{I}
to denote the minimal preimage of
\Omega_{7}(q)^{I}
in
\Omega_{8}^{+}(q)
. Hence
{}^{\wedge}\Omega_{7}(q)^{I}=2\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\Omega_{7}(q)
when
q
is odd, and is equal to
\Omega_{7}(q)^{I}
when
q
is even.

Let
M_{2}
denote an imprimitive subgroup of
L
preserving a decomposition
V=E\oplus F
into totally singular 4-subspaces

E
and
F
. There are two
L
-conjugacy classes of subgroups
M_{2}
and one
L
-conjugacy class of subgroups
N_{2}^{+}
, and
\tau
induces a 3-cycle
(M_{2},M_{2},N_{2}^{+})
on these classes.
Finally, we note that
\Omega_{8}^{+}(q)
has two conjugacy classes of subgroups isomorphic to
\mathrm{SU}_{4}(q)
. Letting
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
be the images in
L
of two such non-conjugate subgroups, the outer automorphism
\tau
induces a 3-cycle
(N_{L}(K_{1}),N_{L}(K_{2}),N_{2}^{-})
.
These observations are important in the proof of Theorem
8.4
when considering actions on
\mathcal{P}_{1}
,
\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}
and
\mathcal{N}_{2}^{-}
.

\triangle

Arguments for the group
\mathrm{P}\Omega_{8}^{+}(3)
are so delicate that we have separated them out in the following lemma.

Lemma 8.3
.

Let
H\leqslant\Gamma\mathrm{O}^{+}_{8}(3)
with
\Omega_{8}^{+}(3)\not\leqslant H
.

(1)

If
H
acts transitively on a set
\mathcal{U}
of subspaces given in Table
2.3
then
H
is listed in Theorem
8.4
.

(2)

If
H
acts transitively on
\mathcal{P}_{4}
then
H
is given in Proposition
8.5
.

(3)

If
H
acts transitively on
\mathcal{N}_{1}
then
H
is given in Proposition
8.6
.

Proof.

(1) Suppose first that
\mathcal{U}
is a set of subspaces given in Table
2.3
. Let
G
be the stabiliser in
\Gamma\mathrm{O}^{+}_{8}(3)=\mathrm{CO}^{+}_{8}(3)
of
\mathcal{U}
and let
H\leqslant G
act transitively on
\mathcal{U}
such that
\Omega_{8}^{+}(3)\not\leqslant H
. Then
G=\mathrm{CO}^{+}_{8}(3)
unless
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{4}^{+},\mathcal{P}_{4}^{-},\mathcal{N}_{1}^{\varepsilon}
or
\mathcal{N}_{3}^{\varepsilon}
. In the first two of the exceptional cases
G
is an index 2 subgroup of
\mathrm{CO}_{8}^{+}(3)
containing
\mathrm{SO}_{8}^{+}(3)
while in the last two cases we have
G=\mathrm{GO}_{8}^{+}(3)
. For
X\leqslant\mathrm{CO}^{+}_{8}(3)
denote the image of
X
in
\mathrm{PCO}^{+}_{8}(3)
by
\overline{X}
. We now deal with each possibility for
\mathcal{U}
from Table
2.3
, in most cases using
Magma
.

(a) First consider
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{4}^{\varepsilon}
, where
\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}
, and
G=\mathrm{CO}^{+}_{8}(3)
. Let
L=\Omega_{8}^{+}(3)
and note that
L
is transitive on
\mathcal{U}
. Now by
[
BG
]
*Table 4.1.2,
|\mathcal{U}|=\frac{1}{2}3^{8}(3^{2}+1)^{2}(3^{4}+3^{2}+1)
if
\varepsilon=+
and
|\mathcal{U}|=\frac{1}{2}3^{8}(3^{2}-1)(3^{6}-1)
if
\varepsilon=-
. In either case
|\mathcal{U}|>3^{15}
, and since
H
is transitive on
\mathcal{U}
we have

|H|>3^{15}
. Also (see
[
KL
]
*Table 2.1C)
|G:L|=8
and
|\mathrm{P}\Omega_{8}^{+}(3)|=\frac{1}{4}3^{12}(3^{6}-1)(3^{4}-1)^{2}(3^{2}-1)<\frac{1}{4}.3^{28}<3^{29}
, and it follows that
|L|<3^{30}
and
|H\cap L|\geqslant\frac{1}{8}|H|>3^{13}>|L|^{1/3}
. Thus
H\cap L
is one of the groups from
[
AB
]
*Table 7 or Proposition 4.23. Moreover for both values of
\varepsilon
the order
|H\cap L|
is divisible by
3^{4}+3^{2}+1=7\times 13
, and the only one of these subgroups with this property is
H\cap L\cong\Omega_{7}(3)
. In particular
H\cap L
is maximal in
L
and since, for
U\in\mathcal{U}
,
L
is transitive on
\mathcal{U}
and its overgroup
L_{\{U,U^{\perp}\}}
is maximal in
L
, we have a maximal core-free factorisation
L=(H\cap L)(L_{\{U,U^{\perp}\}})
. However there are no such factorisations by
[
Factns
]
*Table 4.

(b) Next
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{3}^{\varepsilon}
and
G=\mathrm{GO}^{+}_{8}(3)
. Note that
\mathcal{N}_{3}^{+}
and
\mathcal{N}_{3}^{-}
are fused in
\mathrm{CO}_{8}^{+}(3)
. By
Magma
and Lemma
2.1
, we deduce that
2\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\Omega_{7}(3)\trianglelefteqslant H
, which appears in Theorem
8.4
(g).

(c) Next consider
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}
and
G=\mathrm{CO}^{+}_{8}(3)
. A
Magma
calculation and Lemma
2.1
shows that
2\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\Omega_{7}(3)\trianglelefteqslant H
, which appears in Table
8.4
.

(d) The next case is
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{2}^{-}
and
G=\mathrm{CO}^{+}_{8}(3)
. By
Magma
and Lemma
2.1
, we deduce that either
2\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\Omega_{7}(3)\trianglelefteqslant H
(which appears in Table
8.4
), or
3^{6}\rtimes\mathrm{PSL}_{4}(3)\trianglelefteqslant\overline{H}
and
H
is contained in
P_{4}
. We note that since
\mathrm{SL}_{4}(3)
is quasisimple, Lemma
2.1
applied to the subgroup
\mathrm{PSL}_{4}(3)
of
\overline{H}
implies that
3^{6}\rtimes\mathrm{SL}_{4}(3)\trianglelefteqslant H
, as in Theorem
8.4
(f).

(e) Next consider
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{1}^{\varepsilon}
and
G=\mathrm{GO}^{+}_{8}(3)
. Note that
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{+}
and
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{-}
are fused in
\mathrm{CO}_{8}^{+}(3)
. The possibilities for
H
are given by
[
Reg
]
*Lemma 4.5 but there only the projective versions are listed. Combining
[
Reg
]
*Lemma 4.5 and a
Magma
calculation we obtain the following list of examples:

(i)

H\trianglerighteqslant\mathrm{SL}_{4}(3)
,
\mathrm{SU}_{4}(3)
or
\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(3)
,

(ii)

H\trianglerighteqslant 2\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\Omega_{7}(3)
,
2\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\Omega_{8}^{+}(2)
,
2\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\mathrm{Sp}_{6}(2)
,
2\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\mathrm{SU}_{4}(2)
,
2\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\mathrm{Alt}_{9}
,

(iii)

H\leqslant P_{4}
and modulo the unipotent radical
H
induces a subgroup of
\mathrm{GL}_{4}(3)
which is transitive on 1-subspaces.

The subgroups in parts (i) and (ii) appear in Table
8.3
, and those in part (iii) are as in Theorem
8.4
(e). Note that in (ii),
2\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\Omega_{7}(3)
is the group

{}^{\wedge}\Omega_{7}(3)^{I}
, and
\mathrm{PSU}_{4}(2)\cong\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(3)
but this occurs differently to the group
\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(3)
in (i). Also no groups with normal subgroup
\mathrm{Alt}_{6}
, as listed in
[
Reg
]
*Lemma 4.5(vii) for
m=4,q=3
, act transitively on
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{\varepsilon}
.

(f) Here
G=\mathrm{CO}_{8}^{+}(3)
acts on
|\mathcal{P}_{1}|=1120
singular
1
-subspaces. A
Magma
calculation shows that
H
contains one of the following as a normal subgroup

,

,
2\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\mathrm{Alt}_{8}
,

,

,

,
\mathrm{SU}_{4}(3)
,

, or

. These all appear in Table

.

(g) When
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{2}
, a

.

(h) When

we have

and a

contains

as a normal subgroup, as in Theorem

(i) Finally suppose that

and

that fixes each

-orbit on totally singular 4-subspaces. Note that if

then

or

where

. Applying triality to their images in

and pulling back to

(and both lie in subgroup families in Table

). Similarly, the

(and both lie in subgroup families in Table

).

]

, for

. Finally, a

[
Magma
]

,

,

(g)

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{3}^{\varepsilon}
,
m=4
,
q
is odd and
{}^{\wedge}\Omega_{7}(q)^{I}\trianglelefteqslant H
; or

(h)

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{4}^{-}
,
m=4
, and either
q=2
and
H=\Omega_{7}(2)^{I}
, or
q=4
and
\Omega_{7}(4)^{I}.2\trianglelefteqslant H
.

Proof.

Let
G
be the setwise stabiliser in
\Gamma\mathrm{O}^{+}_{2m}(q)
of
\mathcal{U}
and let
H\leqslant G
act transitively on
\mathcal{U}
such that
\Omega_{2m}^{+}(q)\not\leqslant H
. Note that
G=\Gamma\mathrm{O}^{+}_{2m}(q)
unless
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{m}^{\varepsilon}
, which consists of totally singular
m
-subspaces, or

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{k}^{\varepsilon}
, the set of nondegenerate
k
-subspaces of a given isometry type
\varepsilon
with
kq
odd. In these exceptional cases
G
is an index two subgroup of
\Gamma\mathrm{O}^{+}_{2m}(q)
, and
G\geqslant\Omega^{+}_{2m}(q)
if
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{m}^{\varepsilon}
, or
G\geqslant\mathrm{GO}_{2m}^{+}(q)
if
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{k}^{\varepsilon}
. By Theorem
3.5
we may assume that
m\geqslant 4
, and by Lemma
8.3
we may assume also that
(m,q)\neq(4,3)
. If
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{1}
(with
q
even) or
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{1}^{\varepsilon}
(with
q
odd), then part (e) follows from
[
Reg
]
*Lemma 4.5, see also Remark
8.1
.

Next suppose that
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}
and
q=2
. Then
U\in\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}
contains a unique nonsingular vector
v
and so
G_{U}\leqslant G_{v}
. This implies that
H
acts transitively on
\mathcal{N}_{1}
and so is given by part (e). By (
6
),
|\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}|=2^{2m-3}(2^{m}-1)(2^{m-1}+1)
. Hence if
m>4
then
|H|
is divisible by a primitive prime divisor of
2^{2m-2}-1
. It follows that either
m
is even and
\mathrm{SU}_{m}(2)\trianglelefteqslant H
, or
m=6
and
3\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}M_{22}\trianglelefteqslant H
, or
m=4
. A
Magma
calculation shows that
N_{G}(3\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}M_{22})
is not transitive on
\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}
when
m=6
, while another shows that when
m=4
either

\mathrm{Alt}_{9}
,
\Omega_{7}(2)^{I}
or
\mathrm{SU}_{4}(2)
is normal in
H
. Moreover, Lemma
6.1
shows that
\mathrm{SU}_{m}(2)
is transitive on
\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}
for all even
m
. Thus
H
is as listed in Table
8.4
.

We also note that a
Magma
calculation shows that, when
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{4}^{+}
and
(m,q)=(4,2)
, there are no possibilities for
H
.

The remaining families
\mathcal{U}
to be considered are therefore
\mathcal{P}_{k}
for
k<m
,
\mathcal{P}_{m}^{\varepsilon}
,
\mathcal{N}_{k}^{\varepsilon}
for even

k\leqslant m
with
(\mathcal{U},q)\neq(\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+},2)
and
(\mathcal{U},m,q)\neq(\mathcal{N}_{4}^{+},4,2)
, and
if
q
is odd also
\mathcal{N}_{k}^{\varepsilon}
with
k
odd and
3\leqslant k\leqslant m
. Let
U\in\mathcal{U}
.
If
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{m}^{\varepsilon}
and
U^{\perp}
is isometric to
U
then
G_{U}
is an index 2 subgroup of
G_{\{U,U^{\perp}\}}
, which by
[
King1981a
]
is maximal in
G
. In all other cases,
G_{U}=G_{\{U,U^{\perp}\}}
, and moreover
G
acts primitively on
\mathcal{U}
: if

(\mathcal{U},q)=(\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+},3)
this follows from
[
KL
,
BHRD
]
(recalling that here
G=\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{2m}^{+}(q)
), and in all other cases it follows from
[
King1981
]
,
[
King1981a
]
and
[
King1982
]
(noting that
G
contains either
\mathrm{GO}^{+}_{2m}(q)
or
\Omega_{2m}^{+}(q)
).
Thus in particular, in all cases
G_{\{U,U^{\perp}\}}
is maximal in
G
.

Denote the image of any subgroup
X
of
\Gamma\mathrm{O}^{+}_{2m}(q)
in
\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{2m}^{+}(q)
by
\overline{X}
and let
L=\mathrm{P}\Omega_{2m}^{+}(q)
. Then we have a factorisation
\overline{G}=\overline{H}\,\overline{G}_{\{U,U^{\perp}\}}
. Since
\Omega_{2m}^{+}(q)
is quasisimple, Lemma
2.1
implies that
L\not\leqslant\overline{H}
. Let
\overline{B}
be maximal among core-free subgroups of
G
containing
\overline{H}
and let
G^{*}=\overline{B}L
. Then by Lemma
2.2
and the fact that
(m,q)\neq(4,3)
we have that
\overline{B}
is maximal in
G^{*}
and we have a core-free maximal factorisation
G^{*}=\overline{B}(G^{*})_{\{U,U\}}
.

Suppose first that
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{m}^{\varepsilon}
and
U
is isometric to
U^{\perp}
. Since we have a core-free maximal factorisation
G^{*}=\overline{B}(G^{*})_{\{U,U\}}
,
[
Factns
]
implies that
m=4
,
q=2
or
4
,
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{4}^{-}
, and
\overline{B}=N_{G}(\Omega_{7}(q)^{I})
. A
Magma
calculation shows that when
q=2
the only example we get is
H=\Omega_{7}(2)^{I}
. When
q=4
,
[
BHRD
]
*Table 8.50 implies that
N_{\overline{G}}(\Omega_{7}(4)^{I})=\Omega_{7}(4)^{I}.2
, where the extra 2 is a field automorphism. Constructing an appropriate
\Omega_{8}^{+}(4).2
as a subgroup of
\Omega_{16}^{+}(2)
in
Magma
, enables us to calculate the intersection of
N_{\overline{G}}(\Omega_{7}(4)^{I})
with the stabiliser of a subspace in
\mathcal{N}_{4}^{-}
and show that
N_{\overline{G}}(\Omega_{7}(4)^{I})
is transitive on
\mathcal{N}_{4}^{-}
while
\Omega_{7}(4)^{I}
is not. Thus
\Omega_{7}(4)^{I}.2\trianglelefteqslant H
. This gives part (h).

In all remaining cases
G_{U}=G_{\{U,U^{\perp}\}}
, and
G_{U}
is maximal in
G
. Then
\mathcal{U}
and the possibilities for
\overline{B}
are given by
[
Factns
]
and are collated in Table
8.5
according to the case of the theorem in which the family
\mathcal{U}
is addressed.. Note that the Case (g) appears in disguise in
[
Factns
]
*Table 4, p. 14
because when
m=4
the subgroup
(\mathrm{PSp}_{2}(q)\otimes\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(q)).2
is isomorphic to
(\mathrm{P}\Omega_{3}(q)\times\mathrm{P}\Omega_{5}(q)).2
and is mapped to an
N_{3}
-subgroup under triality. (In constructing Table
8.5
recall that
(m,q)\neq(4,3)
and also that our notation for the entries
P_{m},P_{m-1}
in the tables in
[
Factns
]
is
\mathcal{P}_{m}^{\varepsilon}
.)

By Lemma
2.2
,
\overline{B}=N_{\overline{G}}(\overline{B}\cap L)
and so letting

be the full preimage of
\overline{B}
in

, the Correspondence Theorem implies that

. Table

lists the groups

. We provide the following extra justification for when

:
By
[

*Table 4, if
L=\Omega^{+}_{8}(4)
then there is a

G=AB
where

. However
N_{2}^{-}

is not transitive on
\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}
and similarly
N_{2}^{+}
is not transitive on

\mathcal{N}_{2}^{-}
as, for a given
U\in\mathcal{N}_{2}^{-}
, there exist

with

and

, or

. Moreover, there are two possible
P_{i}
for each choice of

. Since the orthogonal complement of a subspace in

1
-subspaces, it is not possible for

\mathcal{P}_{1}
. Thus
N_{2}^{-}

\mathcal{P}_{4}^{-}
and
\mathcal{P}_{4}^{+}
. This gives case (d2). Moreover, the image of

subgroup and so such a subgroup is transitive on
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and either
\mathcal{P}_{4}^{-}
or
\mathcal{P}_{4}^{+}
. This gives cases (a2) and (d4).
