Case
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{1}
.
We consider cases (a1) to (a3) in Table
8.6
.

(a1) Here
\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{8}^{+}(2)=\textup{SO}_{8}^{+}(2)
acts faithfully on
|\mathcal{P}_{1}|=135
singular vectors. We use
Magma
and find that there are eight conjugacy classes of transitive subgroups of
\mathrm{GO}_{8}^{+}(2)
. Moreover, if
\Omega_{8}^{+}(2)\not\leqslant H
then
H
normalises one of
\mathrm{SU}_{4}(2)\cong\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(3)
,
\mathrm{Alt}_{9}
or
\Omega_{7}(2)^{I}
, as listed in Table
8.1
.

(a2) In this case
B=N_{G}(\mathrm{SU}_{m}(q))
where
m
is even and
H\leqslant B
is transitive on
\mathcal{P}_{1}
. Consider
[
Factns
]
*3.6.3(a), p. 70, and the
argument on
[
Factns
]
*3.5.2(a), p. 60. Let
k=\mathbb{F}_{q}
and
K=\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}
.
Here
V=K^{m}
admits a
K
-hermitian form
[\,,\,]
and the
k
-quadratic form
Q
preserved by
{\rm O}_{2m}^{+}(q)
satisfies
Q(v)=[v,v]
for
v\in V
.
Let
U\in\mathcal{U}
. Then
KU:=\{\lambda u\mid\lambda\in K,u\in U\}

is a singular
1
-subspace of the hermitian space
V=K^{m}
. Since
m\geqslant 4
, the subgroup
T=\mathrm{SU}_{m}(q)
is transitive on
the set of nonzero singular vectors of
K^{m}
by
[
KL
]
*Lemma 2.10.5, p. 49. Moreover, the stabilizer
T_{KU}
is transitive
on the set of
q+1
singular
1
-dimensional
k
-subspaces of
KU
. Thus
\mathrm{SU}_{m}(q)
is transitive on
\mathcal{U}
as recorded in Table
8.1
.
Suppose now that
\mathrm{SU}_{m}(q)\not\leqslant H
. Then we have the factorisation
B=B_{U}\,H
. Since
B_{U}
fixes
KU
, which is a 1-dimensional
K
-subspace that is totally singular with respect to the hermitian form, it follow that
H
acts transitively on the set of totally singular 1-subspaces over
K
. Since
m
is
even and
(m,q)\neq(4,3)
, it follows from Theorem
4.1
that there are no such factorisations.

(a3) In this case
m=4
and
G=P_{1}\,B
where
B=N_{G}({}^{\wedge}\Omega_{7}(q)^{I})
. Write
P_{1}=G_{U}
where
U\in\mathcal{P}_{1}
.
In light of Case (a1) and our assumption that
(m,q)\neq(4,3)
, we will assume
q\geqslant 4
. By
[
Factns
]
*Table 4 we see that
{}^{\wedge}\Omega_{7}(q)^{I}
is transitive on
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and so
we obtain the example
{}^{\wedge}\Omega_{7}(q)^{I}\trianglelefteqslant H
in Table
8.1
.
Suppose now that
{}^{\wedge}\Omega_{7}(q)^{I}\not\leqslant H
. Since
H\leqslant B
, and
H
is transitive on
\mathcal{P}_{1}
, we have
B=B_{U}\,H
and
\overline{B}=\overline{B}_{U}\overline{H}
.
Set
S:=\overline{B}^{(\infty)}=\Omega_{7}(q)^{I}
and let
X
be the natural 7-dimensional module for
S
over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. The formula (
5
) for
|\mathcal{P}_{1}|
gives

|\mathcal{U}|=\frac{(q^{4}-1)(q^{3}+1)}{q-1}=|G:G_{U}|=|S:S_{U}|.

Since
\mathcal{U}=|S:S_{U}|
is coprime to
q
, it follows from
[
Seitz
]
*(1.6) that any maximal subgroup of
S
containing
S_{U}
is a parabolic subgroup. Comparing
|\mathcal{U}|
with the indices of parabolic subgroups given in (
3
), we deduce that
S_{U}
is the stabiliser in
S
of a totally singular
3
-subspace of
X
. Thus
\overline{H}
acts transitively on the set of totally singular
3
-subspaces of
X
.

Since
q\geqslant 4
, Theorem
7.1
(a) (see Table
7.1
) when
q
is odd and Theorem
7.2
when
q
is even, imply that
\Omega_{6}^{-}(q)\trianglelefteqslant\overline{H}
. In particular,
H
fixes a unique nondegenerate subspace
W
of
X
. Thus letting
\tau
be a triality automorphism of
\mathrm{P}\Omega_{8}^{+}(q)
such that
\overline{B}^{\tau}
normalises an
\Omega_{7}(q)^{R}
subgroup we have that
\Omega_{6}^{-}(q)^{\tau}\trianglelefteqslant\overline{H}^{\tau}\leqslant N_{2}^{-}
. Since
\tau
cyclically permutes
N_{2}^{-}
with two
\mathcal{C}_{3}
-subgroups of type
\mathrm{SU}_{4}(q)
(see Remark
8.2
),
\Omega_{6}^{-}(q)\cong\mathrm{SU}_{4}(q)/\langle\pm I\rangle
, and
{}^{\wedge}\Omega_{7}(q)=2\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\Omega_{7}(q)
when
q
is odd,
it follows that
\mathrm{SU}_{4}(q)\trianglelefteqslant H
. Thus
H
is already listed in Table
8.1
.

Case
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{m}^{\varepsilon}
.
There are two orbits of
L
on the set of totally singular
m
-subspaces:
one is labelled
\mathcal{P}_{m}^{+}
and the other
\mathcal{P}_{m}^{-}
. The two orbits are fused in
\mathrm{P}\Gamma\mathrm{O}^{+}_{2m}(q)
. Hence there is no loss of generality in assuming that

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{P}_{m}^{+}
and
U\in\mathcal{P}_{m}^{+}
, a (maximal) totally singular
m
-subspace of
V
.
When
m=4
, triality induces a 3-cycle
(\mathcal{P}_{m}^{+},\mathcal{P}_{m}^{-},\mathcal{P}_{1})
by Remark
8.2
. Thus a suitable image of
\overline{H}
under triality is transitive on
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and so is given in part (a). Recall that triality induces the 3-cycles
(\Omega_{7}(q)^{I},\Omega_{7}(q)^{I},\Omega_{7}(q)^{R})
and
(N_{L}(K_{1}),N_{L}(K_{2}),N_{2}^{-})
on subgroups of
\mathrm{P}\Omega_{8}^{+}(q)
, where
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
are the images in
L=\mathrm{P}\Omega_{8}^{+}(q)
of subgroups isomorphic to
\mathrm{SU}_{4}(q)
. Then also using
[
BHRD
]
*Table 8.50 to obtain the correct structure for
H
in the other cases, we list all possibilities obtained in this way in Table
8.2
.
Thus from now on we may assume that
m\geqslant 5
, and in particular we only need to deal with the cases (d1) and (d2) in Table
8.6
.

(d1) In this case
B=G_{\langle v\rangle}
for some nonsingular vector
v
. Set
W=\langle v\rangle^{\perp}
.
Observe that
(v,v)=Q(v+v)-Q(v)-Q(v)=2Q(v)=0
if and only if
q
is even.
Hence
N_{1}
preserves the decomposition

V=\langle v\rangle\oplus W
when
q
is odd, and preserves the chain
0<\langle v\rangle<W<V
when

q
is even. In both cases the restriction
N_{1}^{W}
of
N_{1}
to
W

contains
\Omega_{2m-1}(q)
by Witt’s lemma, and we note that
\Omega_{2m-1}(q)\cong\mathrm{Sp}_{2m-2}(q)
when
q
is even. Moreover,
\Omega_{2m-1}(q)=B^{(\infty)}=(B^{W})^{(\infty)}
, and so if
\Omega_{2m-1}(q)\leqslant H^{W}
then Lemma
2.1
implies that
\Omega_{2m-1}(q)^{R}\trianglelefteqslant H
, as listed in Table
8.2
.

Suppose now that
\Omega_{2m-1}(q)\not\leqslant H^{W}
.
Now
Y:=\langle v\rangle^{\perp}\cap U
is a totally singular

(m-1)
-subspace that is stabilised by
B\cap P_{m}
.
Hence
B\cap P_{m}\leqslant B_{Y}
and
(B\cap P_{m})^{W}\leqslant(B_{Y})^{W}=P_{m-1}(B^{W})
.
Thus we have a factorisation
B^{W}=(B\cap P_{m})^{W}\,H^{W}=P_{m-1}(B^{W})\,H^{W}
and therefore
H^{W}
is transitive on the set of totally singular
(m-1)
-subspaces of
W
.
When
q
is odd and recalling that
m\geqslant 5
, we deduce from Theorem
7.1
that
\Omega_{2m-2}^{-}(q)\trianglelefteqslant H^{W}
. Similarly, when
q
is even we note that
\Omega_{2m-1}(q)\cong\mathrm{Sp}_{2m-2}(q)
and we deduce from Theorem
5.2
(b) that again
\Omega_{2m-2}^{-}(q)^{R}\trianglelefteqslant H^{W}
. In both cases, it follows from Lemma
2.1
that
\Omega_{2m-2}^{-}(q)^{R}\trianglelefteqslant H
, as listed in Table
8.2
.

(d2) In this case
B=N_{2}^{-}=G_{W^{\perp}}=G_{W}
for some
nondegenerate
(2m-2)
-subspace
W
such that
W^{\perp}\in\mathcal{N}_{2}^{-}
. In particular,
N_{2}^{-}
preserves the
decomposition
V=W\oplus W^{\perp}
and by
[
KL
]
*Lemma 4.1.1,
N_{2}^{-}
contains
\Omega_{2}^{-}(q)\times\Omega_{2m-2}^{-}(q)
as a normal subgroup.
Since
\mathrm{GO}_{2}^{-}(q)
is solvable it follows that
B^{(\infty)}=(B^{W})^{(\infty)}=\Omega_{2m-2}^{-}(q)
. Thus by Lemma
2.1
, if
\Omega_{2m-2}^{-}(q)\leqslant H^{W}
it follows that
\Omega_{2m-2}^{-}(q)^{R}\trianglelefteqslant H
, as listed in Table
8.2
.

Suppose next that
\Omega_{2m-2}^{-}(q)\not\leqslant H^{W}
, so we have a factorisation
B^{W}=(B\cap P_{m})^{W}H^{W}
.
Observe that
U\cap W^{\perp}=0
as
U\in\mathcal{P}_{m}
and
W^{\perp}\in\mathcal{N}_{2}^{-}
. Therefore
\dim(U\oplus W^{\perp})=m+2
, and hence also

\dim(U\cap W)=m-2
as
(U\oplus W^{\perp})^{\perp}=U^{\perp}\cap W=U\cap W
. Since
\dim(W)=2m-2

and
W
has Witt index
\frac{2m-2}{2}-1=m-2
, it follows that
Y:=U\cap W
is a (maximal)
totally singular
(m-2)
-subspace of
W
. Thus
B\cap P_{m}\leqslant B_{Y}
and we have a factorisation
B^{W}=(B_{Y})^{W}H^{W}
. In particular,
H^{W}
is a subgroup of
\Gamma\mathrm{O}^{-}_{2m-2}(q)
that is transitive on totally singular
(m-2)
-subspaces and does not contain
\Omega_{2m-2}^{-}(q)
. Since
m\geqslant 5
, Theorem
6.2
implies that no such group exists.

Case
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{2}^{+}
.
Here
G=\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{2m}^{+}(q)
. We follow the case distinction in Table
8.6
, and we note that in each case

.

(f
+

so suppose that

.

.

-subspaces of

. Suppose first that

for

As

). Letting

must be divisible by a primitive prime divisor

.

Case
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{2}^{-}
.

.

(f
-
1) In this case

.

, and

, then the unipotent radical of

(f
-

8.3.
Actions on

and

First we treat the subspace family

-orbit.

Proof.

is transitive on
\mathcal{P}_{m}
,
H^{+}:=H\cap G^{+}
is an index 2 subgroup of
H
that fixes each of

\mathcal{P}_{m}^{+}
and
\mathcal{P}_{m}^{-}
setwise, and is transitive on both.
Now
\Omega_{2m}^{+}(q)\not\leqslant H^{+}
, by our assumptions on
H
, and so
H^{+}
is given by Theorem
8.4
(d).
One possibility is that
m=3
and
H^{+}\leqslant N_{A}(\Omega_{2}^{-}(q^{2}))\leqslant N_{4}^{-}
where
A
is the stabiliser in
\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{6}^{+}(q)
of
\mathcal{P}_{3}^{+}
and so this becomes
N_{\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{6}^{+}(q)}(\Omega_{2}^{-}(q^{2}))\leqslant N_{4}^{-}
. Suppose now that this is not the case. Then by
Theorem
8.4
(d),
H^{+}

contains a normal subgroup
H_{0}
given by Table
8.2
.

In all but one case
H_{0}
is perfect and is equal to the
soluble residual
(H^{+})^{(\infty)}
of
H^{+}
. In particular
H_{0}
is a characteristic subgroup of
H^{+}
and hence is normal in
H
.
In the exceptional case
H_{0}=2^{4}\colon 5
in
\Omega_{6}^{+}(3)
, and again

H_{0}
is a characteristic subgroup of
H^{+}
and is normal in
H
.
Note that
H_{0}
in this proof refers to the subgroup
H_{0}
listed
in Table
8.2
. The subgroup
H_{1}
listed in
Table
8.7
contains
H_{0}
but may be larger,
such as
H_{1}=\langle H_{0},h\rangle
where
h
is described below.

The subgroups
H
determined by Table
8.2
are transitive
on
\mathcal{P}^{+}_{m}

or

\mathcal{P}^{-}_{m}
, but we require
H
to be
transitive on
both
. This holds if and only if there is an
element
h\in G\setminus G^{+}
such that
h
normalises
H_{0}
.
Such an element
h
interchanges
\mathcal{P}^{+}_{m}
and
\mathcal{P}^{-}_{m}
, and
conjugates the stabiliser in
H
of a subspace of
\mathcal{P}_{m}^{+}

to the stabiliser of a subspace of
\mathcal{P}_{m}^{-}
.

Case

H_{0}=\Omega_{2m-1}(q)^{R}
. Here
H_{0}
stabilises a nonsingular
1
-subspace. Suppose first that
q
is even. Then there is a unique
\Omega_{2m}^{+}(q)
-orbit on nonsingular
1
-subspaces and hence a unique
\Omega_{2m}^{+}(q)
-conjugacy class of subgroups
H_{0}
. Hence
H_{0}
is normalised by some element
h\in\mathrm{GO}_{2m}^{+}(q)\backslash\Omega_{2m}^{+}(q)
. As discussed above,
h
interchanges
\mathcal{P}_{m}^{+}
and
\mathcal{P}_{m}^{-}
, and in this case
H_{0}
itself is transitive on both
\mathcal{P}_{m}^{+}
and
\mathcal{P}_{m}^{-}
, so
\langle H_{0},h\rangle
acts transitively on
\mathcal{P}_{m}
. When
q
is odd there are two
\Omega_{2m}^{+}(q)
-orbits on nonsingular
1
-subspaces and hence two
\Omega_{2m}^{+}(q)
-conjugacy classes of subgroups
H_{0}
. These orbits and conjugacy classes are fixed setwise by
\mathrm{GO}_{2m}^{+}(q)
but fused in
\mathrm{CO}_{2m}^{+}(q)
. Hence
H_{0}
is normalised by some element
h\in\mathrm{GO}_{2m}^{+}(q)\backslash\mathrm{SO}_{2m}^{+}(q)
. Again,
h
interchanges
\mathcal{P}_{m}^{+}
and
\mathcal{P}_{m}^{-}
and
\langle H_{0},h\rangle
is transitive on
\mathcal{P}_{m}
, and we record
H_{1}=H_{0}
in Table
8.7
.

Case

H_{0}=\Omega_{2m-2}^{-}(q)^{R}
. Here there is a unique
\Omega_{2m}^{+}(q)
-orbit on elliptic subspaces of codimension two and hence a unique
\Omega_{2m}^{+}(q)
-conjugacy class of subgroups
H_{0}
. Hence
H_{0}
is normalised by an element of
\mathrm{GO}_{2m}^{+}(q)\backslash\mathrm{SO}_{2m}^{+}(q)
, which interchanges
\mathcal{P}_{m}^{+}
and
\mathcal{P}_{m}^{-}
and so
N_{G}(H_{0})
is transitive on
\mathcal{P}_{m}
. We record
H_{1}=H_{0}
in Table
8.7
.

Case

m=4
and
H_{0}=\Omega_{7}(q)^{I}
. Here
\overline{H_{0}}
is the image under a triality automorphism of
\overline{\Omega_{7}(q)^{R}}
. We saw in the first above that
\Omega_{7}(q)^{R}
is transitive on both
\mathcal{P}_{4}^{+}
and
\mathcal{P}_{4}^{-}
. Moreover, the stabiliser of a nonsingular vector
\langle u\rangle
is not transitive on
\mathcal{P}_{1}
as there are singular vectors in
u^{\perp}
and
V\backslash u^{\perp}
. Thus
\Omega_{7}(q)^{R}
is not transitive on
\mathcal{P}_{1}
. As explained in Remark
8.2
, a triality automorphism induces a 3-cycle
(\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{4}^{+},\mathcal{P}_{4}^{-})
, and it follows that
H_{0}
is transitive on
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and is transitive on exactly one of
\mathcal{P}_{4}^{+},\mathcal{P}_{4}^{-}
. Thus we do not obtain groups transitive on
\mathcal{P}_{4}
in this case.

Case

H_{0}=\mathrm{SU}_{4}(q)
. Here
\overline{H_{0}}
is the image under a triality automorphism of
\overline{\Omega_{6}^{-}(q)^{R}}
. We saw in the second above that
\Omega_{6}^{-}(q)^{R}
is transitive on both
\mathcal{P}_{4}^{+}
and
\mathcal{P}_{4}^{-}
. Moreover, the stabiliser of an elliptic subspace
U
of codimension two is not transitive on
\mathcal{P}_{1}
as there are singular vectors in
U
and
V\backslash U
. Thus
\Omega_{6}^{-}(q)^{R}
is not transitive on
\mathcal{P}_{1}
. Arguing as in the previous case we deduce that there are no groups transitive on
\mathcal{P}_{4}
in this case.

In all other cases
m\in\{3,4\}
and
q\in\{2,3\}
.
If
(m,q)=(4,3)
then the groups occurring in Table
8.7
are precisely those identified in Lemma
8.3
(2). The remaining cases follow from a
Magma
calculation informed by Theorem
3.5
(b).
∎

Now we consider the families
\mathcal{N}_{k}
where both
k
and
q
are odd. Recall that in this situation

\mathcal{N}_{k}=\mathcal{N}_{k}^{+}\cup\mathcal{N}_{k}^{-}
, where
\mathcal{N}_{k}^{+},\mathcal{N}_{k}^{-}
are the two
\Omega_{2m}^{+}(q)
-orbits in
\mathcal{N}_{k}
, and that
the group
\mathrm{CO}_{2m}^{+}(q)
interchanges
\mathcal{N}_{k}^{+}
and
\mathcal{N}_{k}^{-}
and hence is transitive on
\mathcal{N}_{k}
.

Proposition 8.6
.

Suppose that
H\leqslant\Gamma\mathrm{O}_{2m}^{+}(q)
, where
m\geqslant 3
and

is odd, such that
\Omega_{2m}^{+}(q)\not\leqslant H
. Let

and suppose that

either

,

and, modulo

induces a subgroup of

which is transitive on

, and

; or

.

, and

.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{N}_{k}
, and let
G=\Gamma\mathrm{O}^{+}_{2m}(q)
, so
G
fuses the two

\Omega_{2m}^{+}(q)
-orbits
\mathcal{N}_{k}^{+}
and
\mathcal{N}_{k}^{-}
in
\mathcal{N}_{k}
, and
the setwise stabiliser
G^{+}
of
\mathcal{N}_{k}^{+}
has index 2 in
G
.
Clearly
\{\mathcal{N}_{k}^{-},\mathcal{N}_{k}^{+}\}
is a system of imprimitivity for
G
,
and hence for
H
.
As
H
is transitive on
\mathcal{N}_{k}
,
H^{+}:=H\cap G^{+}
is an index 2 subgroup of
H
that
fixes
\mathcal{N}_{k}^{+}
and
\mathcal{N}_{k}^{-}
setwise and is transitive on both, and
H=\langle H^{+},h\rangle
for any

h\in H\setminus H^{+}
with
h^{2}\in H^{+}
.
Now
\Omega_{2m}^{+}(q)\not\leqslant H^{+}
, by our assumptions on
H
, and so
H^{+}
is given by
Theorem
8.4
.
In particular,
k=1
or
(m,k)=(4,3)
.

Suppose first that
(m,k)=(4,3)
. Then by Theorem
8.4
(g) we have
2\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\Omega_{7}(q)^{I}\trianglelefteqslant H^{+}
, and moreover
2\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\Omega_{7}(q)^{I}
is transitive on
\mathcal{N}_{3}^{+}
. By
[
BHRD
]
*Table 8.50, p. 402, the group
\Omega_{8}^{+}(q)
has four conjugacy classes of subgroups isomorphic to

2\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\Omega_{7}(q)
and by
[
K87
]
these
are fused by
\mathrm{GO}_{8}^{+}(q)
. Hence there is a unique conjugacy
class of subgroups of
\mathrm{GO}_{8}^{+}(q)
isomorphic to
2\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\Omega_{7}(q)
. Thus there exists
h
in
\mathrm{CO}_{8}^{+}(q)\setminus\mathrm{GO}_{8}^{+}(q)

normalising
2\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\Omega_{7}(q)^{I}
, and since
h\notin\mathrm{GO}_{8}^{+}(q)
it interchanges
\mathcal{N}_{3}^{+}
and
\mathcal{N}_{3}^{-}
. Thus
2\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\Omega_{7}(q)^{I}
is also transitive on
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{-}
and
\langle 2\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\Omega_{7}(q)^{I},h\rangle
is transitive on
\mathcal{N}_{1}
. Since
2\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\Omega_{7}(q)^{I}
is characteristic in
H^{+}
, part (c) holds.

We assume from now on that
k=1
. If
(m,q)=(4,3)
then the transitive subgroups
H
are those given by Lemma
8.3
(3). Thus we may in addition assume that
(m,q)\neq(4,3)
. Then by Theorem
8.4
(e) either
H^{+}
fixes a totally singular
m
-subspace
W
and
H^{+}
induces a subgroup of
\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{m}(q)
that is transitive on the set of
1
-subspaces of
W
, or
H^{+}
contains a normal subgroup
H_{0}
as follows (recall that
q
is odd):

(i)

\mathrm{SL}_{m}(q)
;

(ii)

\mathrm{Sp}_{m}(q)
or
\mathrm{SU}_{m}(q)
with
m
even;

(iii)

2\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\Omega_{7}(q)
with
m=4
;

(iv)

2\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\Omega_{8}^{-}(q^{1/2})
with
m=4
;

(v)

\Omega_{9}(q)
with
m=8
.

Suppose first that
H^{+}
fixes a totally singular
m
-subspace
W
and induces a subgroup of
\Gamma\mathrm{L}_{m}(q)
that is transitive on the set of
1
-subspaces of
W
. Thus
H^{+}
acts irreducibly on
W
and hence also on
V/W
. Since the action of
H^{+}
on
V/W
is dual to the action of
H^{+}
on
W
, it follows that either
W
is the only totally singular
m
-subspace fixed by
H^{+}
or
H^{+}
fixes precisely two totally singular
m
-subspaces, namely
W
and a complementary space
W^{\prime}
. Since
N_{G}(H^{+})
fixes setwise the set of totally singular
m
-subspaces fixed by
H^{+}
it follows that either
H
fixes
W
, or
H
fixes the decomposition
V=W\oplus W^{\prime}
. In the first case
H
is as in part (a) of Proposition
8.6
. In the second case
H
, and hence also
H^{+}
, is contained in the normaliser in
G
of
\mathrm{SL}_{m}(q)
. Then as seen in the analysis of case (
\delta
) of the proof of
[
Reg
]
*Lemma 4.5 the group
H^{+}
has a normal subgroup
H_{0}
as listed in cases (i) or (ii) above. We deal with these below.

It remains to consider the cases (i)–(v). In all such instances
H_{0}
is characteristic in
H^{+}
and hence is normal in
H
. Thus to complete the proof it suffices to show that examples arise in each case.

For case (i), we see from
[
Factns
]
*Table 1 that the stabiliser
K
in
\mathrm{GO}^{+}_{2m}(q)
of a pair of complementary totally singular
m
-subspaces is transitive on
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{+}
. Moreover,
\mathrm{SL}_{m}(q)
is characteristic in
K
. The group
\mathrm{CO}_{2m}^{+}(q)
fixes the
\mathrm{GO}_{2m}^{+}(q)
-conjugacy class of such subgroups and so there exists
h\in\mathrm{CO}^{+}_{2m}(q)\backslash\mathrm{GO}_{2m}^{+}(q)
that normalises
K
. Such an element
h
interchanges
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{+}
and
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{-}
, and it follows that
K
is transitive on
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{-}
and
\langle K,h\rangle
is transitive on
\mathcal{N}_{1}
. Thus examples arise in this case.

For case (ii) we see from
[
Factns
]
*Table 1 that the normaliser
K
of
\mathrm{SU}_{m}(q)
in
\mathrm{GO}^{+}_{2m}(q)
is transitive on
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{+}
when
m
is even. By
[
KL
]
*Proposition 4.3.18 there is only one conjugacy class in
\mathrm{GO}^{+}_{2m}(q)
of such subgroups and so arguing as in the previous case we again find an example. Next suppose that
\mathrm{Sp}_{m}(q)
is normal in
H^{+}
. Then by the proof of
[
Reg
]
*Lemma 4.5, the group
\mathrm{Sp}_{m}(q)
is contained in the stabiliser
K
of a pair of totally singular
m
-subspaces. As
K
contains
\mathrm{GL}_{m}(q)
, there is only one
\mathrm{GO}^{+}_{2m}(q)
-conjugacy class, and hence only one
\mathrm{CO}_{2m}^{+}(q)
-conjugacy class, of such subgroups
\mathrm{Sp}_{m}(q)
.
Thus there exists
h\in\mathrm{CO}^{+}_{2m}(q)\backslash\mathrm{GO}_{2m}^{+}(q)
that normalises the subgroup
H_{0}=\mathrm{Sp}_{m}(q)
, and such an element
h
interchanges
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{+}
and
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{-}
. So if
H^{+}
is transitive on
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{+}
it is also transitive on
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{-}
and
\langle H^{+},h\rangle
is transitive on
\mathcal{N}_{1}
, and we again get examples.

Finally we deal with cases (iii)-(v). If
m=4
and
H_{0}=2\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\Omega_{7}(q)^{I}
then we have already seen in the case
(m,k)=(4,3)
that there is a unique conjugacy class of subgroups of
\mathrm{GO}_{8}^{+}(q)
isomorphic to
2\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\Omega_{7}(q)^{I}
. Since
2\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\Omega_{7}(q)^{I}
is transitive on
\mathcal{N}_{1}^{+}
then the same argument as before yields an example here as well.
If
m=4
and
H_{0}=2\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\Omega_{8}^{-}(q^{1/2})
then by
[
BHRD
]
*Table 8.50, p. 402, the group
\Omega_{8}^{+}(q)
has four conjugacy classes of subgroups isomorphic to
2\raisebox{2.58334pt}{$\cdot$}\Omega_{8}^{-}(q^{1/2})
and by
[
K87
]
these are all fused in
\mathrm{GO}^{+}_{8}(q)
. Hence the argument above yields an example here.
Finally suppose that
m=8
. Then it is seen in the last line of the the proof of the proposition of
[
Factns
]
*Appendix 3 that
N_{G}(\Omega_{9}(q))
is transitive on
\mathcal{N}_{1}
.
∎

9.
Transitive generalised quadrangles

A
generalised quadrangle

\mathcal{Q}=(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{L})
of

order

(s,t)
is a point-line incidence
relation satisfying:

(GQ1)

each
\ell\in\mathcal{L}
is incident with
s+1
points

p\in\mathcal{P}
,

(GQ2)

each
p\in\mathcal{P}
is incident with
t+1
lines

\ell\in\mathcal{L}
, and

(GQ3)

for each point
p
not on a line
\ell
, there is
a unique point
p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}
and a unique line
\ell^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}
, such that

p
is on
\ell^{\prime}
, and
p^{\prime}
is on
\ell
and
\ell^{\prime}
.

To avoid degeneracies we henceforth assume that
\mathcal{Q}
is
thick
, that is,
s,t>1
.
It well known that
|\mathcal{P}|=(s+1)(st+1)
,
|\mathcal{L}|=(t+1)(st+1)
and

\sqrt{t}\leqslant s\leqslant t
, see
[
BLS
]
*Lemma 2.1.

A beautiful theorem of Buekenhout and Lefévre
[
BF
]
says that
if the point set
\mathcal{P}
and line set

\mathcal{L}
of a generalised quadrangle
\mathcal{Q}
are chosen from the
points and lines of a projective space
\textup{PG}(d,q)
, then
\mathcal{Q}

is a
classical generalised quadrangle
. By this we mean that

(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{L})=(\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2})
where
\mathcal{P}_{i}
is the set of
totally singular
i
-subspaces obtained from the natural module
for
\mathrm{Sp}_{4}(q)
,

\mathrm{GO}_{5}(q)
,

\mathrm{GO}_{6}^{-}(q)
,

\mathrm{GU}_{4}(q)
, or

\mathrm{GU}_{5}(q)
, and incidence is containment. The five thick classical generalised quadrangles are listed in Table
9.1
along with their automorphism groups.

Table 9.1.
The classical generalised quadrangles
\mathcal{Q}
. Let
q_{i}=q^{i}+1
.

\mathcal{Q}

W_{3}(q)

Q_{4}(q)

Q^{-}_{5}(q)

H_{3}(q^{2})

H_{4}(q^{2})

(q,q)

(q,q)

(q,q^{2})

(q^{2},q)

\textup{P}\Gamma\textup{U}_{4}(q)

\textup{P}\Gamma\textup{U}_{5}(q)

We note that the axioms for a generalised quadrangle are symmetric and so we can interchange the role of points and lines, that is, if
\mathcal{Q}=(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{L})
is a generalised quadrangle then
(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{P})
is also a generalised quadrangle and is referred to as the
dual
of
\mathcal{Q}
.
The dual of
W_{3}(q)
is isomorphic to
Q_{4}(q)
and the dual of
Q_{5}^{-}(q)
is isomorphic to
H_{3}(q^{2})
. The dual of
H_{4}(q^{2})
is not usually regarded as being a classical generalised quadrangle.

From our results we are able to determine all the point- or line-transitive
subgroups of
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{Q})
where
\mathcal{Q}
is a classical generalised
quadrangle. In each case
\mathrm{soc}(\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{Q}))
is transitive on the
set
\mathcal{P}
of points and the set
\mathcal{L}
of lines of
\mathcal{Q}
, so it suffices to determine the
transitive subgroups not containing
\mathrm{soc}(\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{Q}))
.

The groups that act regularly on the set of points of a classical generalised quadrangle were determined in
[
BamG
]
and the only examples are
3^{1+2}_{\pm}
acting on
Q_{5}^{-}(2)
and
\textup{C}_{513}\rtimes\textup{C}_{9}
acting on
Q_{5}^{-}(8)
. (Here
3_{\pm}^{1+2}
denote extraspecial groups of order

27
where
3_{+}^{1+2}
has exponent
3
and
3_{-}^{1+2}
has exponent
9
.)

.

Let
\mathcal{Q}=(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{L})

in Table

H\leqslant\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{Q})
where

.

(a)

If
H\leqslant\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{Q})
is transitive on the points
\mathcal{P}
, then
there exists
H_{0}\trianglelefteqslant H
where
(\mathcal{Q},H_{0},q)
is given in
Table
9.2
.

(b)

If
H\leqslant\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{Q})
is transitive on the lines
\mathcal{L}
, then
there exists
H_{0}\trianglelefteqslant H
where
(\mathcal{Q},H_{0},q)
is given in
Table
9.3
.

Proof.

The entries in Tables

,

,

, and

to be the appropriate set of totally singular subspaces and taking the projective versions of the matrix groups listed. For
Q_{5}^{-}(8)
and
H_{3}(8^{2})
we consult the proofs of Theorem
4.2
and

, respectively, to obtain the extra information provided for the projective groups involved when
\textup{C}_{19}\trianglelefteqslant H
. Also for

and
H_{3}(2^{2})
we use
Magma
to see that the groups

and
(\textup{C}_{3})^{3}
remain in the projective setting and are not ‘quotiented out’ by a subgroup of order three.
∎

Remark 9.2
.

We note that since
W_{3}(q)
and
Q_{4}(q)
are dual, any group that is transitive on the set of points of
W_{3}(q)
should also appear as a group that is transitive on the set of lines of
Q_{4}(q)
and vice versa. Similarly, any group that is transitive on the set of lines of
W_{3}(q)
will also appear as a group that is transitive on the set of points of
Q_{4}(q)
and vice versa. Since
\mathrm{PSp}_{2}(q^{2})\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q^{2})\cong\Omega_{4}^{-}(q)
, and
\Omega_{2}(q^{2})\cong\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q^{2})\cong\Omega_{4}^{-}(q)
, Tables
9.2
and
9.3
satisfy this condition. The same duality occurs between
H_{3}(q^{2})
and
Q_{5}^{-}(q)
.

We also note that for
q
even, the groups
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(q^{2})
acting transitively on the set of points of
W_{3}(q)
are distinct from the groups
\Omega_{4}^{-}(q)
that are transitive on the set of lines of
W_{3}(q)
. They are interchanged by a graph automorphism of
\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(q)

[
Asch
]
*Section 14. Similarly, the groups
\mathrm{Sp}_{2}(q^{2})
that are transitive on the set of points of
Q_{4}(q)
for
q
even are distinct from the groups
\Omega_{4}^{-}(q)
that acts transitively on the set of lines.

\triangle

Recall that
(p,\ell)\in\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{L}
is a
flag
if
p
lies on
\ell
, and
an
antiflag
otherwise. The sets
\mathcal{F}
of flags and the set

\mathcal{A}
of antiflags have cardinalities:

\displaystyle|\mathcal{F}|

\displaystyle=|\mathcal{P}|(t+1)=(s+1)(t+1)(st+1),\quad\textup{and}

\displaystyle|\mathcal{A}|

\displaystyle=(|\mathcal{P}|-(s+1))|\mathcal{L}|=st(s+1)(t+1)(st+1).

We note that if
G
acts flag-transitively or antiflag-transitively on a generalised quadrangle
\mathcal{Q}
then it also acts flag-transitively or antiflag-transitively on its dual. It is well known that the actions of
\mathrm{PSp}_{4}(q)
,
\mathrm{PSU}_{4}(q)
and
\mathrm{PSU}_{5}(q)
on totally isotropic 1-subspaces have rank 3, see for example
[
KLrank3
,
L1
,
L2
]
. Thus if
G
is one of these three groups and
p
is a point of the corresponding generalised quadrangle
\mathcal{Q}
then
G_{p}
has two orbits on the remaining points: the points collinear with
p
and the points not collinear with
p
. Since each pair of points lies on at most one line, it follows that
G_{p}
acts transitively on the set of lines of
\mathcal{Q}
incident with
p
and so
G
acts flag-transitively on
\mathcal{Q}
.
Kantor
[
Kantor2
]
conjectured that, up to duality, the only flag-transitive generalised quadrangles are the classical ones and the unique generalised quadrangles of order
(3,5)
and
(15,17)
. This conjecture is still wide-open but recently, Bamberg, Li and Swartz
[
BLS
]
showed that, up to duality, the only antiflag-transitive generalised quadrangles are the classical ones and the unique generalised quadrangle of order
(3,5)
.

Theorem 9.3
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be a classical generalised quadrangle as
in Table
9.1
and let
H\leqslant\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{Q})
with
\mathrm{soc}(\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{Q}))\not\leqslant H
.

(a)

If
H
is flag-transitive,
then either

(i)

\mathcal{Q}
is
W_{3}(3)
or
Q_{4}(3)
, and

H\in\{2^{4}\ldotp F_{20},2^{4}\ldotp\mathrm{Alt}_{5},2^{4}\ldotp\mathrm{Sym}_{5}\}
; or

(ii)

\mathcal{Q}
is
H_{3}(3^{2})
or
Q_{5}^{-}(3)
, and
H=\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(4).2
(two possibilities) or
\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(4).2^{2}
, where in the first case the extension is by a field or graph automorphism.

Moreover, all six groups listed above are flag-transitive.

(b)

The group
H
is not antiflag-transitive.

Proof.

A flag- or antiflag-transitive subgroup

H\leqslant\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{Q})
must be transitive on both
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{L}
,
and hence must occur in both Tables
9.2

and
9.3
(for the same generalised quadrangle
\mathcal{Q}
). Thus noting Remark
9.2
we see that if
\mathrm{soc}(\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{Q}))\not\leqslant H
, then one of the following occurs:

(1)

\mathcal{Q}=W_{3}(3)
and either
2^{4}\ldotp F_{20}\trianglelefteqslant H
or
2^{4}\ldotp\mathrm{Alt}_{5}\trianglelefteqslant H
;

(2)

\mathcal{Q}=Q_{4}(3)
and either
2^{4}\ldotp F_{20}\trianglelefteqslant H
or
2^{4}\ldotp\mathrm{Alt}_{5}\trianglelefteqslant H
;

(3)

\mathcal{Q}=H_{3}(3^{2})
and
\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(4)\trianglelefteqslant H
;

(4)

\mathcal{Q}=Q_{5}^{-}(3)
and
\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(4)\trianglelefteqslant H
.

Suppose first that
\mathcal{Q}=W_{3}(3)
or
Q_{4}(3)
. The possibilities for
H
are
2^{4}\ldotp F_{20}
,
2^{4}\ldotp\mathrm{Alt}_{5}
and
2^{4}\ldotp\mathrm{Sym}_{5}
. Now
(|\mathcal{F}|,|\mathcal{A}|)=(160,1440)
and 1440 does not divide
|2^{4}\ldotp\mathrm{Sym}_{5}|
and so none of these groups are antiflag-transitive. A
Magma
calculation shows that all three groups are flag-transitive, as stated in part (a)(i).

Next suppose that
\mathcal{Q}=H_{3}(3^{2})
or
Q_{5}^{-}(3)
. Then
\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(4)\trianglelefteqslant H\leqslant\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(4).2^{2}
and
(|\mathcal{F}|,|\mathcal{A}|)=(1120,30240)
. Order arguments again eliminate the possibility of an antiflag-transitive group. A
Magma
calculation shows
\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(4)
is not transitive on lines of
H_{3}(3^{2})
but
\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(4).2
is, where the extension is by a field or graph automorphism, but not a graph-field automorphism. Moreover, both are flag-transitive.
∎

The proof of Theorem
9.3
actually shows something quite remarkable: any group that is both point-transitive and line-transitive is also flag-transitive.

Corollary 9.4
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be a classical generalised quadrangle as
in Table
9.1
and let
H\leqslant\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{Q})
be both point-transitive and
line-transitive. Then
H
is flag-transitive.

The full automorphism group of a classical generalised quadrangle acts primitively on the set of points. Moreover, there has been considerable recent effort devoted to classifying the generalised quadrangles with a group of automorphisms that acts primitively on the set of points: it is known that, if
H\leqslant\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{Q})
acts primitively on both the set of points and the set of lines then it must be an almost simple group
[
GQSym
]
. If
H
acts primitively on points and transitively on lines then it has a unique minimal normal subgroup
N

[
BPP3
]
, and if the group
N
is elementary abelian then
\mathcal{Q}
must be one of the unique generalised quadrangles of order
(3,5)
or
(15,17)

[
BGPP
]
. Detailed information about a subgroup
H
of automorphisms which is assumed to act primitively on points (without any other assumptions), is given in
[
BPP2
]
. We shed light on these questions, for the classical examples, by determining all groups that act primitively on the set of points of a classical generalised quadrangle.

Theorem 9.5
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be a classical generalised quadrangle as
in Table
9.1
, and suppose
that
H\leqslant\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{Q})
acts primitively on the set of points of
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathrm{soc}(\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{Q}))\not\leqslant H
. Then
\mathcal{Q}=H_{3}(3^{2})
and
\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(4)\trianglelefteqslant H
.

Proof.

Note that
H
acts faithfully on the set
\mathcal{P}
of points of
\mathcal{Q}
. Since
H
is primitive
on
\mathcal{P}
, each of its nontrivial normal subgroups is transitive on
\mathcal{P}
. In particular
H
is transitive on
\mathcal{P}
, and so has a normal subgroup
H_{0}
given in Table
9.2
.
Since
\mathrm{soc}(H_{0})
is characteristic in
H_{0}
, it is normal in
H
, and hence is
transitive on
\mathcal{P}
. Thus also

\mathrm{soc}(H_{0})
has a normal subgroup, say
H_{1}
appearing in Table
9.2
.
If
\mathrm{soc}(H_{0})
is abelian then
\mathrm{soc}(H_{0})
must be elementary abelian
and regular on
\mathcal{P}
. However the only abelian group appearing in Table
9.2
is
(\textup{C}_{3})^{3}
acting on
Q_{5}^{-}(2)
, and by
[
BamG
]
this group is not point-regular on
Q_{5}^{-}(2)
.
Thus
\mathrm{soc}(H_{0})
is nonabelian, and hence is a direct product of isomorphic nonabelian simple groups.
If
H_{0}=\mathrm{PSp}_{2}(q^{2})
,
\Omega_{3}(q^{2})
or
\mathrm{SU}_{3}(q)
then
H
preserves an extension field structure on the underlying vector space and so preserves the partition of
\mathcal{P}
given by the 1-dimensional subspaces over
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}
. Hence these examples are not primitive.

This leaves four almost simple examples to consider. Suppose first that
\mathrm{soc}(H_{0})=\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(4)
. If
\mathcal{Q}=Q_{5}^{-}(3)
, then the
H
-action on
\mathcal{P}
is equivalent to its action on the set of lines of the dual quadrangle
H_{3}(3^{2})
. However, we saw in the proof of Theorem
9.3
that
\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(4)
is not transitive on the lines of
H_{3}(3^{2})
, so its orbits form a system of imprimitivity for the
H
-action on these lines. Thus
H
is not primitive on the lines of
H_{3}(3^{2})
, and hence is not primitive on the point set
\mathcal{P}
, which is a contradiction. Hence in this case
\mathcal{Q}=H_{3}(3^{2})
, and a
Magma
calculation shows that
\mathrm{PSL}_{3}(4)
acts primitively on the set points of
H_{3}(3^{2})
, as in the statement.
If
\mathcal{Q}=Q_{5}^{-}(3)
and
H_{0}=\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(7)
, then the number of points is
112
which does not divide

|H_{0}|
, so
H_{0}
is not transitive on points (though
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(7)
is), which is a contradiction. Finally, if
\mathcal{Q}=W_{3}(3)
and
H_{0}=\mathrm{Alt}_{5}
, then the number of points is
40
, and so
H_{0}
is not transitive on points (though
\mathrm{Sym}_{5}
is), ruling this case out as well.
∎
